<note>The background of this page is decorated with "snail" or "curl" marbled paper. This technique, popular in the 19th century, involved floating pigments on a chemical bath, combing them into patterns, and "dipping" the paper to transfer the design. It was often used for endpapers because it was both beautiful and effective at hiding the glue and structural elements of the book's binding.</note>

<note>In historical bookbinding, blank pages known as flyleaves were standard. They served a practical purpose, protecting the title page or the first pages of the text from the glue used on the decorative endpapers and providing a buffer against wear and tear. In many scholarly volumes, these pages remain empty, though they were often used by previous owners for handwritten signatures or shelf marks.</note>

<summary>A blank flyleaf that serves as a protective transition between the library-labeled endpaper and the beginning of the book's content.</summary>

<note>The presence of multiple blank pages, or "flyleaves," is typical in high-quality manuscripts and early printed books. These pages acted as a shield against the elements and the mechanical stresses of opening and closing the heavy wooden or leather-bound covers. While they contain no text, they are an integral part of the physical history of the object, showing the care taken to preserve the core contents for future generations.</note>

<note>In the physical construction of a medieval or early modern book, these empty pages of parchment are known as flyleaves. They were intended to protect the internal written content from the friction and dust that might enter through the covers. The absence of any writing, even centuries later, indicates that this volume was likely kept in a protected environment, as these "empty" spaces were frequently used by later owners for grocery lists, pen-testing, or recording family births and deaths.</note>

## The
# Lion in Love
<note>original: "Le Lion amoureux." This is the title of a famous fable by Jean de La Fontaine (1621–1695). In the story, a lion falls in love with a peasant woman and allows his teeth and claws to be clipped to win her hand, leaving him defenseless against his enemies. It serves as a cautionary tale about the vulnerability brought about by passion.</note>

# PREFACE

---

When Giuseppe Pomba <note>Giuseppe Pomba (1795–1876) was a prominent Italian publisher in Turin and the founder of the UTET publishing house</note> conceived the grand idea (and for him, as a publisher, grand ideas were more frequent, and I am inclined to say more natural and familiar, than the foreseeable difficulties in bringing them to fruition, by which, no matter how great they were, he was determined and accustomed never to be daunted)—he formed, I repeat, the grand idea of publishing a complete dictionary of our nation’s language, prepared with a rational method, and placing at its head Tommaseo, the supreme lexicographer <note>Niccolò Tommaseo (1802–1874) was a linguist and writer whose monumental dictionary remains a landmark of 19th-century Italian culture</note>; an office was opened in Turin, in the very house of Pomba, to prepare and organize everything required for this great undertaking; linguistic texts, both printed and in manuscript, already excerpted <term>excerpted (spogliati): the systematic process of reading historical and contemporary texts to extract words and usage examples for inclusion in a dictionary</term>, or yet to be excerpted, or for checking existing excerpts against their sources; additions from common language as well as from scientific and artistic fields, provided by collaborators or scholars who, applauding...

<summary>This page introduces the ambitious project of creating a comprehensive Italian dictionary, spearheaded by the publisher Giuseppe Pomba and led by the scholar Niccolò Tommaseo in Turin.</summary>
<keywords>Giuseppe Pomba, Niccolò Tommaseo, Turin, lexicography, dictionary publishing, Italian language, scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Proemio" (Preface) from the previous page, detailing the gathering of resources and staff for the new dictionary project.</meta>

contributed to the enterprise; in short, they gathered every kind of literary resource necessary for the task. Tommaseo—who was always kind to me from his exile in Corfu <note>original: "corcirese"; referring to Corcyra, the ancient name for Corfu, where Tommaseo lived in exile for his political views</note> in 1851 until his death—appointed me, with Pomba’s consent, to lead that office, and gave me the assistance of Savini of Bologna and others. When I took it upon myself, as was my duty, to review the excerpts <term>excerpts (spogli): word-lists and usage examples extracted from literary works to serve as the raw material for a dictionary</term> of previous lexicographers <term>lexicographer: a person who compiles or writes dictionaries</term>, I paid closer attention to those of Alberti and Gherardini, due to a certain excessive permissiveness they showed in accepting new writers and very recent words; and more than once I hesitated over certain examples that Gherardini had taken from Boccaccio's *Donne famose* <note>Boccaccio's *De Mulieribus Claris* (Of Famous Women), a collection of biographies of historical and mythological women</note>, translated into the vernacular by Master Donato degli Albanzani of Prato Vecchio in Casentino (called for this reason the "Apennine-born") in the Neapolitan edition of 1836. To this edition, beyond the distinguished name of the editor Don Luigi Tosti, a monk of Monte Cassino, the Accademia della Crusca <note>The Accademia della Crusca, founded in 1583, is the most prestigious institution for the study of the Italian language</note> had added its authority by citing it in its seven suppressed volumes and in the latest edition of its dictionary. 

Although these might have seemed sufficient guarantees <term>guarantees (malleverie): assurances of quality or reliability</term>, they did not prevent me from noticing, from the very beginning, that very grave errors had crept into that printing—even in the textual readings—of such a nature as to have opened the door to the creation of new words, and I will even say, words of the poorest quality <note>original: "di pessimo conio"; literally "of the worst coinage," referring to improperly invented or non-existent words</note>. To make it easier for me to notice those errors and correct them, 

<summary>The author describes his appointment by Tommaseo to lead the dictionary office and his discovery of significant linguistic errors in previous respected editions of Boccaccio, which had been mistakenly validated by the Accademia della Crusca.</summary>

<keywords>Niccolò Tommaseo, Giuseppe Pomba, lexicography, Accademia della Crusca, Giovanni Boccaccio, Donato degli Albanzani, textual criticism, Italian linguistics, 19th-century publishing</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the editorial process for the dictionary, specifically focusing on the author's decision to use two manuscripts from Turin as the basis for a new edition of the text, rather than relying on the previously mentioned Neapolitan edition.</meta>

-> - V - <-

This was supported by a study I had conducted a short while before regarding two Turin manuscripts of that vernacular translation <term>vernacular translation (volgarizzamento): a medieval translation of a Latin or Greek text into the common Italian speech (the vernacular)</term>, from which I derived a new text that seemed to me much better than the Neapolitan one cited by the aforementioned Academy. From this arose the first efforts dedicated to that prose from the "Golden Age" of the language <note>original: "buon secolo della lingua"; referring to the 14th century, the era of Dante, Petrarch, and Boccaccio, considered the pinnacle of Italian linguistic purity</note>, which is of great importance for its linguistic quality, its abundance of unusual expressions, and its beautiful vocabulary. It departs from those common subjects in which the art of that time—whether written with the pen, painted with the brush, expressed through music, or otherwise—was particularly engaged. The manuscripts that served as my guide for that first draft are the following; since I did not describe them while I was in Turin, nor am I currently able to see them, I will describe them using the words of Baron Giuseppe Vernazza, in a letter *to his learned and courteous friend* Abbot Giambattista Scioppalalba, given to me in autograph form by Abbot Costanzo Gazzera. I have, however, cross-referenced this description with the *Manuscript Codices of the Royal Turin University Library* <note>original: "Codices Mss. bibliothecae regii Taurinensis Athenaei"</note> by Pasini, Rivautella, and Berta.

> "The parchment manuscript I. IV. 44 consists of 398 leaves and contains two volumes. The first has 140 pages. The second begins at leaf 73 and has 250 pages (1). *The first. Preface by Gian-*

---

(1) In this, as in most descriptions of manuscripts—and the same may be said of books—one does not strictly adhere to the rigors...

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic description of the Turin manuscripts mentioned by Baron Giuseppe Vernazza. # - VI -

...Giannozzo Manetti in the consolatory dialogue on the death of his son, addressed to Mariotto Banchi, at whose request it was translated from Latin into the vernacular: "Since, for my consolation, honorable and dear as a brother, I had by the grace of God written in Latin and completed a certain consolatory dialogue on the death of my son, which took place in the famous monastery of Certosa <note>Likely the Certosa di Firenze, a Carthusian monastery where Manetti sought spiritual retreat</note> where we found ourselves in that solitude to hear with singular devotion the anniversary solemnities in commemoration of the pious and soul-saving <note>original: "salutifera"; literally "health-bringing," but in a religious context it refers to the salvation brought by Christ's sacrifice</note> death of our Lord..." It ends on page 140; and from there we then departed and returned to our cells, joyful and full of devotion and consolation.

->THE END. PRAISE BE TO GOD. AMEN.<-

*The second. On famous women <note>This refers to a translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De mulieribus claris," a collection of biographies of historical and mythological women</note>, [dedicated] to the illustrious Queen Joan of Puglia <note>Queen Joan (Giovanna) I of Naples (1326–1382)</note>, the fortunate book begins—*

---

...regarding bibliographic technology <term>bibliographic technology: the systematic and technical description of books as physical objects, including their paper, binding, and structure</term>, the greatest confusion reigns. The Turin codex in question is said by Vernazza to consist of 398 leaves <note>original: "fogli"; here used loosely for pages or leaves</note>, and by Pasini 197 (as noted in: *Consisting of 197 leaves*, Op. cit. Part II, p. 445). In both, the word *sheet* is misplaced, as we are dealing here with *leaves* <note>original: "carte"; bibliographers distinguish between a large "sheet" of paper/parchment and the individual "leaves" (folios) created when that sheet is folded</note>. It is worse, however, in Vernazza, who makes "sheets" equivalent to "pages"; by assigning 140 to the first volume and two hundred and fifty to the second, he makes their total number lower than the number of leaves, which is an absurdity. Furthermore, that codex is in a single volume, as Pasini indicates in the cited passage, and not in two; and it contains, as can be seen, three different prose works.

<summary>This page concludes a quoted passage from Giannozzo Manetti's dialogue and begins a technical critique of how bibliographers Vernazza and Pasini inconsistently described the physical structure of the Turin manuscript.</summary>
<keywords>Giannozzo Manetti, Mariotto Banchi, Certosa, Joan of Puglia, bibliography, codicology, Giuseppe Vernazza, Giuseppe Pasini, Turin Codex, leaves vs pages</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the bibliographic analysis of Italian translations of Boccaccio's "De mulieribus claris" (Concerning Famous Women), specifically focusing on the version by Donato degli Albanzani (Donato di Casentino). It provides the opening passage of the first chapter on Eve and a description of the final chapter dedicated to Queen Joan I of Naples.</meta>

# - VII -

->The work of Giovanni Boccaccio, Florentine, translated into the vernacular <term>vernacular: "volgare," the common Italian language as opposed to scholarly Latin</term> by Master Donato of Casentino. Eve. I.<-

Since I must write so that famous women may be known for their virtues, it will not seem an unworthy thing to take the beginning from her who was the mother of all men, Eve, who was without doubt the first mother. She was glorious with magnificent virtues because she was not produced in this laborious valley of miseries in which all we other humans are born with toil <note>A reference to the Garden of Eden as opposed to the fallen world</note>.

*The last chapter is CIV <note>104</note>. It continues concerning the same Queen Joan <note>Queen Joan (Giovanna) I of Naples, to whom Boccaccio dedicated the original work</note>. The author who wrote of these famous women did not reach the end of the life of this most famous and glorious queen during past times; and although he promises in this book to write the histories of those women, nonetheless sometimes—either because he was drawn by the pleasure of offering [kind] words, or because he was moved by a desire to say honorable things (which pleases me more)—he leaves out those things that could be said that were wicked (1) <note>original: "rie"; meaning evil, guilty, or unfavorable actions</note> about her and touches only upon her praises.*

*It ends:* Then she was buried with royal honor in her final burial, and it made manifest that the life...

---

(1) Vernazza, by omitting the word *wicked* <note>original: "rie"</note> in his letter to Scioppalalba—a word which is present in the Turin and Florentine codices <term>codices: manuscript volumes</term>—removes the intended meaning from the sentence.

<summary>This page describes a manuscript of Boccaccio’s "On Famous Women" translated by Donato di Casentino, providing the opening lines of the chapter on Eve and discussing the editorial treatment of the final chapter on Queen Joan of Naples.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, Donato degli Albanzani, Donato di Casentino, Eve, Queen Joan of Naples, De mulieribus claris, translation, Vernazza, bibliography, Italian literature</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page concludes the editorial description of the Boccaccio manuscript analyzed on the previous page and introduces a new document within the same collection (Codex B): a letter from the imprisoned nobleman Astorre Manfredi.</meta>

VIII

...human [life] is a trifle (1) and that the saying of the poet <note>A reference to Ovid or Solon: "No man should be called happy until his death and funeral are over"</note> is true: that we must wait for the final day to praise any man, and that no one should be called blessed before he dies and is buried.

->FINIS. PRAISE BE TO GOD.<-

Letter of the magnificent lord Astorre Manfredi <term>Astorre Manfredi: Likely Astorre IV Manfredi (1485–1502), the young ruler of Faenza who was imprisoned and later killed; though the text specifies "Florentine prisons," he is most famously associated with the prisons of Rome</term> sent to a splendid woman whom he loved most highly, from the Florentine prisons. <insert>To</insert> F. Branca (2) at his request. Most noble and courageous lady. *It ends:* From the darkness of the Florentine public prisons. Finis. Praise be to God.

In the present edition, this miscellaneous codex is always cited with the abbreviation Cod. B.

---

(1) *Frivolous*, undoubtedly an adjective, implies a noun which, in this place, could be *thing* <note>original: "cosa"</note> or something similar. However, when I had the Turin codex in my hands, I read it as *fable* <note>original: "favola"</note>, and so it stands in the other codex that follows, and in the Florentine one mentioned further on; thus the word *frivolous* here is a creation of Vernazza <note>Giuseppe Vernazza (1745–1822), an Italian diplomat and scholar known for his work on manuscripts</note>.

(2) I took a copy of this letter and gave it as a gift to Cavalier Giovanni Ghinassi of Faenza, who published it in the *Acts and Memoirs of the Royal Deputation of National History for the provinces of Romagna. Seventh Year (1868) p. 179 et seq.* That orthographic sign which in Vernazza's manuscript can only be read as a capital F was, at that time, read and interpreted by me as an abbreviation for *Sere* or *Messere* <note>Traditional Italian honorifics for a gentleman or master</note>, and with such an interpretation Ghinassi published it, and the most illustrious Commendatore Zambrini also reproduced it in 1876.

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the fragility of human life and transitions to a bibliographic description of a letter written by the imprisoned Astorre Manfredi. The editor provides scholarly footnotes regarding textual variants and previous publications of the letter by 19th-century historians.</summary>

<keywords>Astorre Manfredi, Boccaccio, Codex B, Vernazza, Giovanni Ghinassi, Faenza, Zambrini, Florentine prisons, Italian bibliography, manuscript studies</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the cataloging of Boccaccio's manuscripts, moving from the previously discussed "Codex B" to a second, older manuscript of the "Famous Women" (De Mulieribus Claris) translated into the vernacular by Donato Albanzani.</meta>

— IX —

The other codex of the Vernacular Translation <term>Volgarizzamento: The historical practice of translating Latin or Greek texts into the common Italian "vulgar" tongue to make them accessible to non-scholars.</term> of the *Famous Women* produced by Albanzani—which Baron Vernazza describes second, though in chronological order it ought to hold the first place, because it belongs either to the last years of the 14th century or the first years of the 15th, while the one already described certainly belongs to the later part of that century—bears the shelfmark K. IV. 26 and is briefly mentioned by Pasini under number LIII of the second volume of his catalog. The description given by Vernazza in the previously mentioned letter to Scioppalalba is as follows:

> "Codex K. IV. 26 is parchment, consisting of 86 leaves, or 172 pages. It is preceded by four pages of the Table [of Contents] to quickly find the histories of the said book. *On folio 5.* Prologue and beginning of the first history, that of Eve. Eve, the first mother. Since I must write of the virtues by which famous women are known, it will not seem an unworthy thing to take the beginning from her who was the mother of all men: Eve, who was without doubt the first mother. She was glorious.

> - *It ends:* There follows more of this same chapter and of this woman and of her tragedy and end. The author who wrote of these famous women... and let him be buried. Finished is the book of famous women compiled by Master Giovanni Boccaccio <note>original: "misser zuane bocacio"</note> at the request of the most famous Queen Joanna of Puglia <note>Joanna I, Queen of Naples (1326–1382), often referred to as Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily/Apulia.</note>; later it was translated into the vernacular language <note>original: "indioma uolgar"</note>."

<summary>This page describes a late 14th or early 15th-century parchment manuscript (Codex K. IV. 26) containing Donato Albanzani's Italian translation of Boccaccio's "Famous Women." It includes the manuscript's opening lines regarding Eve and a concluding colophon dedicated to Queen Joanna of Naples.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, Famous Women, Donato Albanzani, Baron Vernazza, Queen Joanna of Naples, manuscript description, vernacular translation, Eve, parchment codex</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes the description of the Turin manuscript K. IV. 26 and compares the linguistic accuracy of various versions of Donato Albanzani’s translation of Boccaccio's "Famous Women."</meta>

— X —

...slated by Master Donato of Casentino <note>Donato Albanzani (c. 1328–1411), a scholar and friend of Petrarch and Boccaccio.</note> for the magnificent Marquis Niccolò da Este <note>Niccolò III d'Este (1383–1441), Marquis of Ferrara.</note>, prince and lord of Ferrara.

->May the scribe’s hand be saved at all hours. <note>original: "Manus scriptoris saluetur omnibus horis"</note><-

This second Turin codex is marked with the letter **A** in the frequent citations within the notes.
The brief passages provided here from the two Turin codices of the <term>volgarizzamento: the practice of translating Latin works into the common Italian "vulgar" tongue.</term> of *Famous Women* are sufficient to show that, while the translator was indeed Tuscan—and such was certainly Albanzani, possessing the great learning attributed to him by his contemporaries (See Mehus, *Preface and Life of Ambrogio Traversari the Camaldolese*)—the <term>amanuenses: professional scribes who hand-copied manuscripts.</term> certainly were not Tuscan. Consequently, even if they had produced their copies from excellent source texts, they almost always resulted in corrupted word endings (particularly in verb forms) and sometimes in the substance of the words themselves, due to a failure to understand their meaning. Nevertheless, judging by several passages published in the notes by Father Tosti, the Cassino codex <note>The manuscript held at the monastery of Monte Cassino.</note> is, on the whole, of inferior quality compared to the two Turin manuscripts. As one example among many that could be cited, consider this excerpt from Chapter XXI, *Iole, Queen of Aetolia*, which reads on page 57: "Because of which it happened that Hercules fell into that shameful ob—" <note>The text likely intended "obbrobrio" (disgrace/shame).</note>

<summary>This page concludes the identification of a 14th-century translation of Boccaccio's work, noting that while the translation itself was scholarly, the non-Tuscan scribes introduced numerous linguistic errors into the Turin and Cassino manuscripts.</summary>

<keywords>Donato Albanzani, Niccolò da Este, Famous Women, Boccaccio, manuscript errors, Tosti, textual criticism, Turin codices, philology</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of textual corruptions in the Turin manuscripts of Donato Albanzani’s translation, focusing on a specific passage from Chapter XXI regarding the myth of Hercules and Iole.</meta>

— XI —

—bedience; honors are forgotten, riches are consumed, hatred is loved, and very often one enters into the dangers of life; and among these things, contentions occur and peace is brief, etc."
If the codex is faithfully copied, everyone understands that with such a guide it is difficult to walk straight. But let it be noted that we have in our hands a <term>volgarizzamento: the practice of translating Latin works into the common Italian "vulgar" tongue</term>, and moreover a copy of it, made perhaps not from the original, but from another copy, and completed God knows when, God knows where, and by whom. In cases of doubt, it is therefore necessary to return to the source, which is the Latin text of Boccaccio, and with it at least straighten out the sense of the discourse. I say in cases of doubt, because when the reading of the translation is secure due to the consistency of the <term>codices: ancient manuscript volumes</term>, it is best to leave it as it is found, whether Albanzani had a different version of the text before him or whether he misunderstood it; and at most, the reader is warned of the discrepancy in a note. There are few cases of manifest error, and in those instances, one may correct it, always providing a notification.

Let us return to the example. From the beginning, the codex proceeds well, then it falters: "Because of which it happened that Hercules fell into that shameful obedience; honors are forgotten, riches are consumed, hatred is loved <note>original: "amasi lodio"</note> (corrected by the editor to *is armed* <note>original: "armasi"</note>, aided by the Latin text *hatreds are armed* <note>original: "armentur odia"</note>), and very often one enters into the dangers of life. And among these things, there is no lack of sorrows."

<summary>This page discusses the philological challenges of editing corrupted manuscript copies of Albanzani's translation, emphasizing the necessity of using Boccaccio's original Latin text to correct obvious errors made by scribes.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, Donato Albanzani, Giovanni Boccaccio, Hercules, manuscript corruption, translation methodology, philology</keywords>

— XII —

...contentions occur and peace is brief, etc." (It was easy to supply the word *sorrows*, as has been done, by taking it from the text: <note>original: "nec carent ista doloribus"</note> *nor are those things without sorrows*). The Neapolitan editor of Boccaccio's Latin text, therefore, occasionally made use of it; but not always, even when doing so was not just useful, but necessary. For example, in Chapter II, which concerns *Semiramis, queen of the Assyrians*, where it is said (p. 6) that "it was a most certain thing that, having put everything in order and being at rest, one day while having her hair combed by her maids <note>original: "donzelle"</note>, according to the custom of the country having her hair braided, and having not yet combed more than half of it, it happened that it was announced to her that Babylon had rebelled and had come under the lordship of a stepson of hers. This thing she took so poorly that she threw away the comb, immediately left behind the feminine task, rose up in anger, and took up arms, and immediately besieged that most strong city"; if in this passage the Neapolitan Editor, reaching the words that follow immediately:

> "And she did not stop to cease those citizens who remained," 

which make no sense, had turned to the Latin source, he would have found there: <note>original: "nec ante quod inordinatorum crinium superfuerat composuit"</note> *nor did she arrange what remained of her disordered hair*, which was more than enough reason to replace the word *citizens* <note>original: "cittadini"</note> with the word *locks* or *hair* <note>original: "crini"</note>, which although cer—

— XIII —

<header>— XIII —</header>

...completely by Albanzani <note>Donato degli Albanzani (c. 1326–1411), a scholar and friend of Petrarch and Boccaccio who translated Boccaccio’s Latin works into Italian</note>, was not understood by the copyist because [writing] in prose and in the first person was always less familiar. At other times, it happened that the Latin text and the Cassino codex <note>A famous manuscript from the Abbey of Monte Cassino</note> were not enough to keep Father Tosti <note>Luigi Tosti (1811–1897), a Benedictine historian and monk at Monte Cassino</note> on track. I return to Chapter XXI, and I pause at the little sermon that Boccaccio gives to lovers, prompted by the effeminacies of Hercules <note>Boccaccio uses Hercules’ period of servitude to Queen Omphale, where he dressed in women's clothes and spun wool, as a warning against the "weakness" of love</note>.

> “Therefore, at the beginning, one must resist; one must restrain the eyes so they do not see vanity, close the ears like the asp, and with opposing (*continual*) labors tame lust, because love offers itself flatteringly to those who do not watch out, and it is pleasant at the first entry (*and it is pleasant at first sight. And if it is received, it delights at the first entry*) with cheerful hope; it encourages one to adorn the body and to refine one's habits (*to adorn the body, for refined habits*), to charms <note>original: "lepori," which can mean wit, grace, or pleasantries</note>, to dances, to songs, to music, to games, to gatherings, and to similar things (*to well-mannered words, to dances, to songs, to music, to games, etc.*).

Here the word *lepore* <note>meaning wit or pleasantry</note> seems so appropriate and natural that one cannot doubt that it fits perfectly. If, however, one considers that the original text has *urban witticisms* <note>original: "facetias urbicas"</note> and not *charms* <note>original: "lepores"</note>; that the Cassino codex consulted by Tosti reads *adorn habits with palore* (*which is an evident <term>metathesis</term> [reversal of letters] of "parole" [words]*); and that in our language we do not...

<summary>This page analyzes a translation error in Boccaccio's work where a manuscript typo ("palore" instead of "parole") led a translator to use the word "lepore" (charm) instead of "words," despite the original Latin referring to "urban witticisms."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological critique of Father Tosti’s edition of Albanzani’s translation of Boccaccio, specifically focusing on "ghost words" or errors that Tosti mistakenly introduced into the Italian lexicon.</meta>

— XIV —

<header>— XIV —</header>

ancient examples of *wit* and *pleasantness* <note>original: "lepore" and "lepidezza"</note> are found—words introduced later—and that all the manuscripts of Albanzani’s vernacular translation contain either *well-mannered words* or *refined speech*, then one must conclude that the word *wit* <note>original: "lepore"</note> was coined by Father Tosti. By his authority, it improperly entered the dictionaries of our language, using the example attributed to Albanzani. And this is not the only word that, in the edition cited by the Crusca <note>The Accademia della Crusca, the primary institution for the study and preservation of the Italian language</note>, had an equal or similar origin, and which has been welcomed with open arms by lexicographers. I have noted several others, though not all, as tracing them all down in detail would require a great expenditure of time in most patient comparisons. One of the words thus coined is *weaving-room* <note>original: "tessoria," a term for a weaver's shop or the art of weaving</note>, which I find accepted in the Turin Dictionary with the example from page 46, taken from the chapter on *Arachne of Asia*, who in Colophon <note>An ancient city in Greek Ionia</note> had *her weaving-room*. The little cross <note>A typographical mark (+) used in dictionaries to denote obsolete or archaic words</note>, an indication of an archaic word which precedes it, is not enough to excuse it, because *tessoria* is an error in place of *tesseria* <note>the standard word for a weaving workshop</note>, which I read in all the manuscripts I have seen, and which is a natural derivation from *to weave* <note>original: "tessere"</note>. The same may be said of *to unweave* <note>original: "ditessere"</note>, also accepted in that Dictionary with the example on page 99 regarding Penelope, who

> “unwove <note>original: "ditesseva"</note> by night secretly everything she had woven by day.”

The two Turin manuscripts have *undid by night*, and the Florentine manuscript, of which I shall speak after having con-

<summary>The author identifies several "ghost words" (like *tessoria* and *ditessere*) that Father Tosti mistakenly included in his edition, which then wrongly entered Italian dictionaries as authentic archaic terms. He argues these were merely transcription errors or neologisms rather than the original vocabulary of Albanzani.</summary>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological critique of the Neapolitan edition of Albanzani’s translation, specifically exposing more "ghost words"—errors created by the previous editor's misreadings of the manuscripts.</meta>

->XV<-

concluded regarding the Neapolitan edition, he secretly undid at night <note>A metaphorical reference to Penelope’s shroud, suggesting that what was built in the edition was undermined by its own errors</note>. If the Monte Cassino manuscript <note>An ancient manuscript from the famous abbey of Monte Cassino</note> contains *to unweave* <note>original: "ditessere"</note>, which does not seem to belong to Albanzani, it would have been proper to mention it in a note, because there is no other known example of that verb. And certainly that ugly word *re-hindered* <note>original: "rimpacciata"</note> (p. 172) does not belong to Albanzani; it was pinned onto Marcia <note>Marcia Catonis, a Roman noblewoman known for her virtue</note>, saying she was "not bound by the priesthood, nor by Vesta, nor by a vow made to Diana, nor *re-hindered* by any other profession." In truth, it is the very common word *hindered* <note>original: "impacciata"</note>; the *r* prefix, which indicates repetition, is out of place in this context. Even worse than *re-hindered* is the word *tamxious* <note>original: "mansiosa"</note> added to *toil*, found in the first chapter on Eve on page 3: "they led themselves and all their descendants for the time that was to follow, from a delightful homeland of eternal rest, into *tamxious* toil." Here, *tamxious* <note>original: "mansiosa"</note> is derived from *in anxious* <note>original: "in ansiosa"</note>, the editor having read an *m* instead of *in*. So as not to become overly tedious to the reader by continuing in this tone, I have preferred to collect the erroneous forms from the Neapolitan edition—now corrected—into a List. At first glance, this list will seem enormous, but it will actually appear meager and incomplete to anyone who takes the trouble to compare the two editions. And, even more than from the list itself, from such a comparison the reader will see that the need for a new edition of our vernacular translation <term>volgarizzamento: the medieval practice of translating Latin texts into the common Italian language</term> arose not only from the errors with which the Neapolitan edition is filled, but from another side—

He justifies the necessity of this new, corrected edition by citing the sheer volume of these linguistic mistakes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Neapolitan edition of Albanzani’s translation of Boccaccio's work. The author transitions from pointing out specific "ghost words" to a broader stylistic critique, arguing that the previous editor failed to capture the authentic spirit of 14th-century Italian prose.</meta>

— XVI —

<header>— XVI —</header>

no less blameworthy in it <note>Referring to the Neapolitan edition</note>, lying in the lack of an appropriate coloring and a movement suitable to a classic prose of the **Trecento** <term>Trecento: The 1300s, a golden age of Italian literature dominated by Dante, Petrarch, and Boccaccio</term>. Because, just as every writer has their own style, so every century (especially the early ones of our language) has its own imprint, movement, and coloring—qualities which, even when reducing the spelling of that time to our own (a thing that becomes necessary if, due to the work of the **copyists** <term>original: "menanti," professional scribes who often introduced errors while transcribing manuscripts</term>, we cannot grasp the genuine spelling of the **fourteenth-century authors** <term>original: "trecentisti"</term>), can in some way be altered, but not removed; otherwise, they are ruined so much as to be unrecognizable. To better clarify these thoughts, I present the entire, beautiful Chapter XII regarding *Thisbe, a maiden of Babylon*, also included in full by Mr. Hortis in his fine book on the Latin works of the **man from Certaldo** <note>original: "Certaldese," a reference to Giovanni Boccaccio, who was born in or associated with the town of Certaldo</note>, adding in a note the reading he followed—not to blame him for having accepted the **Neapolitan edition**, even when it is clearly erroneous (since it was not his duty to track down and correct such errors), but rather to further establish that praising certain books simply because they were edited by famous men is in itself harmful, and a constant cause of damage to **good literature** <term>original: "buone lettere," the humanities or literary arts</term>.

<summary>The author argues that the Neapolitan edition fails to capture the unique stylistic "coloring" of 14th-century Italian and warns against trusting editions solely based on the editor's fame.</summary>
<keywords>Trecento, prose style, Giovanni Boccaccio, Attilio Hortis, literary criticism, philology, copyists</keywords>

(1) **Help** <note>original: "Ajuto"</note> also used by Hortis, with a manifest error: *Even if we have not had from our ancestors who her parents were* <note>original Latin: "Huius et si non a maioribus nostris qui parentes fuerint habuerimus." The error lies in the Italian translator misreading the Latin "habuerimus" (we had) as "ajuto" (help).</note>.

(2) The agreement of all the **manuscripts** <term>original: "codici"</term> does not allow for a reading different from this one, even though it does not agree with the [original Latin] text: *it was nonetheless sufficiently believed that within Babylon she had houses adjoining those of Pyramus, a boy of her own age* <note>original Latin: "intra tamen Babyloniam habuisse cum Pyramo aetatis suae puero contiguas domos satis creditum est"</note>; that is to say: it was nonetheless believed that she had houses in Babylon joined to those of Pyramus, a youth of her age. Leaving the **vernacular translation** <term>original: "volgarizzamento," a translation from a classical language (Latin) into the common tongue (Italian)</term> as it is in the manuscripts, we add in a note what it ought to be <note>The author is pointing out that the medieval translator mistook "adjoining houses" (contiguas domos) for "close relatives" (vicina congiunta).</note>.

(1) Hortis <note>Attilio Hortis, a 19th-century scholar</note>: "living together continually, the childhood affection worked in those, being children, such that." This is a transposition that has no authority from any manuscript <term>original: "codice"</term>.

(2) *As their souls grew* <note>original: "crescendo gli animi"</note> instead of *as the years grew* <note>original: "crescendo gli anni"</note> from the Neapolitan print and Hortis's version is an error condemned by both common sense and the original text: *with increasing years* <note>original Latin: "crescentibus annis"</note>.

(3) *Childhood love* <note>original: "l'amore della puerizia"</note>, which governs the phrase *grew into the greatest passion*, is also required by the Latin: *unfair fate brought it about that... childhood love was increased into a maximum fire* <note>original Latin: "egit iniqua sors ut . . . . puerilis amor in maximum augeretur incendium"</note>, and it is found in the manuscripts.

(4) *At least through signs* <note>original: "almeno per cenni"</note> is found in the manuscripts and the Latin: *and they revealed that [love] between themselves at least by nods* <note>original Latin: "illudque inter se nutibus saltem aperirent"</note>.

| | |

| both several times **by habit**, speaking somewhat together, through the wall that was in between, not feeling ashamed, the freedom to reveal **their** intention **was widened**, so that many times they **revealed** their sighs, tears, and desires, and all their passions; sometimes they prayed for the peace **of their** souls: **embracing and kissing each other** with devotion, faith, and perpetual love (4). **But finally, as their ardor grew, they began to flee and determined that on the following night** they would deceive their families and u- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "uscire," meaning "go out" or "depart."</note> | to which fissure both going secretly several times, **and, by habit** (1), speaking somewhat together, through the wall that was in between, being **less** (2) ashamed, they **widened** the freedom to reveal **their** intention, so that many times they **disclosed** sighs, tears, desires, and all their passions (3). Sometimes they prayed **together** for the peace **of their** souls, **to be able to embrace and kiss each other** with devotion, faith, and perpetual love. Finally, as their ardor grew, **they began to make** |

---

(1) *By habit* does not refer to the trips they made to the crack in the wall, but concerns the speaking together of Pyramus and Thisbe: <note>original Latin: "Ad quam (rimulam) cum clam convenissent saepius, et consuetudine paululum colloquendo..."</note> "To which (fissure) when they had met secretly more often, and by the habit of talking a little..."—the source text.

(2) *Less ashamed* is found in the manuscripts I have seen, and follows the Latin: <note>original Latin: "minus erubescebant"</note> "they were less ashamed."

(3) From *And certainly* until *perpetual love*, the Neapolitan printed edition makes a single sentence out of four periods. This long phrasing is characteristic of Boccaccio, and often harms the clarity of the narrative. Here, however, the text is appropriately divided, as the <term>volgarizzamento</term> <note>A "volgarizzamento" is a medieval or Renaissance translation from Latin into the "vulgar" or common Italian tongue.</note> is in the manuscripts.

(4) The jumble in the printed edition is enormous here, such that Mr. Hortis <note>Attilio Hortis (1850–1926), a scholar of Boccaccio.</note>, unable to let it pass, had to change it to: *sometimes they invoked the peace of their souls, embraces, kisses, faith, and perpetual love*, which is not by Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani, a friend of Boccaccio and translator of this work.</note>, even though it conforms to the original Latin text more than his own translation does.

> (1) **"They began to flee"** <note>original Italian: "Incominciarono a fuggire"</note> is a huge blunder, which not even Mr. Hortis <note>Attilio Hortis (1850–1926), an Italian scholar and librarian who studied Boccaccio.</note> noticed, otherwise he would have avoided it, guided by the source text, *they entered into a plan regarding flight* <note>original Latin: "de fuga inivere consilium"</note>, and by the sense, *and they determined in the following night to deceive their family*. Therefore, they did not "begin" to flee [at that moment].
> 
> (2) *Whoever first could deceive their family*, with the entire construction that follows, is found not only in the manuscripts but in the source text: *whoever first could deceive his own, should leave the house and in turn, if anyone should escape first, going away into the nearest grove of the city... should wait for the later one* <note>original Latin: "primus quis posset suos fallere, domo exiret et se invicem, si quis prior evaderet, in nemus civitatis proximum abiens .... tardiorem expectaret"</note>. Mr. Hortis did not have the time to notice it. I have hesitated to leave "small wood" <note>term: *boschetto* — a small wood or grove</note> which is in the manuscripts I have seen, perhaps because it did not seem likely to Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani, a friend of Boccaccio and early translator.</note> that there would be a [large] forest near Babylon containing the tomb of King Ninus. It is certain that in the manuscripts, what is called a "small wood" here is later called a "forest" <note>term: *bosco* — a larger forest</note>.

| ...ate, found the cloak, tore it with its claws, left it somewhat bloodied, and departed (1). In the meantime, Pyramus, having similarly left his house, arrived at the forest and found the cloak; and keeping watch through the silent night, and seeing it torn, he thought that Thisbe had been devoured by **that** (3) beast. With much weeping, his voice **resounded** <note>The original Italian "rinsonava" is a variant of "risonava" (resounded/echoed).</note> in that place, calling himself wretched for having been the cause of the cruel death of his beloved girl. Scorning to live any longer, he drew out the sword he had brought with him, and being prepared to die near the fountain, which he **was already near**, he plunged it into his chest. **Instantly** Thisbe— | ...movement of things) she fled because of a lion that was coming to the spring, leaving the cloak behind by **oversight** (2). The lion, having fed, after it had drunk, found the cloak, tore it with its claws, left it somewhat bloodied, and departed. In the meantime, Pyramus, having similarly left his house, reached the forest and, having found the cloak (keeping watch through the silent night) and seeing it torn, he thought that Thisbe had been devoured by **some** beast. With much weeping, his voice **resounded** (4) in that place, calling himself wretched and for having been the cause of the cruel death of his beloved girl. And, scorning to live any longer, having drawn... |

***

(1) Thisbe having forgotten the cloak was a *misfortune* <note>Italian: *disavventura*</note> because of what followed; but it was by *forgetfulness* and not by *misfortune* that she left it there. And so Boccaccio said: *leaving the cloak inadvertently* <note>original Latin: "relicto inadvententer pallio"</note>.

(2) Here too many instances of *lo* <note>In older Italian or specific dialects, "lo" was frequently used as a masculine article ("the") or a suffix for "it," which the commentator here finds repetitive or unrefined.</note> occur: *Lo* lion, *lo* cloak, *straciollo* [tore it], *lasciollo* [left it], which, as it seems to some, are not an indication of an ancient form. They indicate, however, dialectal modes that are still alive, and in very poor taste.

(3) Not **that** beast <note>Italian: *quella fiera*</note>, which is also found in Hortis <note>Attilio Hortis, a 19th-century scholar of Boccaccio.</note>, but rather **some** beast <note>Italian: *qualche fiera*</note>; for Pyramus knew nothing of *that specific beast*, and believed instead it was *some beast* or other.

(4) I find *risonava* [resounded] in all the codices <note term="codices">Handwritten manuscript volumes, usually of ancient or medieval origin.</note>, and not *rinsonava*.

Then "request" <note>original: "mandagione"</note> here is a very affected clumsiness, which in place of the "petition" <note>original Latin: "postulatum"</note> of the text—also an ugly word—supposes instead "formal request" <note>original Latin: "postulationem"</note>.

(1) More than wounded, he was dead. "With her blood spilled, she followed the soul of him who was already dead" <note>original Latin: "Effuso sanguine secuta est animam iam defuncti"</note>.

<term>Mandagione</term> — An archaic, somewhat awkward Italian term for a command or request, criticized by the commentator for being "clumsy."
<term>Puerizia</term> — Childhood or adolescence; the text emphasizes that their love began when they were children.

### — XXV —

| | |
|:---|:---|
| the **unhappy parents** (1). Little by little, for certain, the wills of **men** (2) must be restrained, so that, by wanting to oppose his sudden **empire** (3), they are not pushed through desperation into danger. **Desirous and intemperate** passion is almost like a plague and a torment of the youth, in whom certainly **he** (4) must | who are free from marriage, which could have followed. **The worst fortune erred, and perhaps the unhappy parents sinned.** Little by little, for certain, the wills of the youth must be restrained, so that, by wanting to oppose his sudden **impetus, we do not** push them through desperation into danger. The passion **of the desirous is without tem-** |

***

(1) *And perhaps the unhappy parents sinned*, as an insertion, does not agree with the preceding sentence. Instead, it fits well afterward: *The worst fortune erred*, in conformity with the text: <note>original: "Peccavit sors pessima, et forsan miseri peccavere parentes."</note> "Bad luck sinned, and perhaps the wretched parents sinned."

(2) *Men* do not belong here, except insofar as the youth are also men; but the sentiment is appropriate for youth: <note>original: "Sensim quidem frenandi sunt iuvenum impetus."</note> "Indeed, the impulses of youth must be gradually restrained."

(3) The text already cited speaks of *impulses* and not *empires*, <note>The translator is noting a likely error in the manuscript where the word "impeto" (impulse) was misread or miswritten as "imperio" (empire/command).</note> even though certain youthful impulses, precisely because they are unrestrained, do not just command—and here we are—they tyrannize. <note>original: "Sensim quidem frenandi sunt iuvenum impetus, ne dum repentino obice illis obsistere volumus desperantes in praecipitium impellamus."</note> "Indeed, the impulses of youth must be gradually restrained, lest while we want to resist them with a sudden obstacle, we drive those who are despairing into a precipice." Except that today’s youth do not go over the precipice themselves; they send others there.

(4) This is truly the case to repeat the marvels, or rather the astonishment, of my literature professor (after Fornaciari <note>Raffaello Fornaciari was a noted 19th-century Italian philologist.</note> in Lucca) at the Roman Sapienza, the ex-Jesuit Abbot Luigi M. Rezzi, whenever he began to explain the famous ode of *The Fifth of May* <note>Alessandro Manzoni’s famous poem on the death of Napoleon Bonaparte.</note>, which did not just fail to please him—it got on his nerves. "*He was*," he would begin; that was nearly half a century ago. "*Who is this 'He'?*" At first, it isn't understood. To be clear, he should have said (and Manzoni did not name him): *Napoleon* was. So I say here. What is this "**he**"? Since it depends on the word "passion" <note>In Italian, "passion" (passione) is a feminine noun.</note>, it would be better to say "**she**" or "**it**." But in the manuscripts, it is "**she**."

-> XXVII <-

<header>— XXVII —</header>

of the Neapolitan printing, <note>original: "stampa napoletana." This refers to the edition by Luigi Tosti mentioned on the previous page.</note> it is no wonder if others fall into error, and one should at least pity whoever takes the trouble to procure a new edition, possibly free of such serious faults. And this was my purpose: in deriving from the two Turin manuscripts <note>original: "codici torinesi." These are handwritten books held in the National Library of Turin used as primary sources for this edition.</note> described above a **vernacular text** <note>original: "testo volgare." A translation into the common Italian language of the time, as opposed to the original Latin.</note> which seemed to me destined to turn out much better than the printed one, both thanks to the help of the two said manuscripts, and because the **Latin text** was kept constantly as a comparison. However, I soon realized that while the new help provided to me, and the great care I spent on those manuscripts, seemed sufficient to others for producing the translation <note>original: "volgarizzamento."</note> of the ***Famous Women*** with a better reading than the Neapolitan one, they were not enough for me to be fully satisfied. Therefore, I did not consent to publish that work then, as I had been requested, even though it might have seemed prepared for the press. To achieve something that appeared less imperfect even to my eyes, I should have started from a review of the Latin text, which is known by most through the Basel edition of 1539. This edition, though rare and highly sought after for its **woodcuts** <note>original: "silografie."</note> with the initials ***I. K.*** (which are explained as *Jakob Köbel* or *Jakob Kerver*) <note>Köbel and Kerver were noted 16th-century printers and engravers; their work added significant value to Renaissance books.</note> is frequently incorrect. -> XXVIII <-

...having made use of the Turin manuscript, marked L. I. 67 (1), I went specifically to Florence to study the three Laurentian manuscripts <note>original: "codici laurenziani." These are manuscripts held in the Laurentian Library (Biblioteca Medicea Laurenziana) in Florence, designed by Michelangelo.</note> of ***Of Famous Women***, which are located at Shelf XC, 98. 1, 2, and 3. <note>original: "Pluteo." A "pluteo" refers to the historic reading benches/shelves where manuscripts are still chained today in the Laurentian Library.</note> In confirmation of this, I cite the intricate passage—

---

(1) Since it was too briefly described by Pasini in the aforementioned work (Vol. II, p. 357), I provide more extensive information here, taken from Vernazza’s letter to Scioppalalba. The parchment manuscript <note>original: "codice pergameno." A book made of animal skin pages rather than paper.</note> containing the work ***Of Famous Women*** begins: *Giovanni Boccaccio of Certaldo's preface to the illustrious and exalted lady, Lady Andrea, formerly of Monteodorisio and then the noble Countess of Altavilla, for the book on famous women.* <note>original: "Iohanes boccaccius de certaldo... prefatio." Andrea Acciaioli was a Florentine noblewoman and a friend of Boccaccio.</note>

Recently, most excellent lady, withdrawing a little from the idle crowd and being nearly free from other cares, I wrote a small book—more for the solace of friends than for the great benefit of the republic—to the extraordinary praise of the female sex. However, while I was considering in my mind to whom I should first send it, so that it would not wither away in idleness in my possession and might go out into public more securely supported by another's favor, I realized it would be fitting to destine it not to a prince, but rather, since it speaks of women, to some distinguished woman. As I searched for one more worthy than the rest, there came to mind 그 brilliant Italian light, famous not only for the glory of women but for that of kings: the most serene Joanna, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily, whose famous lineage and the brilliance of her ancestors having been weighed, etc. <note>original: "Pridie, mulierum egregia... Iohanna serenissima yerusalem et sicilie regina." This refers to Queen Joanna I of Naples (1326–1382).</note>

*It ends:* And if there is any spirit of pious charity in them, let them correct and amend these weakly written things by adding or removing, so that the work may flourish for the good of someone rather than perish, torn by the teeth of the envious, to the benefit of no one. <note>original: "Et si quis illis pie caritatis spiritus est..." This is a standard "humility formula" used by medieval and Renaissance authors.</note>

*The book of Giovanni Boccaccio of Certaldo, "Of Famous Women," ends; thanks be to God. Amen.* <note>original: "De mulieribus claris liber explicit." The "explicit" is the formal closing statement of a manuscript.</note> *In another hand:* This book belongs to me, Christopher de Carreto. — According to Vernazza, agreeing in this with Pasini, the manuscript consists of 98 *leaves* or *196 pages.*

<meta>This page continues the author's detailed comparison of different versions of Boccaccio's work, focusing specifically on how errors in printed editions can be corrected by consulting the original classical sources Boccaccio used.</meta>

-> XXIX <-

...the end of Chapter 69, ***Theoxena, Queen of Thessaly***, at the point where Poris <note>Poris was the husband of Theoxena; they were fleeing the persecution of King Philip V of Macedon.</note> pretends to go from Thessalonica to the temple for a sacrifice. The Latin text in the Swiss edition <note>Refers to an early printed edition of Boccaccio's work produced in Switzerland.</note> is unintelligible: *when he had pretended that he was going from Thessalonica to the bronze statue for the annual sacrifice to Aeneas the founder...* <note>original: "cum finxisset se a Thesalonica aeneam ad statuam quotannis Aeneae conditori sacrificium profecturum." The error lies in the phrase "aeneam ad statuam" (to the bronze statue).</note> which in the Neapolitan printing is rendered, making use in part of the translation by Betussi <note>Giuseppe Betussi was a 16th-century writer known for translating Boccaccio's Latin works into the Italian vernacular.</note>: "but having made a show of going to Thessalonica for the sacrifice to the statue of Aeneas the builder, to whom he sacrificed every year..." The Laurentian manuscripts do not correct it completely, yet they improve it significantly, reading: *when he had pretended that he was going from Thessalonica to Aenea for the appointed annual sacrifice to Aeneas the founder* <note>original: "Eneam ad statutum." This changes "bronze statue" back to the city of "Aenea" and "appointed" sacrifice.</note>; and Titus Livy <note>Livy was a Roman historian; Boccaccio used his "History of Rome" as a primary source for many of these biographies.</note> in Chapter 4 of Book 40, from which this narrative is taken, clarifies it finally: *They set out from Thessalonica for Aenea* (which here is a place name) *for the regular sacrifice, which they perform every year with great ceremony for Aeneas the founder.* <note>original: "Aenean... ad statum sacrificium." The city of Aenea was located in Macedonia, named after the Trojan hero.</note> And here we see another method of assistance for providing a good text of ***Famous Women***, which lies in returning to the sources from which Boccaccio took the substance of his stories. Father Tosti <note>Luigi Tosti was a Benedictine monk and historian who also edited Boccaccio.</note> gave no thought to this. Mr. Hortis <note>Attilio Hortis was a celebrated 19th-century scholar and librarian who did extensive work on Boccaccio's Latin writings.</note> instead has taken diligent care of it; nor was I negligent in this regard, as can be seen from the notes appended to several chap-

<meta>This page continues the editor's discussion of textual sources, moving from the identification of classical references like Livy to the description of specific manuscripts used to reconstruct the Italian translation of Boccaccio's work.</meta>

— XXX —

...taken, at least in part, now from Tacitus, now from Titus Livy, now from Orosius, or from Valerius Maximus, or from others. However, in this particular matter, my research was not always fully successful, as certain sources—which at first glance I was unable to discover just when I needed them most—were discovered by me later, when the printing was already, without any remedy, well underway. With all the aforementioned aids, the new text had progressed considerably, fashioned essentially upon the two Turin manuscripts <term>codices: handwritten books, particularly ancient ones</term> already described, when my good fortune led me to stumble upon a third manuscript of Albanzani's translation <note>Donato degli Albanzani, also known as Donato da Casentino, was a scholar and friend of Boccaccio who translated the original Latin "De Mulieribus Claris" into the Italian vernacular.</note>, kept in the National Library of Florence. It is on paper and in folio format.

It begins: *Here begins the Book of Famous Women compiled by Master Giovanni Boccaccio at the request of the most famous Queen Joanna of Puglia <note>Joanna I of Naples, to whom Boccaccio dedicated the original Latin work.</note>. It was then translated into the vernacular tongue by Master Donato da Casentino for the magnificent Marquis Niccolò d’Este, Prince and Lord of Ferrara <note>Niccolò II d’Este (1338–1388), a prominent patron of the arts in Ferrara.</note>. Prologue and beginning of the first history of Lady Eve, our first mother.*

"Since I must write of the virtues for which famous women are known, it will not seem an unworthy thing to take the beginning from her who was the first mother of all, Lady Eve."

*Ends.* Finished is the book of famous women compiled by Master Giovanni Boccaccio in—

<summary>The author describes finding a third manuscript of Donato degli Albanzani’s Italian translation of Boccaccio’s "Famous Women" in Florence, noting its dedication to the Marquis of Ferrara.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, Donato degli Albanzani, Famous Women, manuscripts, Florence National Library, Niccolò d'Este, Queen Joanna of Puglia, translation, humanism</keywords>

— XXXI —

...request of the most famous Queen Joanna of Puglia <note>Joanna I of Naples (1326–1382).</note>. Then translated into the vernacular language by Master Donato da Casentino for the magnificent Marquis Niccolò d’Este, Prince and Lord of Ferrara, etcetera. It was completed in 1395 <note>original: "M.ᵃ ccc.⁰ Lxxxxv."</note>, on the 9th day of April. Transcribed by the hand of Z. Ca., goldsmith (1) <note>original: "orevese," a dialect term for goldsmith.</note>. Although the precise year of Albanzani's death is not known, the eighth day of April 1395, when this manuscript—which I have named the "Florentine code"—was finished, cannot be much later than his death. This circumstance, which should be highly valued, along with the true quality of the text in this manuscript, makes it more precious than the other three known copies. It is true, however, that its scribe was Venetian and a goldsmith, as gathered from the word *goldsmith* <note>original: "orevese"</note>, which in that dialect refers to the professionals of that art. Nonetheless, as he was perhaps nothing more than a copyist, he did not have the opportunity to distort the work, which he likely would have done, if...

***

(1) Being unable to return to Florence once again for a long stay to compare this new <term>codice: a manuscript volume</term> with the text I had already drafted based on the two previously mentioned Turin manuscripts, I obtained—through the very effective mediation of Commander Zambrini, the illustrious president of our Commission—a copy made by Count Luigi Passerini, librarian of the joined Magliabechiana and Palatina libraries. At the bottom, it is authenticated by him as follows: *This copy, having been collated, agrees with its original. Florence, July 17, 1874.* I say this to express my gratitude.

<meta>This page continues the editor’s philological analysis of the "Florentine manuscript" of Boccaccio’s "Famous Women," comparing its specific word choices and linguistic preferences against the "Turin manuscripts" (A and B) to determine the most accurate reading for this edition.</meta>

--- XXXII ---

...having carried it out at a later time, he might have had a less correct text before him. In comparing the reading of this <term>codice: a manuscript volume</term> with that of the Turin manuscripts, I found a quite remarkable preference for certain words that are very rarely or never encountered in the others. The word *to bow* or *to bow down* <note>original: "chinare" or "chinarsi"</note> is constantly used there in place of *to bend* or *to yield* <note>original: "piegare" or "piegarsi"</note> found in manuscripts A and B; and as long as it concerns *bowing one's eyes*, *one's face*, or similar, it can be more appropriate, and sometimes elegant. But when in Chapter LXXIV I find Sempronia of the Gracchi <note>A Roman woman, sister of the famous reformers Tiberius and Gaius Gracchus, known for her strength of character.</note>, who could not be *bowed* or *induced* by any authority or threats to do what she was commanded—this being a matter of a violent act—I have placed *bent* <note>original: "piegata"</note> following the other manuscripts. This is because *to bow* is not always *to bend*, and for Sempronia one could have applied the phrase *I shall be broken, but not bent* <note>original: "frangar non flectar"</note>, which, in both a literal and figurative sense, can never be equivalent to *to bow*. Similarly, the Florentine manuscript always has *to reach* <note>original: "giungere"</note> where the others have *to arrive* <note>original: "arrivare"</note>, two words which are not always synonymous. On page 256, the Roman Sempronia *attained the boldness of a man* <note>original: "arrivò a presunzioni d' uomo"; here "presunzioni" implies taking on a role or courage typically reserved for men in that era.</note>, and there *attained* pleases me more than *reached*; whereas on page 267, regarding Portia <note>Portia Catonis (c. 70 BC – 42 BC), the daughter of Cato the Younger and wife of Brutus, famous for her devotion and courage.</note>, daughter of Cato, in the sentence: *Which things, because they reached her eternal praise, are voluntarily preferred to increase her fame*, I have accepted *reached* from the Florentine manuscript, and not *arrived*, insofar as *reached*, re-

<summary>The editor explains his linguistic choices in the edition, specifically why he prefers certain verbs like "to bend" or "to reach" over others based on the character of the historical women described, such as Sempronia and Portia.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, philology, Sempronia of the Gracchi, Portia daughter of Cato, Florentine manuscript, Turin manuscripts, translation choices, vocabulary</keywords>

->XXXIII<-
->III<-

...corresponding to the Latin *evasere* <note>original: "evasere," meaning to emerge or turn out</note> (*they emerged into everlasting praise* <note>original: "in perpetuam evasere laudem"</note>) is almost "they attained" in the sense of "they succeeded." On this basis, I could continue for a long while and discuss the words *cheerful* <note>original: "lieto"</note> and *joyful* <note>original: "allegro"</note>, the latter used in the Turin <term>codici: manuscript volumes</term> and the former in the Florentine, even though *cheerful* does not always correspond to *joyful*: A person who has become habitually mournful due to deep, indelible grief can be *cheerful* at times, but *joyful* never. Regarding the pronouns *she*, *her*, *he*, *him*, *they*, and *them* <note>original: "ella, lei, egli, lui, eglino, loro"</note>, there would be a very long discussion to be had, as one finds almost constantly *her* and *them* used in the nominative case <note>In older formal Italian, "ella" and "egli" were the standard subject pronouns; the use of "lei" and "lui" as subjects was once considered more colloquial or regional.</note> in one manuscript, and *she*, *they*, etc., in another. These and similar variations, which I encountered at every step in the manuscripts, have often kept me troubled and perplexed, as I did not always have a sure guide to be able to choose the <term>sincere reading: the most authentic or original version of a text</term>; whereby I have sometimes found myself forced to use my own judgment, guided by experience with ancient writings and by what we call—with a poorly defined term—"taste." But it is now time for me to take my leave of the reader (not only for this short <term>proemio: a formal introductory preface</term>, which is a very small thing, even less than what I proposed to do had I the time, but for the entire book), which reader I have always had before me as if I were in conversation with him; and I make him a...

<meta>This page continues the editor’s preface, transitioning from a technical discussion of linguistic choices to a humble reflection on the difficulties of the editorial process and the quality of existing editions of Boccaccio's work.</meta>

-> — XXXIV — <-

an open and frank confession in keeping with my character.

Regarding this work—conducted fitfully over twenty years because it was often interrupted against my will—I have already told you, my dear Reader, of its unfortunate origin, which inevitably leaves its mark upon the result. Having set out to correct almost infinite errors, not only did I fail to notice several (and even when I did notice them, I was not always in time to correct them, which would be the lesser evil), but I have added new ones to the old; for if it is true that those who walk with the lame learn to limp, it must also be true that those who wander among blunders—which are moral limps—learn to blunder themselves. And the blunders in the book *On Famous Women* <note>original: "De claris mulieribus"</note> are everywhere, starting with the text itself. If I did not already have sixty-five years <term>thirteen lustra: a lustrum is a five-year period; the author is noting his advanced age</term> behind me—and unfortunately poorly spent—in returning to this enterprise, I would start from the text mangled in the few, and all foreign, editions that exist. It would be a work of much trouble, but not difficult, to restore it entirely with the help of the many <term>codices: ancient manuscript volumes</term> in our libraries, comparing it with the passages of the authors whom Boccaccio continually utilized. Moving from the text to the <term>volgarizzamenti: translations from Latin into the common Italian vernacular</term> of the book of *Famous Women* <note>original: "Donne famose"</note>, I would not stop at the many and entirely ancient ones we possess—which are far inferior to that of Master Donato degli Albanzani—but I would compare...

<meta>This page continues the editor’s discussion regarding the selection of manuscripts and the methodology for his edition of Boccaccio's work. He explains why he chose the translation by Donato Albanzani and argues for a more rigorous critical approach to editing historical texts.</meta>

-> — XXXV — <-

...I would consult them in doubtful places, as one can infer from them the quality of the reading <term>reading: a specific version of a text found in a particular manuscript</term> of the text that each translator <note>original: "volgarizzatore"</note> had before him. And deciding upon the translation by Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani (c. 1328–1411) was a scholar and close friend of Petrarch and Boccaccio; he was famous for translating Latin works into the Italian vernacular</note>; setting aside the Neapolitan edition—useful only where it conforms faithfully to the Cassinese codex <note>A manuscript from the ancient Benedictine abbey of Monte Cassino</note>—I could not satisfy myself with only three manuscripts <note>original: "codici"</note>, namely, the two from Turin that I patiently copied myself, and the Florentine one kept constantly for comparison for the new text I am now publishing. I say this because they vary too much from one another, giving proof of the frequent alterations made by the scribes <term>menanti: professional copyists or scribes, often criticized by scholars for making errors while transcribing</term>.

Instead, I would seek out several others of which we have clues, and which must be found especially in the Roman libraries, which have been made much more accessible and useful today. Having thus facilitated a reliable reading of this beautiful prose, I would have reserved the notes for the critical section, regarding the choice of readings, both in terms of substance and in terms of grammatical value and form. This last part requires new care in almost all our historical texts; it is not simply to point out, as I have most often had to do, serious and frequent errors which, when included in works of great importance, result in greater damage to the world of literature. For modern criticism has the duty to abandon a method—certainly not entirely useless for literary studies, yet most insufficient—such as that followed by some, of pub- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the word "publishing" on the next page.</note>

<summary>The editor explains his preference for Albanzani's translation and his struggle with inconsistent manuscripts from Turin and Florence. He calls for a higher standard of literary criticism that moves beyond merely noting errors to performing deep grammatical and substantive analysis.</summary>
<keywords>Donato Albanzani, textual criticism, manuscripts, Cassinese codex, scribal errors, Italian prose, literary methodology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the editor’s discussion regarding the selection of manuscripts and the methodology for his edition of Boccaccio's work. He transitions from a discussion of specific source materials to his broader philosophy of "popularizing" classic Italian texts for the modern reader by modernizing their presentation.</meta>

-> — XXXVI — <-

...to publish the manuscripts <term>codici: handwritten books from the era before the printing press</term> or republish the first printed editions <term>stampe: early printed versions of a text</term> of high-quality vernacular writings <term>scritture volgari: texts written in the common Italian language rather than Latin</term> (I exclude autograph manuscripts <term>manoscritti autografi: texts written in the author's own hand</term> and the editions based directly upon them), and instead embrace the alternative: to derive from those manuscripts and printed editions a text that, as much as possible, approaches the author's original work, while adapting its spelling <term>ortografia: the conventional system of spelling</term> to modern usage. These intentions, the implementation of which can only succeed in popularizing <note>original: "volgarizzare" — in this context, the editor means making the text accessible to the general public rather than just scholars</note> the reading of our classics <term>classici: the foundational works of Italian literature</term>, are frequently mentioned and put into practice in this volume. If you, my reader, look kindly upon this effort, I shall take heart to provide you with other ancient vernacular works, which we currently continue to read in forms that they certainly did not possess at their origin.

<summary>The editor explains his choice to modernize spelling and reconstruct a "best version" of the text to make Italian classics accessible to the public. He hopes that this methodology will be successful enough to allow him to edit other historical vernacular works in the same way.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, vernacular literature, philology, editorial method, orthography, manuscripts, Boccaccio, Italian classics</keywords>

| | ...gives witness of it. | p. 22. | For a symbol, a cow: and suitable for many things.... | For a symbol, a cow: and with great ingenuity, and suitable for many things <note>The cow was a sacred symbol of several ancient goddesses, including Io, Isis, and Hera/Juno.</note>.... |
| » » | Giving laws to them civilly. | And he gave them laws to live civilly <note>Referring to the transition from "barbaric" states to structured society through the introduction of legal codes.</note>. |
| » 23. | And there is no doubt, that this honor passed.... | And there is no doubt that this error passed.... |
| » 24. | Which also she cannot wash so much.... | Which she cannot afterwards wash as well.... |
| » 27. | They sawed off her right breast. | They dried up her right breast <note>A classic reference to the Amazons, who were said to remove or cauterize the right breast so it would not interfere with their use of the bow or spear.</note>. |
| » 28. | And although we cannot have help.... | And although we cannot have had.... |
| » 29. | Spirits growing. | Growing in years <note>The correction changes "animi" (minds/spirits) to "anni" (years), suggesting the passage describes the physical aging of characters rather than their increasing courage.</note>. |
| » 30. | At times they prayed for the peace of their souls: embracing, kissing with piety (*there was a wall between them*). | At times they prayed together for the peace of their souls, that they might embrace and kiss each other with piety. |
| » » | But finally, as their passion grew, they began to flee. | But finally, as their passion grew, they began to take counsel on fleeing <note>The mention of a "wall between them" and "taking counsel to flee" strongly suggests the myth of Pyramus and Thisbe, the ill-fated Babylonian lovers who spoke through a crack in a wall.</note>. |

**Vocabulary:**
witnessing, young girls, shores, cow, civilly, passed (or elapsed), also, breast, help, spirits (or minds), embracing, kissing, to flee

<summary>This page lists corrections for a manuscript concerning ancient history and mythology, touching upon the symbol of the cow, the customs of the Amazons, and the tragic narrative of lovers separated by a wall.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, philology, Amazons, Pyramus and Thisbe, ancient laws, mythology, symbolism, ancient customs</keywords>

XLIII

| Reference | Original Phrasing | Revised Phrasing |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| p. 30. | They decided in the following night to deceive their kin, and leave home, and go to a forest near the city. | They decided that on the coming night, whoever could first deceive their kin <note>The Italian *i suoi* refers to one's family or household.</note>, should leave the house and go to a small grove <note>Original: *boschetto*. The revision specifies a smaller wooded area than a *bosco* (forest).</note> near the city. |
| » » | Leaving the cloak by misfortune. | Leaving the cloak by forgetfulness <note>A key plot point in the myth: Thisbe drops her cloak while fleeing a lioness.</note>. |
| » » | Devoured by that beast. | Devoured by some beast <note>The revision makes the threat more general or perhaps emphasizes the uncertainty of the character's fate.</note>. |
| » 31. | Frightened nearly as much, she turned back. | Frightened so much that she nearly turned back. |
| » » | And seeing him meet his end. | And seeing her lover meet his end <note>The revision clarifies the relationship between the characters.</note>. |
| » » | In whatever part or seats where they might go. | In whatever seat they might go <note>Likely referring to the lovers' final resting place or their state in the afterlife.</note>. |
| » » | Not allowing, or rather not being able to deny the final request. | Not enduring to deny the final request. |
| » 32. | The soul of the one who was wounded followed. | The soul of the one who was already finished <note>Original: *finito*, a euphemism for death</note> followed. |
| » » | Which (*marriage*) could follow; and perhaps the wretched parents sinned. | Which could follow. Terrible fortune erred, and perhaps the wretched parents sinned <note>Refers to the parents' refusal to let the lovers marry, which led to their secret meeting and death.</note>. |
| » » | To restrain the will of men. | To restrain the wills of the youths <note>Original: *giovani*. The revision shifts the focus from "men" in general to the specific young age of the protagonists.</note>. |
| » » | To resist its sudden command. | To resist its sudden impulse <note>Original: *impeto*. The change suggests an internal emotional drive rather than an external "command" (*imperio*).</note>. |

| Reference | Original Phrasing | Revised Phrasing |
