| p. 33. | And furthermore giving the daughters to the grandsons <note>The Italian *nepoti* can mean either grandsons or nephews. In the context of the Danaids, they were marrying their cousins.</note>. | And betrothing the daughters to the grandsons. |
| p. 34. | If they wanted his safety <note>Original: *salute*. This can mean health, safety, or salvation. The note "(equivoco)" suggests the previous scribe misunderstood the intended meaning.</note> (*misunderstanding*). | If they wanted their own safety. |
| » » | Those young men being mad with heavy drunkenness. | Those young men being rotten with drunkenness. |
| p. 35. | He did not think how much shame, how much deceit, a detestable example of shame he left, that they should have to be perverse. | He did not think how much audacity, how much deceit, how much of a detestable example of shame he left to his daughters, that they should have to be perverse. |
| p. 36. | But wherever it was, that one of cruel memory was killed by Lynceus <note>Lynceus was the only husband spared by the Danaids; the text notes a correction in the name.</note>. | But wherever it was, that one of cruel memory was killed by Linus. |
| p. 37. | For the noble family of the children. | For the notable <note>Original: *notabile*. The revision shifts the focus from "noble" status to the family being "remarkable" or "worthy of note."</note> family of the children. |
| » » | Venerable Goddess by ancient reason. | Venerable gods by ancient religion. |
| » » | Niobe . . . . having seen her troop of children honored with royal honors. | Niobe . . . . having seen her troop of children, and adorned with royal ornaments. |
| » » | She had born to them, seeing them, fourteen children <note>In the myth, Niobe famously boasted of her fourteen children (the Niobids) to the goddess Leto.</note>. | She had born, seeing them, fourteen children. |
| p. 38. | The wrath of God is more provoked. | The wrath of God is more ready. |
| » » | As did the insipid Niobe. | As did the foolish <note>Original: *insipiente*. This implies a lack of wisdom or spiritual understanding rather than just being "insipid" or dull.</note> Niobe. |
| p. 39. | Pretended to perform the last service for the father. | Pretended to perform the final funeral rites <note>Original: *esequie*. The revision specifies that the "service" was a funeral.</note> for the father. |

# — XLV —

| Page | Original Phrasing | Revised Phrasing |
|:---|:---|:---|
| p. 40. | Led into a perfect age. | Led into a mature age <note>Original: *provetta età*. While the first version used "perfect," the revision specifies "provetta," meaning an advanced or experienced age.</note>. |
| p. 41. | Oetes, the most wicked king of Colchis. | Aeëtes, the most famous king of the Colchians <note>The revision corrects the name to "Eeta" and changes his reputation from "wicked" to "famous."</note>. |
| p. 41. | And similarly instructed. Meanwhile, she had the knowledge of herbs.... | And highly instructed in evil deeds <note>Original: *malefizii*. In this context, it refers to sorcery or harmful magic.</note>, because by whatever master she was instructed, she had such great knowledge of herbs . . . . |
| p. 42. | It seemed to her a very light thing to use the blade. | It seemed to her a very base thing <note>Original: *vilissima*. The correction suggests that using a weapon was beneath her dignity or character, rather than merely "easy."</note> to use the blade. |
| p. 42. | So that her father might have cause to stay his journey. | So that her father might have cause to stop his journey <note>A subtle spelling correction from *restare* to *ristare*.</note>. |
| p. 43. | And wishing to make the kingdom of Jason acquired. | And Medea, wishing to have the kingdom acquired for Jason. |
| p. 44. | These ignorant ones are caught, pulled, snatched away, and held with biting vices by beauty. | These ignorant ones are caught, pulled, snatched away, and held with biting hooks <note>Original: *uncini*. The revision replaces "vices" with "hooks," a more vivid metaphor for the entrapment of physical attraction.</note> by beauty. |
| p. 46. | Where, living, she had her weaving shop <note>Original: *tessoria*. The text notes: "An error passed into Tommaseo's Dictionary with this example." Niccolò Tommaseo was a famous 19th-century Italian lexicographer.</note>. | Where, living, she had her weaving shop <note>Original: *tesseria*. The correct term for a weaving establishment.</note>. |
| p. 46. | And being unable to bear being defeated, with an impatient [spirit]... | And being unable to bear being defeated with a patient soul, she ended [her life] with a noose <note>This likely refers to the myth of Arachne, the weaver who hanged herself after being beaten by Minerva.</note>. |

# — XLVI —

| | | |
|:---|:---|:---|
| | ...with a [patient] spirit, she ended her life with a noose. | ...ended her life. <note>The revision clarifies the logic: "As if the sentence structure were: Not being able to endure, with a patient mind, being defeated."</note> |
| p. 47. | Arachne <note>Original: *Aragne*. The mythological weaver who was turned into a spider.</note> were alone, and were not a solace. | Arachne were alone, and were a solace to us. |
| p. 51. | They stood little more than motionless and in love. | They stood little more than motionless and oblivious <note>Original: *smemorati*. Meaning they were so stunned they forgot themselves.</note>. |
| p. 54. | As if she had been afraid of the lover who was so horrible. | As if she had been afraid of the lover who was so horrible because of his clothing <note>Original: *per lo abito*. Likely referring to Hercules wearing the skin of the Nemean Lion. The note adds: "By removing 'because of his clothing,' Hercules remains horrible in himself to Iole."</note>. |
| p. 56. | With contrary labors. | With continuous labors <note>A correction from *contrarie* to *continue*.</note>. |
| p. 56. | Encourages one to adorn the body, to adorn one's character, to graces <term>lepori</term><note>The term *lepori* refers to wit, pleasantries, or charms.</note>, to dances, to songs.... | Encourages one to adorn the body, to adorned character, to decent words, to dances, to songs. <note>The translator notes that "graces" (*lepore*) was an error created by the Neapolitan edition by misunderstanding *palore*, which is a <term>metathesis</term> (rearranged letters) of *parole* (words).</note> |
| p. 59. | Having already crossed the river, he began to flee with his club <note>Original: *mazza*. Hercules is famously depicted with a club.</note>. | Having already crossed the river with his beloved <note>Original: *amanza*. A poetic term for a sweetheart or mistress.</note>, he began to flee. |
| p. 68. | Even with the unworthy action of the barbarians. | Even with the indignation <note>Original: *indignazione*. A correction from *indegna azione*.</note> of the barbarians. |

It highlights how typographical errors in early editions can obscure the original author's intent.</summary>

| » » | Follow him. | Follow her. |
| » 70. | Forgetful love of gold. <note>Original: *smemorato*. This implies a love so consuming one forgets themselves.</note> | Immoderate love of gold. <note>Original: *smoderato*. A correction from "forgetful" to "excessive" or "unrestrained."</note> |

| » 73. | He found the wounded body. | She touched the stinking body. <note>The correction changes "found" (*trovò*) to "touched" (*toccò*) and "wounded" (*ferito*) to "stinking" (*fetido*), significantly altering the sensory detail of the scene.</note> |
| » » | Thus he had comforted him. | Thus he had comforted her. <note>A grammatical correction of the gendered ending from *-o* to *-a*.</note> |
| » 73. | In which they say there are demonstrations of future things: I know not by what diabolical work, even though he knew by closely observing the veins of sheep. | In which they say there are demonstrations of future things, I know not by what diabolical work. Furthermore, she knew by closely observing the veins of sheep. <note>This refers to <term>haruspicy</term>, an ancient form of divination involving the inspection of the entrails of sacrificed animals.</note> |
| » 75. | To bring back those named, to my power... | To bring back those not named, to my power... |

| | ...because of the idiom of the envious and barbarous vernacular <note>original: "volgare barbaro"; likely referring to a language other than Latin or a corrupted dialect</note>, I think, it has hidden our praises among the passages of the Mediterranean Sea and the caverns of Asia, and taken them away from the Latins. | ...of the barbarous vernacular, I think, it has hidden our praises among the passages of the Mediterranean Sea and the caverns of Asia, and taken them from the Latins. |
| p. 192. | Having dispatched some enemies who remained around the parts of the mountain; against the Gallo-Greeks <note>original: "Gallo-greci"; the Galatians, a Celtic people who settled in central Asia Minor (modern Turkey)</note>, a harsh and barbarous people, he moved a harsh war. | Having dispatched some enemies who remained around the parts of the coastline, having gone by his own will to the regions of the mountains of Asia, against the Gallo-Greeks, a harsh and barbarous people, he moved a cruel war. |
| p. 193. | Upon which work the centurion <term>centurion: a professional officer in the Roman army</term> kept his hand and eyes intent. | Upon which work the centurion kept his mind <note>original: "animo"</note> and eyes intent. |
| » » | With which she returned to the bosom of her own. | With which she returned into the bosom of her people. |
| p. 195. | He yielded to his adultery and to the love of one of his female servants. | He yielded to the love and the adultery of one of his female servants. |
| » » | That you might cause harm to the love owed to her. | That you might act to the detriment of the love owed to her. |
| p. 196. | She could not be rebuked with any reproach of servitude <note>original: "servitù"; the state of being an enslaved person or a servant</note>. | She could not be vituperated <note>original: "vituperata"; blamed or insulted harshly</note> by any reproach of servitude. |

<note>The vocabulary list includes terms like "idioma" (idiom), "volgare" (vernacular), "barbaro" (barbarian/foreigner), and "spelonche" (caves), reflecting the translator's focus on the transition from classical history to the common tongue.</note>

<summary>This page compares linguistic variations in an Italian translation of classical history, specifically documenting wars against the Galatians in Asia and shifting moral descriptions of characters. The variants highlight the difference between physical descriptions (hand, mountain) and abstract concepts (mind, coastline) in the manuscript tradition.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript variants, Gallo-Greeks, Galatians, Asia Minor, Roman history, linguistic analysis, vernacular translation</keywords>

| | ...[custo]dy of that servant girl, and made her firm. | the servant girl, who knew of the deed, to make him steadfast. |
| p. 216. | The fatigued breast of the husband. | The anxious breast of the husband. <note>The variant is between "affaticato" (tired) and "affannato" (labored breathing or distressed).</note> |
| » » | He was kept safe in the bosom of his precious wife. | He was kept safe in the bosom of his compassionate wife. <note>The scribe likely confused "preziosa" (precious) with "pietosa" (pious/compassionate).</note> |
| » 218. | Thinking it a more honorable thing to follow her husband for the cost of fortune. | Thinking it a more honorable thing to follow her husband struck down by fortune. <note>A classic scribal error: "per costo" (for the cost) versus "percosso" (beaten/struck).</note> |
| » 219. | They fear the trouble of the sea and save themselves by light effort. | They fear the trouble of the sea, they set sail with light effort. <note>The variant "salvonsi" (save themselves) versus "solvonsi" (to loosen the moorings or set sail).</note> |
| » » | She was so armed with poetic knowledge. | She was so adorned with poetic knowledge. <note>The variant is "armata" (armed) vs "ornata" (adorned).</note> |
| » » | To not be useful for the delights of men. | To not be useful except for the delight of men. |
| » » | A bitter drink. | A drink for loving. <note>original: "amatorium poculum" (a love potion).</note> |
| » 224. | By which cruelty is fixed. | By which security is established. <note>The variant "crudeltà" (cruelty) versus "sicurtà" (security).</note> |
| » » | He not only attracted the hatred of the cruel king. | The hatred of the cruel king was not only distressed. <note>The variant "s'attirò" (attracted) vs "si attristò" (made sad/intensified).</note> |
| » 224. | By a secret testament. | By a signed testament. <note>The variant "segreto" (secret) vs "segnato" (signed/marked).</note> |
| » 225. | Because among the Egyptians it was highly in use, out of dishonor, to take mothers and daughters as wives. | For among the Egyptians it was excluded only because of dishonor to take mothers and daughters as wives. <note>The variant "in uso" (in use) vs "escluso" (excluded) completely reverses the meaning of the cultural observation.</note> |

| » » | He took heart against him. | He took up arms against him. <note>The variant is between "animo" (spirit/courage) and "armi" (weapons).</note> |

| » » | The most haughty treachery of the Emperor. | The treachery of the most haughty Emperor. |

### CHAPTER LXXIII.

# **Dripetrua, Daughter of Mithridates.**

We have read that Dripetrua was queen of Laodicea and the daughter of the great Mithridates <note>Mithridates VI of Pontus, known as one of Rome's most formidable enemies.</note>. Though she should be commended for the loyalty <note>Original: *fede*. In this context, it refers to filial piety and duty.</note> which we are bound to give our fathers (1), in my opinion, mother nature made her even more worthy of praise for a certain unheard-of work. For, if we are to give credit (2) to the books of the ancients, she (3), having been born with a double row of teeth <note>A rare dental condition recorded by ancient historians like Pliny the Elder as a physical marvel.</note>, was an admirable wonder <note>Original: *mostro*. In the medieval and Renaissance sense, this term refers to a "monstrum" or divine omen/marvel, rather than something inherently evil or frightening.</note> to all of Asia in her time. Although she suffered no impediment to eating from such an unusual quantity of teeth, it was nonetheless not without a striking ugliness; which, as I have already touched upon (4), she silenced with praiseworthy loyalty (5); because, having been defeated...

---

(1) Manuscript A. The Latin reads: *which although that loyalty made commendable, by which we are indebted to our parents.*
(2) *To lend credit*, according to the Florentine manuscript.
(3) *She [having been] born*, according to the same manuscript.
(4) Manuscripts A and the Florentine. *As I have already related*, according to the Cassinese manuscript and the printed edition. The Latin text reads: *As has already been discussed.*
(5) *With praiseworthy faith*, Manuscript A, *with laudable faith*, the Cassinese and the printed edition. *She made it silent*, according to the Florentine manuscript. Thus Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani (c. 1326–1411) was a noted humanist and friend of Petrarch who translated Boccaccio’s Latin works into the Italian vernacular.</note> rendered the text: *he suppressed the deformity... with praiseworthy loyalty*, where the vernacular translation is more effective than the original Latin.

<keywords>Dripetrua, Mithridates VI, Boccaccio, Donato Albanzani, Queen of Laodicea, physical anomalies, filial piety, textual criticism, Italian humanism</keywords>

...Mithridates her father [having been defeated] by Pompey the Great (1), refusing no labors nor dangers, she always followed him (2), showing by such faithful service that her defect <note>Referring to the double row of teeth mentioned on the previous page.</note> should be attributed to nature, and not to her father.

## CHAPTER LXXIV.

### **Sempronia of the Gracchi** (3).

Sempronia was the daughter of Tiberius Sempronius Gracchus, a most famous man in his time, whom he (4) fathered by Cornelia, who had been the daughter of the great Scipio Africanus; and she was later the wife of Scipio Aemilianus, a most famous man, who afterwards acquired the surname of his grandfather (5) for the destruction of Carthage; and she was the sister of Tiberius and Gaius Gracchus. And she did not fall away <term>dischiattò</term> <note>A term meaning to deviate from one's noble lineage or "stock" (schiatta).</note> from her ancestors in greatness and firmness of soul. And it is said that, after the death of her brothers, because of their discords (7), it happened that she was brought to trial by

---

(1) *Pompey the Great* <note>original: "Grande Pompeo"</note>, the Turin and Florentine manuscripts. *Great Pompey* <note>original: "Gran Pompeo"</note>, the printed edition.
(2) *Followed him* <note>original: "Il seguì"</note>, Florentine manuscript.
(3) The text: *Of Sempronia* <note>original Latin: "De Sempronia"</note>. Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani, the 14th-century translator of this work into Italian.</note> added: *of the Gracchi* to distinguish her from the Sempronia who follows in Chapter LXXVII.
(4) *Fathered him* <note>original: "Lui generò"</note>, Florentine manuscript.
(5) *Grandfather* <note>original: "Avo"</note>, the Turin manuscript.
(6) *And did not degenerate* <note>original: "E non degenerò"</note>, the Florentine manuscript. Just as "to degenerate" comes from *genus* <note>original Latin: "non degenerans"</note>, so *dischiattare* comes from *schiatta* (stock), a noun still living, and better than *lignaggio* (lineage) or *stirpe* (ancestry), not to mention *razza* (race), which is more proper for animals than for men. *Dischiattare* is now in disuse. However, it is very beautiful, especially in the phrase *to not fall away from one's ancestors in greatness of soul*.
(7) Not because of her own, but because of the popular discords, following Valerius Maximus <note>A 1st-century AD Roman author of "Memorable Deeds and Sayings," a major source for Boccaccio.</note>, from whom this Chapter is taken, who says that

<meta>This page continues Chapter LXXIV regarding Sempronia, sister of the Gracchi. a tribune of the people in the council of the people (1), and not without great terror of mind (2). And in that place, while the multitude encouraged her, and the entire office of the tribunes urged her to kiss Equitius of Fermo, from the Marches, as her nephew and the son of Tiberius Gracchus her brother, she was being forced to accept him as a member of the Sempronii family. She certainly—although she was (3) in a place where even princes were accustomed to tremble, and though she was urged on one side and the other by the discordant noises (4) of the ignorant multitude, and was threatened on the other side with a harsh face (5) by the authority of the Tribunes—the constancy of that woman did not bend in any way. Rather, remembering that her brother Tiberius had fathered only three sons, one of whom had died young while in military service in Sardin—

---

*...the peace of the citizenry was disturbed by a storm of discord and betrayal.* (Valerius Maximus, page 250, Romagnoli edition).

(1) The text: <note>original: "ut a tribuno plebis coram populo in iudicium traheretur"</note> ("that she might be dragged into judgment by a tribune of the plebs before the people"). Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani, the 14th-century translator of this work.</note> did not distinguish "plebs" <term>plebe</term> <note>The lower class of Roman citizens.</note> from "populace" <term>popolo</term>, because in his own time they were in practice less distinct. But in the time of the Romans, and in ours, it was and is a different matter entirely. And it will always be so until the *plebeians stop being so "populous."*

(2) It is noteworthy that the "greatest consternation of mind" <note>original: "maxima consternatio mentis"</note> of the original Latin text was rendered by Albanzani as *great terror of mind*. "Consternation" belongs to the Golden Age of Latin literature, and the root word *sterno* <note>meaning to spread out or strike down</note> makes the mind think of being flattened or overthrown. But "consternation" is more than just being cast down. Why is it then that in Dictionaries the word *costernazione* is not found with examples from the <term>trecentisti</term>? <note>Authors of the 1300s, considered the masters of the Italian language.</note>

(3) *Although she was in that place*, according to the Florentine manuscript.

(4) *Discordant noises* is the best reading from Manuscript A and the Florentine manuscript (there is a gap in Manuscript B) and it corresponds to the Latin text: <note>original: "etsi . . . . hinc inde dissonis clamoribus imperitae multitudinis ageretur"</note> ("although... she was driven here and there by the discordant cries of the ignorant multitude"). There is a grave error in the printed edition: *she was stimulated on one side and the other by forgetful noise.* The word *by* <note>original: "da"</note> before *discordant* is necessary for the sense.

(5) Correct the printed edition in the same way: *and that with a harsh face she was threatened by the authority of the tribunes.*

[Sardi]nia; the other had died as a child in Rome shortly before his father perished (1), and the third child, shortly after the death of his father, had died while with his nurse, having likewise been born posthumously <term>postumo</term> <note>Born after the death of the father.</note> (2). (6) *Signoria* <term>authority</term> is the *imperium* of the text, which here means "commandment." In the Florentine manuscript: *induced by any judgment*, which is even less appropriate than *signoria*.

(7) *To do what she could* is from the Turin and Cassinese manuscripts, and is to be preferred over *to do what seemed right* from the Florentine manuscript; but it does not capture Boccaccio's thought: <note>original: "ad id agendum quod iubebatur"</note> ("to do that which was commanded").

(8) *Having failed* is the excellent reading of the Turin and Florentine manuscripts. The Cassinese manuscript, in the phrase *having been weary*, kept a distant trace of *failed*, which the editor replaced with *having been made in vain.*

(9) The tangled sentence of the text: <note>original: "quae tam animo se Equitio data repulsa, et insani hominis proteruia frustrata, et a tri-"</note> ("which rejection having been given to Equitius so courageously, and the impudence of the insane man frustrated, and by the tri[bunes]...")

->the perseverance of that woman’s noble spirit was praised.<- And perhaps there will be some who say that, although Sempronia was worthy because of her past (1), she should nonetheless not be placed among famous women on account of this strength, because women, in every purpose, are of an obstinate and stubborn opinion. But I, although I do not deny this, nonetheless think they are to be praised if they align themselves with the truth, in which Sempronia certainly remained firm. There are still others who claim she was of such an indomitable mind that, if she had been able (2), nothing would have been done against her judgment that she would have left without vengeance. And for this reason, it was thought that she consented to the death of Scipio her husband, because, after he destroyed Numantia <note>An ancient settlement in Spain; Scipio Aemilianus's victory there in 133 BCE was a major Roman triumph.</note>, when asked if he thought Tiberius Gracchus had been justly killed, he had no respect for the family tie and praised the harsh death of that discordant man <note>Tiberius Gracchus was Sempronia's brother; Scipio's public approval of his brother-in-law's murder caused a deep family rift.</note>.

***

*the matter having been investigated more accurately, and the perseverance of the woman’s noble spirit having been praised* <note>original: "bunis [sic] accuratius exquisito negotio cognita, et generosi animi mulieris perseuerantia laudata est"</note>, has given rise to the convolutions of the translation, which are found in all the manuscripts <term>codici</term>, and which cannot be removed without resorting to arbitrary changes.
(1) *Predecessors*, according to the Florentine manuscript.
(2) In the Turin and Cassino manuscripts always *ella* [she], and in the Florentine always *lei* [her]. I cannot persuade myself that the good Tuscan writers—even at the obvious expense of harmony, and I am tempted to say the melody, of which they were most studious—always wanted to wander in those labyrinths of *lei*, *ella*, or *elle* [various forms of "she"], which are encountered in the manuscripts by the hand of the scribes <term>menanti</term> <note>Professional copyists who transcribed texts before the invention of the printing press.</note>. I believe that they sometimes made a choice, as is evident in the sentence that follows: *And for this reason it was thought that she consented* [la consentisse], from the Florentine manuscript; one can swear this belongs to Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani, the 14th-century scholar and friend of Petrarch who translated Boccaccio’s work from Latin into the Italian vernacular.</note>, in place of the usual *lei* of that manuscript, and the *ella* of all the others.

### CHAPTER LXXV.

## Claudia Quinta, a Roman.

Claudia Quinta was a Roman woman. It is not entirely clear from what lineage she was born; but nevertheless, through a certain marvelous daring, she acquired perpetual fame <note>original: "perpetua fama"; some manuscripts use the term "perpetuale"</note>. This woman, because she wore constant and varied ornaments of a very delicate nature, and walked with her face too carefully groomed <note>In ancient Rome, excessive use of cosmetics and jewelry was often seen as a sign of "dishonesty" or a lack of chastity</note>, was considered by women of greater gravity to be not only lacking in virtue, but even unchaste <note>original: "non solamente non onesta, ma disonesta"</note>. 

And, while Marcus Cornelius and Publius Sempronius were consuls <note>This refers to the year 204 BCE</note>—that is, in the fifteenth year of the Second Punic War <note>original: "seconda guerra degli Africani"</note>—it happened that the Mother of the Gods <note>The goddess Cybele, whose cult was being moved to Rome to ensure victory over Carthage</note> was brought from Pessinus to Rome by way of the River Tiber. And to receive her from the ship, according to the response of the oracle, Scipio Nasica was judged to be the "best man" by the entire Senate. Having gone to that place with all the women, when the ship was nearby, it happened that as the sailors tried to reach the nearest shore, the ship carrying the statue ran aground <note>original: "andata in secco"</note>, and could not be—

***

(1) *Perpetual*, according to the Turin and Florentine manuscripts. 
(2) *Not only unchaste but most unchaste.* The Florentine manuscript. 
(3) *In the fifteenth*, the Cassinese manuscript and the printed edition. 
(4) *To the concordant reading of the Turin and Florentine manuscripts, I have deemed it necessary to add "was" [fu]. The Cassinese manuscript, according to the printed edition, says: "having gone to that place with all the women up to the nearest ship."* 
(5) *The Turin manuscripts say "to the nearby shore"; the Florentine "to the neighboring shore"; the Cassinese and the printed edition "near the shore."* 
(6) *This is better than "arrived aground" in the Cassinese. Manuscript A uses "Secca."*

...adorned with the marvelous splendor of virtue <note>Note 1: The original text, confirmed by other manuscripts, reads "Decorated with the distinguished splendor of chastity" (original Latin: *Decorata insigni pudicitiae splendore*), rather than "marvelous honor of virtue."</note>. And although the matter happened <note>Note 2: Codex A and the Florentine manuscript use the word "came to pass"; others use "happened."</note> according to the desire <note>Note 3: The Florentine manuscript uses a more poetic form of "desire."</note> of Claudia, it is not that I <note>Note 4: The Turin and Florentine manuscripts use different spellings for "that I." Such scribal habits often lead to errors. For example, in the famous scene of Francesca in Dante’s *Divine Comedy*, the phrase "Move the voice" is thought by some to actually mean "I move the voice."</note> judge it to be of sound mind, even though she was innocent, to dare such things. Because wanting to do what is beyond nature, so that one may show oneself to be without fault, is rather to tempt God than to purge the blame of the imposed sin. But we must live holily, act holily <note>Note 5: The phrase "acting holily" is missing from the printed edition but is present in the original Latin text: "We must act holily, we must live holily" (*Sancte nobis agendum est, sancte vivendum*).</note>, and if we are not esteemed as good <note>Note 6: This follows the Turin and Florentine manuscripts. The printed edition’s reading, "if we are blamed," has no known basis in the earlier texts.</note>, God will not let it pass without our benefit. And certainly He wills this <note>Note 7: The printed edition reads "certainly He wills so that He may confirm our patience," but it leaves out the actual thing being willed.</note> to confirm our patience <note>Note 8: "My patience" in Codex A and "his patience" in Codex B are considered scribal errors.</note>, and so that pride may be taken away, and virtue practiced; and so that we may rejoice within ourselves, knowing others to be unworthy. It is enough for us, indeed it is a very great thing <note>Note 9: This is a Latin-style phrasing no longer in common use—"it is enough for us, or rather, very great" (original Latin: *satis nobis multum est, imo per maximum*)—but it has a surprising brevity and effectiveness.</note> if we live well, being known...

<summary>The author reflects on Claudia Quinta's miracle, arguing that while she was innocent, seeking supernatural signs to prove one's virtue is a dangerous "tempting of God" compared to simply living a holy life.</summary>

### CHAPTER LXXVI.

## **Hypsicratea, Queen of Pontus.**

Hypsicratea, although her lineage is not known (3), was nonetheless the wife of the great Mithridates and a great queen of Pontus <note>Pontus was an ancient kingdom on the southern coast of the Black Sea, in modern-day Turkey.</note>. She possessed marvelous beauty and an invincible love for her husband (4); and in this she is so (5) worthy of praise that, because of it, she earned perpetual splendor for her name. While Mithridates was in a long and costly war with the Romans—even though he, according to barbarian custom, had other wives and concubines (6)—she, burning with the greatest love, whether he went into battle or prepared his fleet (7), was always his most faithful and...

->that admirable treatise on philology <term>philology: the study of language in historical sources</term> and aesthetics which in Tommaseo’s Dictionary is found under the verb *to Be*.<-

(1) <note>original: "E non secondo il conoscere di Dio, chè troppo ci vorrebbe"</note> "And not according to the knowledge of God, for that would be too much," from the Florentine manuscript.
(2) From the Turin manuscripts. The Cassinese manuscript and the printed edition read "And if he thinks ill," while the Florentine reads "And if they think ill."
(3) <note>original: "Benchè non sappia"</note> "Although it is not known," from the Cassinese manuscript and the printed edition.
(4) The Florentine manuscript reads "her husband" <note>original: "suo marito"</note>; the others and the printed edition follow the main text.
(5) From the Cassinese and Florentine manuscripts. This is a more intensive phrasing than the "so much" found in the Turin manuscripts.
(6) "Other wife and friends" in the Turin manuscripts; "other wives and concubines" <note>original Latin: "coniuges aliae et concubinae"</note> in the original Latin text.
(7) The Turin manuscripts use only "prepared," which, following "went into battle," implies the preparation of military forces.

...inseparable (1) companion. And because for such a great undertaking womanly dress did not seem appropriate, and it was indecent (4) for a woman to accompany a king so suited to battle, she first cut her blonde hair—in which women take such great pride—so that she might appear as a man. She endured not only covering her beautiful face and hair (5) with a helmet, but also staining it with sweat, dust, and the rust of armor. of matrimonial sweetness, and how that friendship was without fault! With what strengths, and how holy (1) was the soul of that woman fortified! Seeing this state of affairs, and finding him <note>referring to his son Pharnaces</note> relentless (1), and thinking this to be the final ruin of his fortunes, he poisoned Hypsicratea along with all his wives, friends, and daughters. And although she had given so much assistance to his life, exhausting herself <gloss>she exhausted herself</gloss> in labor, he killed her so that she would not live after him. And certainly, Mithridates' ungrateful act could not diminish the deserved glory of Hypsicratea. Her body, which was mortal, was dispatched by death (2) before its time by poison. But her name has come down to us and will live perpetually (3) in glorious fame through the testimony of the venerable writings, and cannot (4) be overcome by the future passage of time.

according to the testimony of the ancients, she was, among Roman women, famous both for her lineage (1) and for her beauty. And she was quite fortunate in her husbands and children; though I do not recall their names, let us come to this, which is the thing for which she can be praised: and those things for which her renown (2) was clear should be placed in the first rank. Thus, she was of such a ready and agile (3) wit that she immediately understood and even imitated, following (4) through, everything she saw anyone say or do. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Sempronia’s character. Having previously described her intellectual talents, the author now investigates the "roots" of her moral decline, shifting focus from her innate nature to the failures of her upbringing and the loss of traditional feminine modesty.</meta>

<sig>17</sig>

Of which evil (1), which we see so strongly in some, judge the root as you will (2). But I will not condemn nature. Although her force is great at the beginning of things, she can be so bent that, with little effort, you can almost guide (3) a newborn thing as you wish. And thus, by neglecting it, it always turns for the worse (4). And I certainly think that, in the childhood of girls, the indulgence of fathers often spoils their character (5); who (6), following their own will and leaning (7) toward lewdness, feminine trepidation (8) little by little draws near, and immediately audacity grows, multiplied by some foolish opinion, by which they say that whatever pleases is decent. And once they have gone astray once, we labor in vain to ensure that the honor of girls is not defiled, and that the shame of the brow <note>A poetic reference to "blushing" or the outward sign of modesty.</note> is not cast down (9), and to pull back those who are failing (10). (2) *And do not judge the roots as you will* is the reading in the printed edition.
(3) *Almost to lead* in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.
(4) The beginning of things. <note>original Latin: "Quod natum est, et si sit neglectum, semper vergit in peius," meaning "That which is born, if it is neglected, always turns for the worse."</note>
(5) *They spoil* in all the manuscripts. And then: "fathers, by forgiving, spoil [them]."
(6) This refers to the girls.
(7) The text is clearer. <note>original Latin: "Quibus licentiose declinantibus," meaning "To those who are declining licentiously."</note> *Bending* in the Turin manuscripts.
(8) This is the reading in all manuscripts, except that in the Cassinese it is *tepidity* and in the Florentine *tepidity*. But the phrase <note>original Latin: "foeminea cedit trepiditas," meaning "feminine trepidation gives way."</note> requires a verb opposite to *draws near*. <note>The translator is noting a contradiction: the Italian says "trepidation draws near," but the original Latin says "trepidation/shame leaves."</note> Father Tosti, borrowing from <term>Betussi: a 16th-century translator of Boccaccio</term>, uses *gives place*. Perhaps <term>Albanzani: the 14th-century scholar Donato Albanzani</term> translated it as *moves away*.
(9) *That it not be placed down*, in the Florentine manuscript.
(10) *Fallen* in that manuscript. But let *failing* <note>original Italian: "caduche"</note> remain, as it corresponds to <note>original Latin: "labentes," meaning "sliding" or "failing."</note> in the text. These are those in danger of falling, not those who have already fallen.

...do women meet the lust of men halfway, but they even incite them (1). Greed for money is a mortal evil in any woman, and the clearest testimony of a corrupted heart (4). ### CHAPTER LXXVIII.

## **Of the wives of the Flemish and the Germans <note>original: "De coniugibus Cymbrorum" — the Cimbrians were an ancient Germanic tribe.</note>.**

The great multitude of the wives of the Cimbrians, who were defeated by Gaius Marius in a bitter battle, was worthy of praise for their sacred and constant commitment to virtue; indeed, they should be especially magnified because the more of them there were, the higher they should be raised with greater honors. And this is because we have very often read of a few women having achieved the observance of chastity, but we have not read—or have very rarely heard—that many have achieved it together. Therefore, while the deeds of the Romans were in their prime <note>The Italian translator notes that "in flower" or "in their prime" is a better reading than "in happiness" found in other manuscripts.</note>...

---

<margin>1. The three manuscripts known to me agree on "standard of women" for the "matronly robe," but if the "woman's garment" of the printed version is from the Cassinese manuscript, it is undoubtedly to be preferred, because in antiquity the "stola" was also the matronly dress. Bangio uses "garment of honesty."</margin>
<margin>2. The "well-known Sempronia" of the text is more than simply "known."</margin>
<margin>3. From the Florentine manuscript; "become glorious" is used in the others and the printed edition.</margin>
<margin>4. "Of the spouses of the Cimbrians" in the original Latin text.</margin>
<margin>5. From the Turin and Florentine manuscripts, or "because the more they were" following the Cassinese manuscript and the printed edition.</margin>
<margin>6. "We have read and heard" in the printed edition; but "read" does not belong here, as it was already mentioned. The Latin text says "we have never or very rarely heard."</margin>
<margin>7. From the Cassinese manuscript and the printed edition alone, which is much better than "the deeds of the Romans being in happiness" found in other manuscripts.</margin>

...followed the flight of their husbands; but they drew up (1) the wagons, of which they had a great number (2), into the form of a barricade <note>The Italian "steccato" suggests a defensive stockade or enclosure made of wagons, a common tactic of the Germanic tribes known as a "wagon-fort."</note>, with a foolish but courageous purpose to defend, for as long as they possibly could (3), their liberty and chastity, using charred stakes, stones, and swords. But when the people of Marius arrived in battle formation (4), the women, putting up no long resistance, realized they were striving in vain. Because of this, they asked if they could come to an agreement with the general. And when they could not (5), they had resolved in their minds at least by what means they might save their liberty and their honor, should they lose their husbands in battle, and should they lose the ancestral homes (6) of their forefathers and all their wealth. And therefore, with one mind, they asked—not for peace for their fleeing husbands (7), nor to return to their homeland, nor that their losses be repaid in coin—but that they all be taken to Rome and placed as nuns <note>The original Italian "monache" (nuns) is an anachronism used by the translator to explain the status of the Vestal Virgins to a contemporary audience.</note> with the Vestal Virgins. (1) Regarding the syntax, I have followed the <term>Cassinese manuscript</term>. MS B reads *led*. The printed edition reads *they led*. The other two read *brought together*.
(2) This reading is from the <term>Florentine manuscript</term>. The others read *multitude*.

(7) That manuscript reads *fleeing husbands*.
(8) The use of *gli* for *loro* [them] is found in the Turin manuscripts.
(9) The printed edition reads: *And not a most honorable testimony of a pure mind*. The Latin text reads: <term>Which, since it seemed most honorable, and a testimony of a sincere mind</term> <note>original Latin: "Quod cum honestissimum visum foret, et syncerae mentis testimonium"</note>.

-> — 263 — <-

...fury (1) in the obstinate perseverance of their desire, they sought a cruel deed. And first, having dashed (2) their small children (3) to the ground, and having killed them (4) to free them from slavery in the only way they could, the following night they hanged themselves with ropes (5) and horses' bridles within the barricade they had made, so that they would not be dragged away to the shame of their chastity and the contempt of the victors (6); they left the enemies no other prey of themselves but their hanged bodies. Other women would have gone humbly to meet the victors, with human reason broken (7), with their hair loose, with their hands raised (8), filling everything with prayers and weeping; and, what would have been a more wicked thing, some would have...

---

(1) Of the three <term>manuscripts</term> <note>The original Italian "codici" refers to handwritten books or codices.</note> seen by me. *Kindled with fury in the obstinate perseverance of their vow*, the printed edition agrees with the Cassinese manuscript and the Latin text: <note>original: "succensae furore in obstinatam voti sui perseverantiam"</note> *incensed with fury for the obstinate perseverance of their vow*.
(2) *Beaten*, according to the Florentine manuscript and the printed edition: *having dashed to the ground the small children*.
(3) *Their children*, in manuscript A and the Florentine manuscript.
(4) *And having slain them*, in the same manuscript.
(5) *Ropes* in the Florentine manuscript and manuscript A. The Latin text reads: <note>original: "Laqueis, lorisque" (with nooses and leather straps)</note>. *Halters* in the Cassinese manuscript and manuscript B.
(6) *And in contempt of the victorious enemies* in the printed edition, where the word "enemies" is clearly redundant.
(7) The Latin phrase <note>original: "fractis hominum viribus"</note> *with the strength of men broken* is rendered as *having beaten down the strength of men*.
(8) *And not "bound"* [tied], which is a serious error in the printing. The original text reads: <note>original: "Tensis manibus" (with hands outstretched)</note> *with hands raised*. Mr. Attilio Hortis, in that treasure of erudition that is his *Studies on the Latin Works of Boccaccio* (Trieste, 1879, large quarto, pp. XX and 956), cites several passages from the <term>vernacular translation</term> <note>The term "volgarizzamento" refers to the 14th-century practice of translating Latin classics into the common Italian tongue, in this case by Donato Albanzani.</note> of *On Famous Women*, taking them from the 1836 Neapolitan edition. On pages 82 and 83, he begins from "Other women" and continues to the end. It would be unfair to require Mr. Hortis to compare the translation with the original text every time. However, it should be noted here how certain serious errors, passing from one transcription to another, are perpetuated, as must have happened with "bound hands" in place of "raised hands."

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Cimbrian women’s mass suicide following their defeat by the Romans. -> — 264 — <-

...demanded with flatteries and embraces, if they could have preserved their belongings and returned to their homeland; not remembering any womanly virtue, they would have allowed themselves to be dragged away by anyone like beasts (1). But those Cimbrian women (2), more virtuous (3) with a steadfast heart, preserved spirits worthy of a better fortune (4); and they did not allow the glory of their people to be defiled with any shame of their honor (5). And, obstinately fleeing slavery and shame with the noose, they showed that their men were defeated not by force, but by a failure of fortune; and for their chastity they acquired a very long life, losing only those few years which they might have survived if they had not hanged themselves (6). And they left to those who were to follow a reason to marvel (7), that so great a multitude of women, not by conspiracy nor by public deliberation, in the space (8) of a single night, agreed (9) to a single resolve of death...

---

(1) The text reads: <note>original: "A se permisissent more pecudum trahi"</note> (They allowed themselves to be dragged in the manner of beasts).
(2) Mr. Hortis has substituted *Cimbrian women* for the *Flemish women* found in Albanzani's translation. <note>Donato Albanzani was a 14th-century translator; "Fiamminghe" (Flemish) was a common medieval mistransliteration or misunderstanding of "Cimbri."</note>
(3) From the Turin and Florentine <term>manuscripts</term> <note>The original Italian "codici" refers to handwritten books.</note>; this is missing from the printed edition.
(4) <note>original: "Ast Cimbrae, constanti pectore melioris fortunae servare animos"</note> (But the Cimbrian women, with a steadfast heart, preserved spirits [worthy] of a better fortune). It seems to me that we must understand the word "worthy" here.
(5) The Latin text has <note>original: "nec ulla passae sunt ignominia maiestatis gentis suae gloriam foedare"</note> (nor did they suffer any ignominy to defile the glory of the majesty of their race), with which Manuscript A, the Cassinese manuscript, and the printed edition agree. But, along with Mr. Hortis, I do not consider it the correct reading, since "the glory of their majesty" is entirely contained within the "defiling of glory," and the "ignominy" within the "shame of her honor."
(6) *Appiccate* in the Florentine and Cassinese manuscripts, *impiccate* in the Turin manuscripts. <note>Both terms mean "hanged," though "appiccate" is a more archaic or regional variant.</note>
(7) The Florentine manuscript: *And they left to those who were to succeed them a reason to marvel that such a number...*
(8) The three manuscripts known to me read *in space*, as does the printed edition.
(9) *A multitude... agreed*. Ancient writers, much more than we do today, would match a singular collective noun with a plural verb.

## CHAPTER LXXIX.

### Julia, daughter of Gaius Julius Caesar.

Julia was perhaps the most famous woman in the entire world, both for her lineage and for her husband; but she was even more famous for her (1) most holy love, and for her sudden death. She was the only daughter of Julius Caesar <note>Gaius Julius Caesar (100–44 BCE), the Roman general and dictator who played a critical role in the transformation of the Roman Republic into the Roman Empire.</note>, born of Cornelia his wife, the daughter of Cinna <note>Lucius Cornelius Cinna was a four-time consul and a leader of the Populares faction in Rome.</note>, who had been consul four times. This Julius had his origins, through many kings and other successors, from Aeneas, the glorious (2) leader of the Trojans (3) on his father's side, and on his mother's side from Ancus Marcius, who had previously been king of the Romans <note>The Julii claimed divine descent from Venus through Aeneas, while the Marcii claimed descent from Rome's fourth king.</note>. And he was greatly honored with the glories of battles, triumphs, and a perpetual dictatorship. Later, she was the wife of Pompey the Great <note>Gnaeus Pompeius Magnus (106–48 BCE), a leading Roman general and statesman who was initially Caesar's ally and son-in-law before becoming his primary political and military rival.</note>, a most famous man among the Romans at that time. He, for a long space of time, wearied not only the earth but the heavens, conquering kings, casting them down (4) and raising them up anew, subjugating nations, pursuing pirates, winning the goodwill of the people of Rome, and acquiring favor with the kings of the entire world. This man that famous woman loved—though she was but a young girl and he already an old man—so ardently that, because of it, she—

---

(1) *For the [per lo]* is found in <term>codex</term> <note>The Italian "codice" refers to a handwritten manuscript book.</note> A, the Cassinese manuscript, and the printed edition. *For the [per il]* is in the Florentine <term>manuscript</term>, which is from the 15th century, and *by the most holy [pel santissimo]* is in <term>codex</term> B.
(2) *Glorious leader [glorioso duca]* is found only in the Florentine <term>manuscript</term>, and corresponds to the <note>original: "inclyto duce"</note> (illustrious leader) of the original Latin text.
(3) The Florentine <term>manuscript</term> reads *Trogliani* <note>A misspelling of "Troiani" (Trojans).</note>.
(4) *Casting them down [ponendoli]* is only in the Florentine <term>manuscript</term>; the others use *putting them [mettendoli]*; *down [giuso]* appears in all versions this time.

<keywords>Julia, Gaius Julius Caesar, Pompey the Great, Aeneas, Ancus Marcius, Roman lineage, marital devotion, Trojan ancestry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Julia, daughter of Julius Caesar and wife of Pompey the Great. The previous page established her noble lineage and her marriage, which was intended to cement the political alliance between Caesar and Pompey. This section describes the tragic and accidental circumstances of her death.</meta>

...she met her death before her time. For, while Pompey was performing a sacrifice at the assembly of the aediles <note>The aediles were Roman officials responsible for public maintenance and the organization of festivals. The "assembly" (comitia) refers to the gathering where these officials were elected.</note> <margin>This phrase is missing from the printed edition but present in the manuscripts, corresponding to the Latin *comitiis aedilitiis*.</margin>, a beast was slain <margin>The manuscripts and print use *ammazzata* (slain), though the Florentine manuscript uses *uccisa* (killed).</margin> which he was holding. Because of the blow, the animal thrashed <margin>The Florentine manuscript uses *scassandosi*, perhaps derived from the Latin *quassare*, meaning to shake violently or shatter.</margin> in various directions, and Pompey was splattered with much of its blood. Because of this, he stripped off his garments and sent <margin>The printed edition uses "stripping and sending" rather than "having stripped and sent."</margin> them home. It happened that the servant who was carrying them encountered Julia first. As soon as she saw her husband's bloodstained clothes <margin>According to the Turin and Florentine manuscripts. ## Porcia, daughter of Cato <margin>In the manuscripts known to me I always find "Cato" <note>The translator is noting that they are retaining the name "Cato" as found in the source manuscripts, rather than modernizing or changing it.</note>, and so I leave it.</margin> (1) of Utica.

## CHAPTER LXXXIII.

### **Sulpicia, wife of Lentulus.**

Sulpicia, the wife of Lentulus Cruscellio, found perpetual fame for herself through a nearly perfect devotion. For Lentulus was proscribed by the Triumvirs <note>The Triumvirs were Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus, who seized power in 43 BCE and published lists of enemies to be executed or exiled.</note> during the same period of adversity mentioned above. Having escaped through flight to Sicily, he remained in that place in exile and in poverty. When Sulpicia was made certain of the matter <note>Following the three manuscripts known to me, adhering to the Latin: "Sulpicia was made more certain of this matter" <note>original Latin: "facta eius rei Sulpitia certior"</note>. She resolved to want to bear it (meaning the exile, even though the last noun mentioned was "the matter") together with her husband. The reading in the printed edition—"she resolved to wish to suffer together with her husband"—is less accurate.</note>, thinking that it was not a proper thing for wives to receive joyful honors with their husbands and stand by them <note>And not "to stand among those in prosperous fortune," as found in the printed edition.</note> in prosperous times, yet reject adversity and flee from them if the need arises. But Sulpicia could not easily obtain permission to go to her husband, because her mother, Julia, kept watch over her with the greatest diligence to ensure she did not follow her husband into exile <note>Between the mentions of the husband in manuscript B and the Monte Cassino manuscript there is a gap, which was filled in the printed edition as: "...to the husband with great diligence was..."</note>. ### CHAPTER LXXXIV.

**Cornificia the poet.**

I do not recall having found whether Cornificia was Roman, or from another country (1); but, by the testimony of the ancient writers, she was most worthy of being remembered. And, during the reign of the Emperor Octavian <note>Augustus Caesar, who ruled from 27 BC to 14 AD.</note>, she was so adorned (2) with the knowledge of poetry that she did not seem to have been nurtured in Italy, but in Greece. She was famous in equal glory with Cornificius, a most famous poet of his time, who was her brother. And not content only with such famous knowledge, as I believe, being urged by the sacred Muses, she very often placed her practiced hands to the pen to write Heliconian verses <note>Verses inspired by Mount Helicon, the mythical home of the Muses.</note>, having set aside the <term>distaff <note>original: "rocca." The tool used for spinning wool, symbolizing traditional domestic female labor.</note></term>; and she wrote very notable verses (3), which were highly esteemed in the time of Saint Jerome. And she was an honor to women, because she looked down upon the traditional "art of females" and applied her intellect most extensively to the studies of the greatest poets (4). Let those women feel ashamed who are of a low spirit, and who distrust (5) themselves—

---

(1) <gloss>Reading from the Florentine manuscript. The others and the printed edition: *or if she was a woman from another country.*</gloss>
(2) <gloss>And not "armed" [*armata*] as in the printed edition. The original Latin says: *she shone with poetic doctrine* <note>original: "Poetico effulsit dogmate"</note>.</gloss>
(3) <gloss>Reading from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition, which is preferred over *she wrote many of the notable verses* found in the Florentine manuscript. The original Latin: *She wrote many and remarkable epigrams* <note>original: "Plurima ac insignia scripsit epigrammata"</note>.</gloss>
(4) <gloss>Reading from the three manuscripts known to me. The printed edition reads: *she applied her intellect to the studies of the greatest poets.*</gloss>
(5) <gloss>From the Turin manuscripts. The Florentine manuscript reads: *distrust themselves.*</gloss>

# CHAPTER LXXXV.

## **Mariamne, Queen of the Jews.**

Mariamne was a Hebrew by nation, born to Aristobulus, King of the Jews, and Queen Alexandra, daughter of King Hyrcanus (4). She was of such great and unusual beauty by reputation that she was not only believed to surpass other women in beauty, but was thought to be a celestial image rather than a mortal woman (5). But finally, as things went from bad to worse, according to what some say, it happened that Mariamne was accused before Herod by one of his servants (2), who had been corrupted (3) <note>The Italian *contaminato* here carries the sense of being bribed or "suborned" to give false testimony.</note> by Cypros, Herod's mother, and by Salome, his sister, to whom Mariamne was extremely burdensome. And the accusation was that Mariamne had tried (4) to corrupt that servant (5) so that he would give Herod a love potion (6) which she had prepared. And, according to some others, [she was accused] because she, of her own accord and not by her mother's doing, at the time we have mentioned, had sent her most beautiful portrait to Antony <note>Mark Antony, the Roman Triumvir.</note>, to draw him into a desire for her and into hatred against Herod, since she had conceived a hatred for Herod herself (7). Since Herod believed these things (and Mariamne's own ill-will made him inclined to believe them), irate and inflamed with a painful fury, he complained to his friends in a—

---

(1) *Struggle* <note>original Italian: "fatica"</note> is found in three manuscripts known to me. It is better than the word *difficulty* <note>original: "difficoltà"</note> found in the printed edition.
(2) *Servant* <note>original: "famiglio"</note> in Manuscript A; *page* or *young man* <note>original: "donzello"</note> in Manuscript B and the printed edition; *young servant* <note>original: "dongiello"</note> in the Florentine manuscript.
(3) *Corrupted* or also *suborned*, which renders the classical Latin *subornato ab eiusdem pincerna* <note>original Latin: "suborned by his cupbearer"</note> from the source text.
(4) From the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition. *Tried* or *forced herself* <note>original: "forzata"</note> in the Florentine manuscript.
(5) *Servant* <note>original: "famiglio"</note> here is used in all the manuscripts and the printed edition.
(6) A slip of the pen results in *a bitter drink* <note>original: "una bevanda amara"</note> in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition. However, the true reading is that of the Florentine manuscript, *a love potion* <note>original: "bevanda da amare"</note>, which corresponds to the Latin source text: *ut illi amatorium poculum propinaret* <note>original Latin: "so that she might offer him a love potion"</note>.
(7) A good reading from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition, confirmed by the source text: *effigiem.... post conceptum in Herodem odium, ad Antonium ut illum in sui desiderium provocaret et in Herodis odium transmisisset* <note>original Latin: "the portrait... after conceiving a hatred toward Herod, [she] had sent to Antony so that she might provoke him into a desire for her and transfer him into a hatred for Herod"</note>. This confirms the error in the reading of the Florentine manuscript, which claimed *she had sent her most beautiful portrait to Antony to draw him to her delight, since she had taken a hatred against Herod.*

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Mariamne’s trial and execution. Following Herod’s accusations on the previous page, the focus shifts to the judgment passed against her and her stoic reaction to her impending death, contrasted with the betrayal of her mother, Alexandra.</meta>

<page-num>285</page-num>

...long speech. And because of this, encouraged by those people—and by Alexandra, Mariamne’s mother, in order to regain his favor—he was led to command that she be put to death (1), condemned to capital punishment as one who sought the death of the royal majesty. She certainly stirred up such a noble spirit (2) in herself that, despising (3) death and preserving the full (4) beauty of her countenance (5), not yielding in any way in the manner of a woman (6), she listened in silence to her mother rebuking her, and with a dry face (7) <note>i.e., without tears</note> looked at the others who were weeping; and without any fear, as if going to a most joyful triumph, with a bold countenance (8), and making no plea for her safety, she went to her death, receiving it from the <term>executioner<note>original Italian: "manigoldo", a term for a hangman or public executioner</note></term> as something she desired (9). Because of this steadfast composure (10), not only was the hatred dampened (11)...

---

(1) The use of the article for the pronoun is found only in the Florentine manuscript. It is worth noting that *lei* <note>her</note> is almost always found there, whereas in the others it is *ella* <note>she</note>.
(2) This is how Albanzani rendered the phrase from the Latin source: *tantum in se excitavit animi* <note>original Latin: "she stirred up so much spirit in herself"</note>.
(3) *Despising* <note>original: "dispregiando"</note> is found in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.
(4) *On the ground* <note>original: "in terra"</note> in the Florentine manuscript is an error [for *intiera*, meaning "entire" or "undiminished"].
(5) The phrase *of her countenance*, which corresponds to the Latin *vultus sui servato decore* <note>original Latin: "the beauty of her face being preserved"</note>, is missing from the printed edition. The Turin manuscripts say *of her face*.
(6) The Turin manuscripts have *in no part*, while the Florentine has *not in any part*. The printed edition says *in any thing*, while the original Latin text has *nulla ex parte* <note>original Latin: "in no part"</note>.
(7) *Dry* <note>original: "sciutta"</note> in the Florentine manuscript, and *sutta*, which is a <term>Lombardism<note>A linguistic feature or dialect from the Lombardy region of Italy</note></term>, in manuscript A.
(8) From the Florentine manuscript; it renders the Latin *aspectu alacri* <note>original Latin: "with a brisk or eager appearance"</note> better than the *happy face* found in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.
(9) The Florentine manuscript, which always uses *desio* [desire], here uses *desiata* [desired]. *Desiderata* is found in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.
(10) *Steadfastness* <note>original: "sicurtà"</note>, which is in the manuscripts known to me, corresponds to the Latin *qua immota securitate* <note>original Latin: "with which unshaken security or composure"</note>. The printed edition should be corrected, as it reads *firm cruelty*, which is out of place here.
(11) *Attracted* <note>original: "attirò"</note> in the printed edition is a massive error, since she had already attracted the king's hatred to the point that she was its victim. [The correct word *attristò* suggests the hatred of the onlookers was turned to sadness or the King's own resolve was shaken].

### CHAPTER LXXXVI.

## **Cleopatra, Queen of Egypt.**

Cleopatra was a woman of Egypt and became a subject of legend throughout the whole world, although, through many kings who reigned in the intervening years (1), she traced her lineage back to Ptolemy, King of Macedonia, the son of Lagus. She was the daughter of Dionysus, or, as some prefer, the daughter of King Minos. Nonetheless, she came to power through wickedness (2); and she was famous for almost no other reason than her beauty. But, conversely (3), she was known throughout the world for her greed, cruelty, and lust. According to what some say (so that we may begin at the start of her reign), Dionysus or Minos—whatever his name might have been—was a great friend of the Roman people. Upon his death during the first consulship of Julius Caesar, by a sealed will (4), he decreed that the eldest of his sons, whom some believe was named Lifania <note>This name is likely a corruption of Ptolemy XIII, Cleopatra's brother</note>, should take Cleopatra as his wife, who also...

---

(1) In the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition, the text reads *per multos medios reges* <note>original Latin: "through many middle kings"</note>. *Through many kings who were placed in between.*
(2) Thus Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani, the 14th-century translator of Boccaccio</note> has translated the Latin *per nephas* <note>original Latin: "through wickedness" or "contrary to divine law"</note>.
(3) From the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition, and it is literally the *e contrario* <note>original Latin: "on the contrary"</note> of the text. *Conversely*, in the Florentine manuscript, which is even more of a Latinism than the first.
(4) An excellent reading from the Florentine manuscript required by the text, *signatis tabulis* <note>original Latin: "signed/sealed tablets"</note>, which corrects the "secret will" <note>original Italian: "secreto testamento"</note> found in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.

<keywords>Mariamne, Herod, Cleopatra, Egypt, Ptolemy, Julius Caesar, lineage, succession, Albanzani</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Cleopatra VII, picking up from the decree of her father that she should marry her brother and rule jointly. The text focuses on her early power struggles and the intervention of Roman generals Pompey and Caesar.</meta>

...was the eldest daughter. And upon his death, they were to reign together. This came to pass, for among the Egyptians, it was forbidden (1) only by reason of dishonor to take mothers and daughters as wives. And certainly, Cleopatra desiring to rule alone (2), as some have said, she poisoned that youth (3) of fifteen years of age, who was her brother and husband, and she remained alone at the head of the kingdom. Then they say that, the great Pompey having already occupied almost all of Asia by force (4), having gone to Egypt, he substituted another boy (5) in place of that brother, and made him king of Egypt. For this reason, the indignant Cleopatra took up arms (6) against him. While things stood thus, Pompey having been defeated by Caesar in Thessaly, and then that boy whom he had made king having been killed (7) on the shore of Egypt, Caesar arrived (8) at that place and found that they were making war among themselves, those who were called to speak before him their...

---

(1) A very serious error in the printed edition is the phrase "was greatly in use by reason of dishonor to take mothers and daughters as wives" (in place of *forbidden*). It was *schiuso* in manuscript B, *exciuso* in manuscript A, and *exchiuso* in the Florentine manuscript <note>All variations of "excluded" or "forbidden"</note>. The text and the sense were sufficient: *mothers and daughters only were excluded from marriage* <note>original Latin: "matres filiasque tantum a coniugiis exclusisse"</note>.
(2) And not "only" <note>Italian: *solo*</note> as in the printed edition <note>The translator notes the difference between *sola* (alone/by herself) and *solo* (only)</note>.
(3) According to manuscripts A and the Florentine. The printed edition and manuscript B say "young man" <note>Italian: *giovane*</note>; the Latin source says "youth" <note>original Latin: "adolescentulum"</note>.
(4) "By force" <note>Italian: *per forza*</note> is in the manuscripts but omitted in the printed edition. The source text requires it: *had occupied with arms* <note>original Latin: "occupasset armis"</note>.
(5) And not as the printed edition says: "substituted another brother in place of that brother."
(6) This is "took up arms" <note>Italian: *pigliò l'armi*</note>, quite different from the "took courage" <note>Italian: *pigliò animo*</note> found in the printed edition, which in this chapter blunders even more than usual.
(7) "Dead" <note>Italian: *morto*</note> here carries the meaning of "slain." The source text reads: *And with the boy king made by him having been slain on the Egyptian shore* <note>original Latin: "Et puero rege a se facto littore in Aegyptiaco caeso"</note>.
(8) From the Florentine manuscript. The Turin manuscripts and the printed edition use "arrived" <note>Italian: *arrivato* vs. the Florentine *giunto*</note>.

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Cleopatra VII, following her arrival before Julius Caesar to plead her case for the throne of Egypt. The previous page ended with Caesar finding the siblings at war upon his arrival in Alexandria.</meta>

<page-num>288</page-num>

...reason. Leaving aside the young Ptolemy <note>Cleopatra’s younger brother, Ptolemy XIII</note> (1), Cleopatra—who was malicious by nature and possessed great self-confidence—presented herself before Caesar adorned in royal splendor. She believed she could secure the kingdom if she could entice Caesar, the conqueror of the world, into her lust. Being a woman of great beauty, and knowing how to capture those she desired through the art of her eyes and the charm of her words, she drew the lustful prince to her pleasure with little effort. Amidst the murmurs of the Alexandrian people, she remained with him for his amusement, and by him she gave birth to a son—as nearly everyone agrees (2)—whom she later named Caesarion, after his father. 

Finally, the boy Ptolemy, having been set aside by Caesar and incited by his own followers, turned his weapons against his supposed liberator. He marched to the Delta with an army against Mithridates of Pergamum, who was coming to Caesar’s aid (3). There, Ptolemy was defeated by Caesar, who had arrived by another route (4). The boy fled in a boat, but because of the great weight of the many people who crowded into it, the vessel sank. Thus, with the situation settled and the Alexandrians having surrendered (5), Caesar prepared to depart against Pharnaces, King of Pontus, who had been...

---

(1) I provide the original text here for the clarity of this vernacular translation: <note>original Latin: "Ut de Ptolemeo iuvene sileamus," meaning "So that we may be silent regarding the young Ptolemy."</note>

(2) The Latin text reads: <note>original: "ut fere omnibus placet"</note>. In Italian, the phrase "to agree" (*accordarsi*) is used here to mean "to consent," though it is a rare usage. It differs from the simple verb "to grant" (*accordare*), as it implies a more spontaneous and full agreement of opinion or speech. According to Tommaseo’s Dictionary, "to grant" often carries a sense of condescension.

(3) The word "aid" (*soccorso*) is found in the Florentine manuscript; "assistance" (*aiutorio*) is used in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.

(4) This phrasing in the manuscripts follows the original Latin: <note>original Latin: "ibique a Cesare qui itinere alio illum prevenerat superatus," meaning "and there he was overcome by Caesar, who had gotten ahead of him by another route."</note> The printed edition is less accurate, reading: "in aid of Caesar who had arrived by another route: and in that place he was defeated by Caesar and fled in a boat."

(5) This follows the Florentine manuscript. The Turin manuscripts and the printed edition use a different form of "surrendered" (*arrenduti*).

...favorable to Pompey <note>referring to Pharnaces, who had supported Caesar's rival, Pompey the Great</note>, as if Caesar owed Cleopatra payment for his pleasure and because she had remained loyal—since she desired nothing else—**he granted her** (1) the kingdom of Egypt. He removed her sister Arsinoe from the country, so that perhaps, under her sister's leadership, no new rebellion might be attempted against him. And so, having already acquired the kingdom through a double sin <note>likely referring to her adultery with Caesar and the civil war against her brother</note>, Cleopatra gave herself over to her pleasures. Having become almost a **prostitute** (2) for the kings of the East, and being greedy for treasure and jewels, she not only used her arts to strip her lovers of such things, but it was even said that she left the sacred Egyptian temples empty of their vessels, statues, and other treasures.

Then, after the death of Caesar, once Brutus and Cassius had been defeated and Mark Antony was traveling into Syria, she went out to meet him. She **easily** (3) captured that lustful man with her beauty and her **charming eyes** (4), holding him miserably captive in her love. She led him so far that—having already caused her brother to be killed with poison to **remove** (5) all suspicion regarding her rule—she arranged for her sister Arsinoe to be killed by Antony’s hand in the Temple of Diana at Ephesus. That unhappy woman had fled there seeking safety, but Cleopatra received this death from her new lover as payment for her adulteries, in place of a **first gift** (6).

---

(1) The Florentine manuscript reads *he granted her*; Manuscript B reads *he gave to her*; Manuscript A reads *he grants him*; the printed edition reads *he granted her*, which does not seem to me to be from the Cassinese manuscript, as the form is too stylized.

(2) According to the Florentine manuscript; the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition use the word *whore*.

(3) According to the Florentine manuscript; the others use *lightly*.

(4) The printed edition lacks the phrase *lustful man with her beauty and her charming eyes, and held him*, which is present in the Latin source: <note>original Latin: "impurum hominem pulchritudine sua et lascivientibus oculis facile cepit," meaning "she easily captured the impure man with her beauty and wanton eyes."</note>

(5) From the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition. The Florentine manuscript uses the phrase *to take away*.

(6) The Latin text reads: <note>original Latin: "id adulteriorum suorum a nuovo amasio loco primi muneris assumptura," meaning "about to receive this [Arsinoe's death] for her adulteries from her new lover in place of a first gift."</note>

And because that wicked woman already knew Antony's character, she did not fear to ask him for the kingdom of Syria and Arabia. And certainly, although this seemed to him a very great and unsuitable thing <note>The translator notes that the phrase "unsuitable thing" (Italian: *non convenevole*) aligns with the original Latin *incongruum* better than the Florentine manuscript's "unworthy thing."</note>, he nonetheless gave her some portion of each of those to satisfy the desire of the woman he loved. Returning to Egypt through Syria, she was magnificently received by Herod Antipater <note>The text refers to Herod the Great, whose father was Antipater; the author here conflates the names.</note>, who was King of the Jews at that time. And she did so not for a short time, nor in the hope of marrying again later, but persevering even into old age and unto death.

And, certainly, enough has not been said in words; but perhaps something remains for consideration <margin>(4) This reading is from the printed edition, following the original Latin: *consideratione forsan aliquid superextat* <note>original: "perhaps something remains beyond consideration"</note>. The manuscripts known to me read: *But perhaps something remains for the conscience*, a word which, in the context of knowing and understanding, implies reflection, and is therefore synonymous with "consideration" in this sense. To have a conscience of oneself, one must have reflected and considered upon oneself.</margin>. This consideration, because it certainly exceeds the knowledge of those who write, let it suffice to have left it to be contemplated, and with worthy consideration to be magnified by those of sacred intellect <margin>(5) The printed edition lacks the phrase: *and with worthy consideration to be magnified*, which is found in the original Latin text: *considerantium merita consideratione extollendum satis sacri ingenii reliquisse* <note>original: "the merits of those considering should be extolled by a sufficiently sacred intellect"</note>.</margin>.

## CHAPTER LXXXVIII.

### Agrippina, Wife of Germanicus.

Agrippina was the daughter of Marcus Agrippa and of Julia, the daughter of the Emperor Marcus <note>The text uses "Marcus" to refer to Augustus (Octavian), whose full name was Gaius Julius Caesar Octavianus; however, he was born the son of Octavius and later adopted. The author likely uses "Marcus" as a general Roman honorific or is conflating names.</note>, although Gaius Caligula—the son of this same Agrippina—once he had become emperor of the world, loathing the low social status of his maternal grandfather <note>Marcus Agrippa was of humble, non-aristocratic origins before becoming Augustus's right-hand man.</note>, used to say that his mother had not been fathered by Agrippa, but by the adultery committed by Octavian with his own daughter Julia. He <note>Caligula.</note> foolishly desired <note>The Florentine manuscript uses the word *disiando* (desiring) rather than *desiderare*.</note> to be believed more noble if his mother had been born of such an incestuous union than if she had been born from a base <margin>The reading "vile" (base or low-born) is confirmed by all manuscripts and follows the Latin text *ignobili*. A common error in printed versions, which use "simile" (similar), was likely a misreading of "sì vile."</margin> father according to lawful marriage. 

But whoever's daughter she might have been <note>The original phrasing "di chi" is used here to mean "whoever," mirroring the Latin *Sed cuiuscumque fuerit filia*.</note>, she was married to Germanicus, a beautiful man of her own age, who was much needed by the Republic and was, by adoption, the son of Tiberius Caesar. For this marriage she was quite famous; but she was even more famous because she, with a stubborn purpose, resisted the treachery of the most arrogant emperor <margin>The text should read "the treachery of the most arrogant emperor" rather than "the most arrogant treachery of the emperor." The Latin source describes the *insolentissimi principis* (the most insolent prince/emperor).</margin> <note>This refers to the Emperor Tiberius, who became a bitter enemy of Agrippina and her children.</note>.
