Threats and beatings <note>The Latin term *verberibus* ("beatings") is rendered as *busse* in manuscript B and the printed edition, and as *botte* in manuscript A and the Florentine manuscript.</note> were of no use in making her take food. Therefore, so that he would not lack material for his cruelty, he [Tiberius] resorted to making her take food by force, so that he might provide nourishment to one who did not want it, in whatever way it might be placed into her stomach <note>*Messo* ("put") is the reading in the Turin manuscripts; *posto* ("placed") is in the Florentine manuscript. The first editor had to supplement this with the translation by Betussi <note>Giuseppe Betussi was a 16th-century author who translated Boccaccio’s Latin work into Italian in 1545</note>. One can see the difference there.</note>. 

But the more Agrippina was pressured with injuries, the more hardened she became in her purpose <note>The Turin manuscripts, following the Latin text *tanto acrioris efficiebatur propositi* ("she became of a much sharper purpose"), read "the more hardened she became in her purpose." The printed edition reads "persevering with a harsher purpose," and the Florentine manuscript says "she became so much more embittered in her purpose."</note>, persevering in what she had begun; and, by dying, she overcame the pride of the wicked <note>The phrase "wicked prince" (*scellerato principe*) in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition faithfully follows the Latin *scelesti principis*. This is stronger than the word "unfair" (*iniquo*) found in the Florentine manuscript; the former comes from the word for "crime" (*scelus*), while the latter comes from "not equal" (*non equus*).</note> prince. She made it known that, although he might wish it, he could cause many to die, but he could not save anyone who wished to die, despite all the power of his rule. By this death, certainly, although she gained much glory among her own people, she nonetheless left much more shame upon Tiberius.

### CHAPTER LXXXIX.

## **Paulina, a Roman woman.**

Paulina, a Roman woman, acquired a nearly inextinguishable reputation <note>The Turin manuscripts and the printed edition use "reputation" (*nominanza*), while the Florentine manuscript uses "fame" (*fama*).</note> for a certain simplicity <note>In this context, "simplicity" refers to a mixture of innocence and gullibility.</note> of hers. This lady—

<summary>Agrippina the Elder successfully ends her life through starvation despite Emperor Tiberius's attempts to force-feed her, leaving him in shame; the text then introduces Paulina, a Roman woman famous for her naive nature.</summary>
<keywords>Agrippina the Elder, Tiberius, suicide, hunger strike, Paulina, Roman women, Giuseppe Betussi, manuscript variations</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the tragic end of Agrippina the Elder to the story of Paulina, a Roman noblewoman. The narrative shifts focus from political tragedy to a moralizing tale about religious devotion and deception. The footnotes continue to compare various Italian manuscript traditions (Florentine vs. Turin) against Boccaccio's original Latin text.</meta>

<page-num>303</page-num>

During the reign of Emperor Tiberius over the Romans, just as she was considered famous above all others for the beauty of her face and body (1), so too, after she was married, she was esteemed by all (2) for her glorious virtue <term>virtue: the original Italian *onestà* implies both moral integrity and sexual chastity</term>. And, besides her husband, she cared for nothing with singular devotion except to serve and acquire the grace of Anubis, the god of the Egyptians, whom she loved with the greatest devotion (3). 

And since beautiful women are loved by young men everywhere, and especially those who are diligent in their care for chastity, a young Roman man named Mundus <note>Original: *Mondo*</note>, captivated by her beauty, had begun to solicit her intensely with his eyes, with gestures, and with charms, sometimes with promises and gifts, and at other times with prayers and flattery, to see if by chance he might obtain what he ardently desired. But every effort was in vain, because the most chaste woman, devoted only to her husband, rejected everything from the lover. 

He, pursuing his enterprise and realizing through clear signs that the path was blocked (4) for him by the lady’s constancy, turned his wit toward deception. Paulina was accustomed to visiting the temple of Isis every day, and she honored Anubis with continuous sacrifices. When the young man learned of this, with love showing him the way (5), he devised an unheard-of trick...

***

(1) This is the reading in the manuscripts, and not "beauty of body" as found in the printed edition. The original Latin text reads, "beauty of face and body" <note>Original: *formositate oris et corporis*</note>.
(2) "By every man" is the reading in the printed edition and the Turin manuscripts. "By all" is from the Florentine manuscript and is simpler, especially since the original Latin text has "by all" <note>Original: *a cunctis*</note>.
(3) This is one of the examples in which the word "her" <note>Original: *lei*</note> in the Florentine manuscript could cause ambiguity: "whom... she loved."
(4) "The path was taken from him" is from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition. The Florentine manuscript reads, "the path was removed from him."
(5) This is a reading that is not clear enough in the Florentine and Turin manuscripts, one of which contains this phrasing. The Latin is much more clearly understood: "love suggesting the trick" <note>Original: *amore suadente dolum*</note>.

...another time (1). And, having considered that the priests of Anubis <note>Anubis: the jackal-headed Egyptian god of the dead; his cult, along with that of Isis, was highly popular but often viewed with suspicion by conservative Roman authorities</note> might be of great assistance to his desire, he went to them and, with magnificent gifts, won them over to his purpose.

It so happened that, at his pleading, the priest most venerable for his old age spoke with pleasant words to Paulina when she arrived in her accustomed manner, telling her that Anubis had come to him during the night and had commanded him to tell her (2) that he was greatly delighted by her devotion, and that he desired to speak with her in the temple while she slept. When Paulina had heard this (3), thinking that this had occurred because of her own holiness, she rejoiced immeasurably within herself at those words, and believed them to be as true (4) as if she had heard them with her own ears from the god Anubis.

She reported all these words to her husband, who, being even more foolish than his wife, gave his consent when she asked to stay in the temple for the night (5). (2) A good reading from manuscript B. The others and the printed edition say, "had commanded him to tell that woman."

(3) "Had understood" <note>Original: *ebbe intesa*</note> is from the same manuscript; "had heard" is from the others and the printed edition.

(4) "Believed them so true" is a good reading from manuscript A, adhering to the original Latin text: <note>Original: *adeoque vera credidit* — "and so she believed them true."</note> The other manuscripts and the printed edition read "believed them to be true."

(5) And not as in the printed edition: "in which Paulina slept all night."

...that god. To her, while she was already asleep <note>original Latin: "Cui iam soporatae Mundus a sacerdotibus intromissus." The awkward phrasing here in the Italian reflects a very literal translation from the Latin: "To whom, already asleep, Mundus was let in by the priests."</note>, the lover was let in <note>The Turin <term>codices: hand-written manuscript books</term> use the word "placed," while the printed edition uses "put."</note> by the priests; and subsequently, dressed in the ornaments of Anubis, he appeared <note>A reading from Manuscript A and the printed edition follows the Latin "exposito ornatu Anubis" (having displayed the attire of Anubis). ...and took the desired delight (1). But as day was breaking, he told that deceived woman that she had conceived a son. And when it was already day (2), the bed having been carried out of the temple by the priests, Paulina brought back to her husband that...

---

(1) In the printed edition, there is a gap between the words *slept all night* and *desired delight*. It is likely that this gap existed in the original <term>Cassinese codex: a specific handwritten manuscript from the Abbey of Monte Cassino</term>, or perhaps the editor introduced it. Finding the passage missing in his source, the editor wisely chose not to fill it with the translation by Betussi <note>Giuseppe Betussi was a 16th-century Italian writer known for his popular translations of Boccaccio</note>, judging that this long passage was, as is indeed the case, too explicit, and could be omitted without harming the story. Many who held the view that Boccaccio, with his work *Famous Women*, intended to atone for the license of the *Decameron*, have clearly not read this chapter regarding the Roman Paulina, not even in the Latin, which I reproduce here in this note. This allows one to see that Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani, a friend of Boccaccio and the first to translate "Famous Women" into Italian</note>, while translating into the vernacular, took care to soften certain phrases of Boccaccio that were too "ardent."

<note>The following is the translation of the original Latin text Boccaccio wrote, which the Italian editor found too scandalous for the main text: "He [the husband], more foolish than his wife, agreed to the requester, that she might stay the night in the temple. Therefore, in the sacred house, with everyone ignorant except for her and the priests, a bed worthy of a god was prepared. As darkness now overshadowed the earth, Paulina entered the place; with witnesses removed, after prayers and sacred rites, she approached the bed to await the god. To her, while she was already drowsy, Mundus was let in by the priests. Dressed in the finery of Anubis, he was present, and eager for the woman he loved, he rushed to kiss her. As she woke up in astonishment, he commanded her to be of good heart, saying he was Anubis whom she had long venerated, fallen from heaven by her prayers and devotion, and had come to lie with her so that from him and her a similar god might be born. She first asked her lover whether the gods above were able or accustomed to mingle with mortals. To which Mundus immediately replied that they could, and gave the example of Jupiter falling into Danae’s lap through the roof tiles, and by that union fathering Perseus, who was later taken up into heaven. Having heard these things, she joyfully agreed to the request. Mundus, naked in place of Anubis, entered the bed and enjoyed the desired embrace and intercourse. But as night was turning into day, departing, he told the deceived woman she had conceived a son."</note>

(2) Between the phrase *as day was breaking* used earlier, which reflects the Latin *cum iam nox iret in diem* (when night was already turning to day), and *when it was already day*, which is the Latin *mane autem facto* (morning having been made), there is some confusion in certain manuscripts; thus the printed version reads: *But as day was breaking . . . . and having already had the bed brought back*, and so on.

(1) From the Turin and Florentine <term>codices: ancient handwritten manuscript books</term>. The printed version says, *what was done*, corresponding to the Latin *quae acta sunt retulit* <note>original: "quae acta sunt retulit" — he reported the things that were done</note>.

(2) The word *both* which is in this text is missing from the printed version, which reads: *ab ambobus fuisset expectatum pariendi tempus* <note>original: "ab ambobus fuisset expectatum pariendi tempus" — the time of birth would have been awaited by both</note>.

(3) *He who knew* according to the Turin manuscripts. *Him who knew* in the Florentine.

(4) *Yield* is from the two aforementioned codices, while the Florentine has *bow*, with the phrase *more readily*, which I have placed before the *rather* found in the others.

(5) *Desirous* is from the Florentine codex, *eager* from the Turin ones.

(6) This passage is also missing from the printed version, and very likely from the <term>Cassinese codex: a manuscript from the monastery of Monte Cassino</term>. (7) The text here is misunderstood: *beata es Paulina cum ex me Anube deo conceperis* <note>original: "beata es Paulina cum ex me Anube deo conceperis" — you are blessed, Paulina, since you have conceived by me, the god Anubis</note>; that is, when you have birthed a god from me, Anubis.

(8) The printed version is less accurate: *from that word it followed that Paulina, marvelling...*

For Paulina, having marveled and called to memory (1) many of the things she had heard and what had been done, suddenly realized the trickery; distressed, she returned to her husband and told him of the deception of Mundus and the priests, just as she had perceived it (2). (1) This is a good reading from <term>Codex A: an ancient handwritten manuscript</term>, "having returned to memory"; Codex B and the Florentine manuscript agree, but the printed version does not say "recalled more things."

## CHAPTER 90.

### Agrippina, mother of Nero.

Agrippina, the mother of the Emperor Nero, was no less famous for her lineage, her high-ranking relatives, and her power, as well as for the monstrosity of her son and herself (3), than for her own famous deeds (4). She was the daughter of Germanicus Caesar—an excellent young man of praiseworthy life—born to the Agrippina mentioned previously <note>Agrippina the Elder, who was featured in an earlier chapter</note>. She was called Julia Agrippina, the sister of the Emperor Gaius Caligula, and she was the wife of Gnaeus...

---

(3) The Turin and Florentine manuscripts read: "the monstrosity of her son and of herself." The word "herself" is found in the Cassinese manuscript and the printed edition. <note>original Latin: "Monstruositate sua" — by her own monstrosity.</note>

(4) The word "famous," which is in all the manuscripts and the Latin text <note>original Latin: "claris facinoribus" — famous deeds or crimes</note>, is missing from the printed edition.

Domitius—a most offensive (1) and stern man of the Ahenobarbus family (2)—was the one by whom she gave birth to Nero, who was born feet-first and became a monstrous beast to the entire world (3). But after Domitius died of the disease of leprosy (4) while Nero was still a child, and because Agrippina was a great beauty, her brother Gaius <note>Emperor Caligula</note>, a most dishonorable man, drew her into a foul adultery (5). Once he was made emperor—either because she had committed a fault with Lepidus in the hope of gaining power, or because he was urged on by one of her enemies (6)—he deprived her of nearly all her wealth (7) and confined her to an island (8). But after he was killed by his own guards and Claudius was installed as emperor, he had her return. (1) An example of *fastidiosissimo* <note>original: "most annoying/offensive"</note> used in a moral sense, to be added to the vocabulary. <term>Codex B</term> says "most famous."

(5) With the word "adultery," the translator Albanzani renders the Latin text's "vile violation" <note>original: *turpi stupro*</note>.

(6) The printed version says "some enemy of his," meaning Lepidus's enemy. I interpret "rival" as referring to a rival of Agrippina.

(7) That word "nearly," which corresponds to the Latin *fere*, is not superfluous as it seemed to the first editor.

(9) The Florentine manuscript uses "immediately." ...her beauty inspired a great desire (1) for marriage on the part of the chaste prince Claudius, through the favor of Pallas, even though he was the brother (2) of Germanicus, her father. This was in opposition to Lollia Paulina, who was supported by the freedman Callistus, and against Aelia Paetina, who was supported by Narcissus. However, it seemed that decency stood in the way of his desire, because she was his niece (3) through his brother; but through the speech of Vitellius, who was himself corrupt, it happened that Claudius was compelled (4) toward that which he already desired by the petitions of the Senate. Vitellius moved that the Senate should issue a decree stating that uncles (5) <note>original: *barbani*</note> could take their nieces (6) as wives. And so Agrippina, with Claudius's consent and the Senate's petition, entered into marriage with him. She was eventually titled Augusta: and she would travel to the Capitol in a carriage, a privilege which previously (7) was granted only to priests. And she began to act cruelly toward those who had been her rivals. Finally, being most cunning and having waited for the right time—as she had both sons and daughters—she persuaded him, being encouraged by Memmius Pollio, who was then consul, and strongly urged by Pallas...

---

(1) The Florentine manuscript uses *disio* <note>original: "desire"</note>, as is usual for that text.
(2) "Had been the brother" in the Florentine manuscript and the printed edition follows the authority of the original Latin text, *Germanici fuisset frater* <note>original: "he had been the brother of Germanicus"</note>. But even if he "had been" so in the past, Claudius was still the brother of Germanicus at that time; therefore, I have chosen the reading from the Turin manuscripts.
(3) *Nipote* <note>original: "niece"</note> in Codex B and the printed edition. The Florentine manuscript has *necia* and Codex A has *neça*, terms which survive today in the Lombard and Venetian dialects.
(4) *Stretto* <note>original: "pushed/compelled"</note> in Codex B and the printed edition.
(5) *Barbani* <note>original: "uncles"</note> is found in all manuscripts and the printed edition. <term>Barba</term> and <term>barbano</term>, meaning "uncle," are words that remain in certain regional dialects.
(6) Here too, the manuscripts use the dialect forms *neççe* (Codex B) and *necie* (Florentine).
(7) The printed edition reads *e non con* <note>original: "and not with"</note>, and *la qual cosa era anzi solo concesso* <note>original: "which thing was before only granted"</note>. In this context, the word *anzi* <note>original: "before"</note>—which I do not find in the manuscripts—used in place of *per innanzi* <note>original: "previously/formerly"</note> is a source of confusion.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Agrippina the Younger's schemes. Having secured her marriage to the Emperor Claudius, she now focuses on consolidating power for her son, Nero, by displacing the Emperor's biological son, Britannicus.</meta>

<page-num>311</page-num>

The freedman <note>Referring to Pallas, Claudius's influential advisor</note>, who—because he was committing adultery with Agrippina—was her greatest supporter, persuaded him (1) to adopt as his own son his stepson Nero (a thing which no one remembered ever being done in the family of the Claudii) and to give him Octavia as his wife. Octavia was the daughter he had fathered with Messalina, and she had been betrothed (2) to Lucius Silanus, a most noble young man. Once she had obtained these things, thinking that the wild beast had fallen into the snare (3), she was not only spurred on by her weariness of Claudius’s continual (4) greed <note>The Italian *avarizia* here refers to Claudius's legendary gluttony or "engorging," as noted in the philological commentary below</note>, but was also frightened (5) that before Claudius’s death, his son Britannicus might reach a firm age <note>meaning adulthood or the age of legal maturity</note> (6). While Narcissus was pleading many things on behalf of Britannicus—acting as if he considered Agrippina's purpose to be contrary to his own—previously (7), she found a deadly invention to bring about Claudius's death. Claudius certainly took delight in boleti <term>mushrooms, specifically porcini</term> (8), and said they were the food of the gods; he said this because they were born without seeds. Considering this fact, Agrippina studied—

---

(1) This word *persuaded him* <note>original: "indusselo"</note> in Codex B, the Florentine manuscript, and the printed edition is a repetition; it is not to be blamed, especially in long sentences where the governing verb is too far away. Codex B omits it.
(2) *She was betrothed for him to Lucius Silanus* in the printed edition. The original Latin text reads: *Lucio Syllano nobili iuveni desponsata fuerat* <note>original: "she had been betrothed to the noble youth Lucius Silanus"</note>.
(3) The error in the printed edition—using "table" <note>original: "tavola"</note> instead of "snare" <note>original: "tagliuola"</note>—is too massive. The original Latin: *Rata in casses beluam incidisse* <note>original: "Thinking the beast had fallen into the nets"</note>.
(4) *Continual greed* <note>original: "continua avarizia"</note>, which is the reading of the manuscripts known to me, conforms to the Latin text *Claudii assiduarum ingurgitationum* <note>original: "Claudius's constant engorgings"</note>; this corrects the printed edition, which has "contrary greed."
(5) *Frightened* <note>original: "impaurita"</note> in these manuscripts corresponds to the *exterrita* <note>original: "terrified"</note> of the Latin text, and refutes the editor’s suggested addition, "bewildered" <note>original: "smarrita"</note>.
(6) The *aetas solida* <note>original: "firm or solid age"</note> of the Latin text is better rendered as *firm age* <note>original: "ferma età"</note> in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition than as "perfect age" <note>original: "perfetta età"</note> in the Florentine manuscript.
(7) Here the printed edition is poorly punctuated: *thinking his purpose the opposite, previously she found...*
(8) A quality of mushrooms commonly called *porcini*.

[...skilfully] cooked them, and poisoned them, and these (1), according to some, she placed (2) before him while he was already affected by wine. Some say that while he was eating in the temple with the priests, Agrippina sent them to him via Halotus <note>original: "Paraloto"</note>, his eunuch, who had been corrupted by her and who performed the duty of taster <note>The "credenza" was the ritual of tasting food to ensure it was not poisoned</note>. But as it seemed Claudius might recover his health through vomiting and a bowel movement, by the contrivance of Xenophon the physician, poisoned feathers were given to him to assist the vomiting, so that what the wife desired might follow (3). Finally, having been carried into his chamber, he died without the knowledge of anyone (4), except for Agrippina. His death was not made public by Agrippina until—with the help of time (5) and leaving aside Britannicus as being younger—Nero, already an adult, was raised up as Emperor. 

This event was so pleasing to Nero that he immediately placed (6) his mother before every man in both public and private matters, as one who had well deserved it. It seemed that he had taken the title for himself, but the sovereignty for his mother. And thus Agrippina was magnificent to the whole world at the height of the Roman Empire. (3) *The Florentine manuscript, as is its custom, uses "desiava" <note>a poetic/archaic variant</note> for "desired."*

(4) *From the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition. The Florentine manuscript reads: "Without anyone knowing it."*

(5) *Although the Cassinate manuscript has "help of time," which is also found in the other manuscripts, the first editor, conforming to the Latin, printed "help of friends" <note>original: "aiutorio degli amici"</note>. But one only resorts to the original Latin text in cases where the reading of the vernacular translation is doubtful. When the translation's reading is certain, it must be respected, even if it is an error; however, the error should be noted.*

(6) *The printed edition should be corrected where it has "proposed" <note>original: "propose"</note> instead of "placed before" <note>original: "prepose"</note>, which is required by the sense and the Latin source text "praeponeret."*

...married her uncle, and had killed him with mushrooms, and had raised the vile <note>The word "sconcio" implies something distorted, shameful, or vile.</note> (1) youth to the empire through deceit and force, she was led to a detestable death, although she had deserved it. Finally, she having escaped the snares he had set against her, Nero realized he needed to proceed with *more* cautious deceit. And, while he was searching, he was taught by Anicetus—the prefect of the fleet at Misenum, who in childhood had been his tutor—that a weak ship could be built; if Agrippina entered it, not noticing the trick, she could be cast into peril. ...past, showing her the affection of a son, and, embracing her, he led her into the house. Then, since the ship had been prepared for her death, and as she was about to go to dinner (1), she boarded it, being accompanied (2) by Crepereius Gallus and by Acerronia, both freedmen: and as they were sailing (3) at night, once the signal was given to those who were aware of the plot (4), the tiller (5) of the ship, which was heavy (6) with much lead, fell and killed Crepereius. ## CHAPTER LXXXI.

### Epicharis the servant.

Epicharis <note>The Italian text uses "Epitare." This refers to Epicharis, a freedwoman who later became a key figure in the Pisonian conspiracy against Nero.</note> was believed to be a foreign woman (4) rather than a Roman: and not only was she not famous because—

***

(1) The printed version does not say *the servants entered; to her Herculeus gave a blow with a club*, whereas the text reads *the servants having entered to her, first Herculius...* <note>original Latin: "introgressi ministri ad eam, primus Herculius..."</note>

(2) *And thus being killed, she was cremated that same night, and covered with earth with cheap funeral rites; at last, a slight mound [was made] for her on the road near Misenum and Julius Caesar’s villa* <note>original Latin: "Et sic occisa, nocte eadem cremata est, et vilibus exequiis terra contecta, levem demum tumulum sui (?) in via prope miscuum... et Caesaris Iulii villam eidem apponentibus."</note>. (The typo *miscuum* for *Misenum* in the Bern edition shows the magnitude of the errors in that version). The Turin and Florentine manuscripts conform to the text, except they render "levem tumulum" as "vile burial" <note>original: "vile sepoltura."</note>. The printed edition differs much more.

(3) *And finally he had her buried* is missing from the Florentine manuscript.

(4) *Of another nation than Roman* according to the Florentine manuscript.

<meta>This page continues the biography of Epicharis, who was introduced at the end of the previous page. It details the start of the Pisonian conspiracy against Nero and her attempt to recruit Volusius Proculus.</meta>

->— 317 —<-

she was not of noble lineage, but even worse, because she was the daughter of a slave and a freedwoman. And, what is a much more shameful thing, having never taken pleasure in any of the virtuous arts <note>original: "buone arti." In this context, it refers to the "liberal arts" or the education and moral pursuits expected of a high-born woman.</note>, yet toward the end of her life, she showed herself to have a noble soul and manly strength (1).

Certainly, as the pride and debauchery of the Roman Emperor Nero grew among the Romans and all Italians, it happened that the leader Lucius Piso, along with several Senators and other citizens, formed a conspiracy against him. While they were attempting to bring the matter to a conclusion through various discussions, I do not know by what means, but every detail came to the knowledge of the aforementioned Epicharis.

However, as the matter was being delayed too long in her opinion—as if she were burdened by resentment—she went into the countryside. By chance, while staying at Pozzuoli, so that she might not spend her time in vain, she went to Volusius Proculus, the prefect of the Roman fleet <note>Volusius Proculus was a high-ranking naval officer who had personally assisted Nero in the murder of his mother, Agrippina.</note>. He was the one who had killed Agrippina; she thought she could provide great help to the conspiracy if she could draw him to their side. And, having pointed out to him in long detail the perfidy, the annoyances, the dishonesty (2), the habits, the pride, and then the ingratitude of Nero toward him—noting that for such a great deed (that is, the death of Agrippina) Nero had not promoted him in any way, despite how well he had served him—she revealed the plot to him. With all her strength, she strove to add him...

***

(1) To decipher the intricate and convoluted—and therefore obscure—construction of the opening of this narrative, it is necessary to call upon the original text for help: <note>original Latin: "Epicharis is believed to be a foreign rather than a Roman woman, and not particularly distinguished by any brilliance of birth, but was born of a freedman father and was a freedwoman herself; and, what is far more shameful, she delighted in no virtuous pursuits. However, toward the end of her life, she revealed that she possessed a noble soul with manly strength."</note>

(2) The Latin text has *flagitiis, fastidiis, ineptisque moribus* <note>"crimes, disgusts, and foolish habits"</note>, which was perhaps translated by Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani, a 14th-century Italian scholar and friend of Petrarch and Boccaccio, who translated Boccaccio's Latin works into the vernacular.</note> as *the foul habits*. We have followed the reading of the Turin manuscripts rather than that of the printed edition, *the unseemliness of the habits*, or the Florentine manuscript, *the disturbances, the habits*.

Because Volusius, wanting to see if he could, through his service, win back <note>Note 1: The Florentine manuscript alone uses the word *chinare* (to bend or incline).</note> the emperor's favor for himself, as soon as he was able to reach the emperor <note>Note 2: This phrase is missing from the printed edition.</note>, he reported all of Epicharis's words to him. Yet, after long tortures were inflicted upon her, so that the torturers would not seem to be defeated by a woman <note>Note 5: This detail is missing from the printed edition, although it is found in the original Latin: *ne superari viderentur a foemina* ("lest they should seem to be overcome by a woman").</note>, she did not confess any secrets to the torturers. Finally, having been kept until the following day, being unable to walk on her own feet <note>Note 6: Based on the Florentine manuscript; the Turin manuscripts say "with her feet," while the printed edition says "at her feet."</note> and fearing that if she were tortured a third time she would not be able to endure it, she untied a band from her breast and tied it to the chair in which she was being carried...

<meta>This page concludes the account of the death of Epicharis (referred to as Epitare), a freedwoman who was a key figure in the Pisonian conspiracy against the Emperor Nero. ->— 319 —<-

she was being carried. And having fashioned a noose, she placed it <margin>(1) The Florentine manuscript reads "placed it on her neck," whereas other manuscripts and the printed edition say "put it."</margin> around her neck, and letting herself fall, she found death. She did this so that she might not bring harm to the conspirators, disproving <margin>(2) "Disproving" (*fallendo*), found in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition, is much more appropriate and effective than the Florentine manuscript's "having no place."</margin> the ancient proverb that women cannot keep secret <margin>(3) The B manuscript and the printed edition read "women keep silent about what they do not know"; manuscripts A and the Florentine manuscript read "do not say."</margin> even that which they do not know. And thus she left Nero uninformed and in fear. 

This deed, though it seems a very great thing for a woman, will appear even more marvelous if one considers the fickleness of the noble men involved in that same conspiracy. Although their involvement was known from sources other than Epicharis <margin>(4) The printed edition's phrasing "those who were known by others than Epitare" is poorly constructed.</margin>, there was not one among them of such robust youth <margin>(5) "Robust youth" from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition is much more faithful to the original Latin text, *robustae iuventutis* (of robust youth), than the "daring youth" found in the Florentine manuscript.</margin> that he could even endure hearing the names of the tortures to save his own life, while that woman endured the tortures themselves for the safety of others <margin>(6) This follows the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition. The Florentine manuscript reads: "that which that woman suffered for the safety of others."</margin>. On the contrary, they immediately confessed everything they knew of the conspiracy. And so, not one of them spared themselves or their friends, while that glorious woman spared everyone except herself <margin>(7) "Except herself" is found in the Florentine and B manuscripts. Others read "if not herself."</margin>. I would believe that the nature of things sometimes errs when she joins the soul to the bodies of men; that is, having infused <margin>(8) The Piedmontese manuscripts use *infonduto*, the printed edition uses *infonduta*. *Infuso* (infused) is found only in the Florentine manuscript.</margin> such a soul into the breast...

<note>The text here reflects on the philosophical irony that a freedwoman (Epicharis) exhibited the "masculine" virtue of Stoic endurance, while the high-born Roman men exhibited "feminine" weakness by confessing at the mere mention of pain.</note>

<page-num>320</page-num>

[the spirit] of a woman, which she believed she had infused into the breast of a man. But because God is the giver of such things, it is unbecoming to believe that He is <margin>(1) The Turin manuscripts use "be," the Florentine uses "shall be," and the printed edition "were not." Boccaccio's reasoning here is curious: Nature might make an error, but since God is the author of Nature, it is not right to believe He makes mistakes. The phrase "to be negligent" in this translation corresponds to the Latin *dormitari* <note>original: "to be drowsy" or "to nap"</note>.</margin> negligent concerning His work. Therefore, one must think that we receive everything perfect <margin>(2) The word "perfect," which is in the main text and the manuscripts, is missing from the printed edition. And I think that men should be ashamed when they are surpassed, not only by the passions of a woman, but even by any most constant endurance of hardships; for, if we are superior by virtue of being male, why is it not permitted <note>original: "lice"</note> that we should be superior in strength? <margin>(4) Some manuscripts and the printed edition use "is permitted" (*lice*), which we consider a correct reading. Others, like the Florentine manuscript, use a phrasing that corresponds to the Latin text: "it is fitting <note>original: *decens est*</note> that we should prevail in fortitude." It is well known in Italian that "to say" (*dire*) sometimes has the meaning of "to be fitting"—for example, saying a certain color "does not say" (*non dice*) to a young woman, meaning it "is not becoming." In that context, "permitted" (*lice*) would have a different value entirely.</margin> If this is not the case, we rightly appear effeminate in our character.

### CHAPTER LXXXIII.

### Sabina Poppea.

Sabina Poppea was a noble Roman woman, the daughter of T. Ollius (2) <note>The manuscript reads "Ellio," but historical records and the author's own footnote clarify this refers to Titus Ollius.</note>, a man of no great nobility, though she did not take her name from him, but rather from her grandfather on her mother’s side, Poppaeus Sabinus, a famous man distinguished by a triumph and a magnificent consulship <term>consulship: the highest elected political office in the Roman Republic and Empire</term>. Nor was she lacking in other womanly virtues, had she only possessed an honest soul. For she was of singular beauty, and resembled her mother (3), who, in her day, surpassed all others in beauty...

---

<margin>(1) *To mingle* <note>original: "meschiare"</note> from the A and Florentine manuscripts is more elegant than the very common *to mix* <note>original: "mescolare"</note> found in manuscript B and the printed edition.</margin>

<margin>(2) The manuscripts I am familiar with have some reading "Lellio" (for T. Ellio) and some "Lollio" (for T. Ollio); these variations are also found in the manuscripts of Tacitus, from whose *Annals* (Book XIII, ch. 45 and following) this entire account is taken, even adopting its phrasing. In the printed edition, the Latin text was followed (despite the reading of the Cassinese manuscript), calling her the daughter of Poppaeus Sabinus—a massive error immediately contradicted by the narrative itself, which asserts that Poppea did not take her father's name, but instead assumed that of her maternal grandfather, Poppaeus Sabinus. "But she had taken the name of her maternal grandfather, of the illustrious memory of Poppaeus Sabinus, who was distinguished by consular and triumphal honors" <note>original: "Sed nomen avi materni sumpserat, illustri memoria Poppaei Sabini consulari et triumphali decore praefulgentis"</note> (Tacitus, *Annals*, cited passage).</margin>

<margin>(3) *Resembling* <note>original: "somigliante"</note> in manuscript B and the Florentine. *Similar-looking* <note>original: "simiglievole"</note> in manuscript A and the printed edition.</margin>

...every nobility, excellence of character (1), and divine beauty. Within her resided all the desires of men (2), and the joys and delights (3) of happiness. <margin>(1) *Nobility granted, elegance of manners, and divine beauty* <note>original Latin: "Concessa nobilitas, elegantia morum, et divina formositas"</note>; the original text, which condemns the reading of the printed edition, *every nobility and excellence of character*, where *nobility* refers to *character*.</margin>

In the feminine form, *indugia* was then used more frequently than the masculine *indugio* <note>delay</note>, perhaps because it is closer to the Latin root *induciae* <note>a truce or pause in hostilities</note>.</margin>

### CHAPTER LXXXV.

#### Proba, wife of Adelphus.

Proba, in name and in deed, was a woman most worthy of being held in eternal memory for her knowledge of literature <term>science of letters: In this context, it refers to her mastery of poetry and classical learning</term> (3). And though her noble lineage and nation are unknown,

***

(1) Reading from the Turin manuscripts. The Florentine manuscript reads: *virtues are not accustomed to enter alone into the hearts of men*. The printed edition instead has this other reading: *only virtues are not accustomed to enter into the hearts of men*. This phrasing could suggest that, besides virtues, vices can also enter a man's heart. However, with the first reading, it is understood that *virtues, not being accustomed to enter alone into the heart*, usually enter in pairs <note>The author is reflecting on the psychological idea that a single strong character trait, even a violent one, usually implies the presence of other significant qualities.</note>.

(2) *Lost* <note>original Italian: *smarrite*</note> from the Florentine manuscript is more precise and effective than *forgotten* <note>original Italian: *smenticate*</note> used in other manuscripts. The original Latin text reads: *Although they have been removed from memory* <note>original Latin: *Quanquam a memoria sublata sint*</note>. The "filler" text in the printed edition is poorly constructed and makes little sense: *Truly I estimate however much they are forgotten, that Triaria was much more famous for other merits.* In any case, *forgotten* is the intended sense because the virtues are discussed in the previous sentence.

(3) This is the superior and unique reading of the Florentine manuscript, which is preferred over the others and the printed edition: *Proba, by name and by deed, was a woman most worthy that her memory be preserved through her knowledge of literature.*

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Proba, transitioning from her mysterious origins to her mastery of classical literature and the conception of her most famous work.</meta>

-> — 335 — <-

some are pleased to believe—and I myself am inclined to think it based on conjecture—that she was Roman. Some other famous men say that she was born in the land of Orta, was the wife of a man named Adelphus, and was of the Christian religion. Under whatever teacher she may have been, it can be clearly seen that she was excellent in the liberal arts <term>liberal arts: the traditional core of Roman education, including grammar, rhetoric, and logic</term>. Among her other studies, she was so well-instructed and familiar (1) with the verses of Virgil—with such diligent care that, as every work composed by her bears witness—she seemed to always have them present in her memory. Reading these works perhaps at times with more subtle consideration, the thought occurred to her (2) that from them one could describe the entire history of the Old and New Testaments pleasantly and fluently, using verses full of profound meaning (3). And certainly, it is not without wonder that such a high consideration entered the mind (4) of a woman; but it was a much more wonderful thing that the project was brought to fruition. Therefore, intent upon this pious thought, searching here and there through the verses of the *Bucolics*, the *Georgics*, and the *Aeneid*, taking now from one and now from another part of the verses, and sometimes taking them (5) whole, with marvelous skill she adapted them to her purpose, arranging them so aptly in—

***

(1) The word *familiar* <note>original Italian: *dimestica*</note>, which appears in the manuscripts and the text, matches the Latin *learned and familiar with the Virgilian poem* <note>original Latin: *Virgiliani carminis docta atque familiaris*</note>; it is missing from the printed edition.

(2) Only the Florentine manuscript uses "the thought reached her" <note>original Italian: *giunse in pensiero*</note>, which corresponds to the Latin *the thought occurred* <note>original Latin: *existimationem incidit*</note>.

(3) "Pleasantly and fluently" <note>original Italian: *piacevolmente e speditamente*</note> is found in the Turin manuscripts, while the Florentine has only "pleasantly"; this corresponds to the Latin *with a calm and unhindered verse* <note>original Latin: *placido atque expedito... versu*</note>. "Splendidly" instead of "fluently" is an error in the printed edition.

(4) Manuscript B uses *cierebro*, the Florentine *celebro*, while manuscripts A and the Cassinese use the standard word for "brain" or "mind" <note>original Italian: *cerebro*</note>.

(5) The Florentine manuscript uses "taking for herself" <note>original Italian: *pigliandosi*</note> again. "Taking them" <note>original Italian: *togliendoli*</note> from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition better renders the Latin *plucking* <note>original Latin: *carpens*</note>.

<summary>This page describes how the poet Proba, deeply learned in the works of Virgil, conceived the ambitious plan to retell the Bible using Virgil's own lines. This technique, known as a "cento," demonstrated her profound mastery of classical literature in service of her Christian faith.</summary>
<keywords>Proba, Virgil, Cento, Bible history, liberal arts, Christian poetry, Adelphus</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proba Falconia's literary technique, specifically how she seamlessly wove Virgil's pagan poetry into a Christian narrative, and reflects on her deep knowledge of the Bible.</meta>

<page-num>336</page-num>

-ly (1), and completing the broken lines, observing the law of the feet <term>feet: the rhythmic units or meter of a verse</term> and the dignity of the verse, such that no one, unless they were very expert (2), could notice where the addition was. And with this, starting from the beginning of the world, she composed everything that is read in the Old and New Testaments, up until the descent of the Holy Spirit, so neatly (3) that one who did not know of that composition (4) would easily believe (5) that Virgil had been a prophet and an evangelist. From which no less commendable a thing is understood in this woman (6), than that she knew the Holy Scriptures entirely, or at least fully (7); a thing which we know—to our sorrow—happens quite rarely even among the men of our own time. Furthermore, the noble woman wished that her work, thus composed, should have the name *Cento* <term>Cento: a poem made up of verses or passages from other authors</term>; a work which I have seen many times (8). And as for...

---

(1) *Aptly* <note>original Italian: *Attamente*</note> in the Turin manuscripts brings the literal meaning of the Latin *apte*, and refutes the "openly" found in the Florentine manuscript and the "optimally" of the printed edition.

(2) A good reading from the Turin and Florentine manuscripts, *as no one unless most expert* <note>original Latin: *ut nemo nisi expertissimus*</note>. The printed edition should be corrected, "the dignity of the verses" (better: "dignity of the verse"): "that one who was very expert."

(3) *Neatly* <note>original Italian: *Pulitamente*</note>, which is in the Turin manuscripts and the print, perfectly renders the *compte* <note>elegant/adorned</note> of the text. Not so the "wisely" of the Florentine manuscript.

(4) From Manuscript B. The phrasing "That of that composition he who did not know it" in the other manuscripts and print is convoluted.

(5) From the manuscripts I know, and it corresponds to the *facile* <note>original Latin: *easy/easily*</note> of the text, which refutes the "truly" of the print.

(6) Thus Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani, a friend of Boccaccio and translator</note> translated the words of the text: *From which no less commendable a thing is taken* <note>original Latin: *Ex quibus non minus commendabile sumitur*</note>.

(7) *Fully* <note>original Italian: *pienamente*</note> would seem synonymous with *entirely*, which is not what Boccaccio said with *integram vel satis plenam notitiam*, which signifies "full enough," that is, sufficient knowledge.

(8) This is from the manuscripts and the text: *which we ourselves have seen very often* <note>original Latin: *quod ipsi persaepe vidimus*</note>. It is missing from the printed edition.

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proba Falconia’s literary achievements, transitioning from her technique of rearranging Virgil's poetry to speculation about her lost works and her potential mastery of Greek literature.</meta>

<page-num>337</page-num>
<sig>22</sig>

...then, the more we consider that work worthy of perpetual memory (1), the less we believe that the famous genius of this woman was content with this work alone (2). Indeed, I think that if she lived many years, she composed other praiseworthy things which, through the fault of scribes and to our own loss, have not reached our age (3). But I ask, what more praiseworthy thing has been heard (4) than that a woman has scanned the verses of Virgil and Homer, and has taken those which were suited to her work, and has joined those chosen pieces with wonderful connection (5)? And let those worthy men consider—who, although they are wonderfully profound (6) in the pro— <note>The text cuts off mid-word; it likely refers to the "profession of sacred letters."</note>

---

(1) *And not how much more worthy we think that of the press*, because the Latin *illud* <note>original: "that thing"</note> refers to *opus* <note>original: "work"</note>, a word which, when rendered into the vernacular as *opera* <note>original: "work/labor"</note>, requires the feminine *quella* <note>original: "that one"</note>.

(2) Thus the Turin and Florentine manuscripts. The printed edition, following the Cassino manuscript, reads *of this work alone*; and I think the text requires it: *huic tamen (unless it is actually "tantum") acquievisse labori* <note>original Latin: "to have rested/acquiesced in this labor [alone]"</note>.

(3) In all the manuscripts, a sentence is missing that corresponds to the following in the Latin: *quae inter (ut nonnullis placet) fuit Homeri Centona eadem arte, et ex eadem materia, qua ex Virgilio sumpserat, ex Homero sumptis carminibus edita. Ex quo, si sic est, sumitur eius cum ampliori laude, eam doctissime graecas novisse literas ut latinas.* <note>Translation of Latin: "among which [works] (as it pleases some) was a Homeric Cento, produced with the same art and from the same material from which she had taken from Virgil, but using verses taken from Homer. From which, if it is so, her praise is further increased, for she most learnedly knew Greek letters as well as Latin."</note> Father Tosti filled this gap with the version by Betussi <note>Giuseppe Betussi was a 16th-century translator of Boccaccio</note>, but I do not follow him in this, as the reader can understand the easy Latin for themselves.

(4) The printed edition says, *But I ask, what thing has been more laudable, without anything further*, while the word *heard* <note>original Italian: *udita*</note> in the manuscripts is also required by the text: *Sed quaeso nunquid optabilius audisse faeminam...* <note>original Latin: "But I ask, is it not more desirable to have heard of a woman..."</note>.

(5) A good reading from the manuscripts, adhering to the text: *seu lecta artificioso contextu nectentem* <note>original Latin: "or weaving chosen things together with an ingenious connection"</note>. Therefore, the error in the printed edition should be corrected: *joined with wonderful knowledge* <note>The editor notes a typo in the printed version where "connection/joining" (congiungimento) was mistaken for "knowledge" (conoscimento).</note>.

(6) *Profound* <note>original Italian: *Profondissimi*</note> is from the Turin and Florentine manuscripts; the printed edition uses *most excellent*. The text, *sacrarum literarum insignis professio* <note>original Latin: "the distinguished profession of sacred letters"</note>, allows for both readings, and I have chosen the first, so that the quality of being "profound" in a study (which is less common than "going to the bottom" of it) may be supported by a new example from a fourteenth-century writer <term>trecentista: a writer or artist from the 1300s (the 14th century)</term>.

<summary>The author argues that Proba Falconia likely wrote more than just her famous Virgil-based poem, possibly including a similar work based on Homer. The text includes scholarly comparisons of various 14th-century manuscripts and early printed editions to determine the most accurate wording.</summary>
<keywords>Proba Falconia, Homeric Cento, textual criticism, translation history, Virgil, Homer, Italian manuscripts, Father Tosti, Betussi</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proba Falconia’s literary achievements, following the previous page's mention of those "profound in the profession of sacred letters." The text highlights the difficulty of her task—composing a life of Christ using pagan poetry—and contrasts her intellectual industry with the idleness of other women.</meta>

<page-num>338</page-num>
<header>— 338 —</header>

[pro]fession of sacred Scripture, nevertheless, it is a very grave and laborious task to pull out parts from here and there across its vast breadth, and gather them (1) into the life of Christ with words scattered in prose, just as she did using the verses of unfaithful men <note>Proba used the "pagan" verses of Virgil to tell the story of the Bible.</note>. And if we consider the customs of women, the distaff, the needle, and the loom would have been enough for her, if she had wished to live ignobly, according to the custom (2) of the majority (3). But because she, diligent in sacred study, polished (4) all the rust of laziness from her mind, she achieved eternal fame (5). Would to God that those women would look upon her with a good heart—those who consider it a great thing to stay in their rooms and occupy irretrievable time with vain fables. And oftentimes, from early morning until evening, and staying awake all night, speaking wicked or vain words (6): or truly giving themselves over to their own self-indulgence (7). And let them certainly think how great a difference there is in seeking fame...

---

(1) *Gather them*: Manuscripts A, the Florentine, and the printed edition use *Ragunarle*. Manuscript B uses *Raunarle*, which later became the common *radunare* <note>original Italian: "to gather or assemble"</note>.

(2) *Custom*: Used only in the Florentine manuscript <note>original Italian: "consuetudine"</note>.

(3) *The majority*: This manuscript uses this phrase with some novelty <note>original Italian: "parte maggiore"</note>, while the others, as usual, have "the greater part" <note>original Italian: "la maggior parte"</note>.

(4) *Polished all the rust from her mind* (corresponding to the Latin: *she wiped away every rust of sluggishness from her mind* <note>original Latin: "ingenio segnicei rubiginem abstersit omnem"</note>), although metaphorical, is a beautiful phrase. *Cleaned* <note>original Italian: "Nettò"</note> from the Florentine manuscript alone is much less effective.

(5) *Reached*: The aforementioned manuscript uses *giunse*, as it never wants to use *arrivare* <note>the Italian word for "to arrive" or "to reach"</note>, which is also a metaphor.

(6) This reading, which is from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition, corresponds to the Latin text *speeches either harmful or empty* <note>original Latin: "sermones aut nocuos aut inanes"</note>. It is to be preferred over that of the Florentine manuscript, *vain and useless words*.

(7) *Or to be at leisure only for their own playfulness* <note>original Latin: "Seu sibi tantum lasciviendo vacare"</note>. But the Latin words *lascivire* and *lascivia* do not always have the less-than-honest meaning of the Italian *lascivia* <note>often meaning "lust" or "lewdness" in modern Italian</note>, or the obsolete *lascivare*. Here Boccaccio intended to refer to the feeding on pomp and vanity typical of certain women.

<summary>This page concludes the praise of Proba Falconia's poetic ingenuity, contrasting her scholarly dedication with the "rust of laziness" and vanity found in idle women of Boccaccio's time. The footnotes provide philological comparisons between different Italian manuscripts and the original Latin text.</summary>

### CHAPTER LXXXVI.

## **Faustina Augusta.**

Faustina Augusta—who was later placed among the gods <note>A reference to the Roman practice of apotheosis, where deceased members of the imperial family were officially deified by the Senate.</note>—acquired much glory in both life and death, more through the kindness of her husband than by her own merit. She was the daughter of the Emperor Antoninus Pius and of Faustina, his wife. She was also the wife of Marcus Antoninus <note>Known to history as Marcus Aurelius.</note>, who had previously been adopted as a son by Antoninus Pius; and, upon the death of her father, she reigned together with her husband. By decree of the Senate she was called Augusta, which was no small glory <note>The manuscripts and the original Latin text use the phrase "no small glory for a woman in that time," correcting an error in the later printed edition which simply called it "no small thing."</note> for a woman in that time. And although emperors before this were called "Augustus," I find no empress before her who had been called "Augusta" by a decree of the Senate. Furthermore, she was of such singular beauty that something of the divine seemed mixed <note>The translator Albanzani specifically chose the word "mischiata" (mixed) here over "mescolata" (blended), a distinction noted by scholars of the manuscripts.</note> with her mortality. So that this beauty might not be consumed by old age or death, it happened that her face—both when she was a young girl and later in more mature age—was engraved upon coins of gold, silver, and copper,

...and it lasts until this time. In which, although the appearance of the face <note>original Italian: "abito della faccia"; Latin: "habitus oris." The translator is noting the difficulty of capturing the Latin sense of "habitus" (disposition or character) in the vernacular.</note> (1) is missing, along with the movement of the eyes, the living color, and the cheerfulness of the countenance <note>original Italian: "piacevolezza del viso"; Latin: "hilaritas faciei."</note> (2), the form nonetheless shows the greatest beauty. And certainly, as much as her fame is known throughout the whole world, so too is the infamy of the stain of her dishonesty. It was believed that she, besides her husband (3), was not content with a single lover (4), but rather kept company with many, whose names infamy has revealed. Named among her adulterers was one called Vettillo, and likewise Orfico was so reputed, and after him Moderato. But the one who surpassed (5) all others was called Tercolo, who is said to have been found at dinner with her by Antoninus <note>Referring to her husband, the Emperor Marcus Aurelius Antoninus.</note>. And to these is added (6) Marcus Vero, despite the fact that he was her son-in-law, the husband of Lucilla, her daughter <note>Historically, Lucilla married Lucius Verus, the co-emperor; the text here likely refers to him, though the name is slightly altered.</note>. And, what is the foulest thing of all, it is said that she loved a gladiator (7) so much that for ap...

---

(1) The <term>habitus oris</term> <note>Latin: "countenance" or "appearance of the face"</note>, which is a phrase common to the Latins, was translated by Albanzani as *habit of the face*, even though *habit* <note>Italian: "abito."</note> in the common tongue does not have the same meaning as *habitus*, which takes its value not from "to have" <note>Latin: "habere."</note> but from "to be" <note>Latin: "esse."</note>.

(2) The printed edition says *cheerfulness of the face*, which is the <term>hilaritas faciei</term> <note>Latin: "cheerfulness of the countenance"</note> of the text. But here *facies* is *face* or *countenance*.

(3) And not as in the printed edition *beyond the marriage*. The Latin is <term>praeter virum</term> <note>Latin: "besides the husband."</note>.

(4) The <term>amasius</term> <note>Latin: "lover" or "paramour."</note> of the text is *lover* and not *friend* as in that printed edition.

(5) Let the printed version be corrected, which says *he who passed through all others*, which here also has an ambiguous meaning.

(6) *Giunge* is used in the sense of *adds*. <term>His superadditus Marcus Verus</term> <note>Latin: "Added to these was Marcus Verus."</note>.

(7) The <term>gladiator</term> of the text is translated by Betussi as a certain *brigand* <note>Italian: "masnadiere," a term for a thug or highwayman.</note>. Let this suffice to judge the quality of his translation, which was sometimes used by the Neapolitan editor to fill the gaps in the Cassinese manuscript.

(1) From Manuscript A, since Manuscript B incorrectly has *to comfort her*. The Florentine manuscript reads: *wished rather to tolerate [her] so as not to arrive at greater shame.*

(2) A reading from the Florentine and Turin manuscripts, to be preferred over the printed version in this translation of an obscure passage of the text, which seems out of place: "we very often remember that even more honest men are accustomed to be shaken by an action." <note>original Latin: "saepissime meminimus etiam facto labefactari honestiores consueuisse"</note>. # CHAPTER 87 (2).

## Zenobia, Queen of the Palmyrenes.

Zenobia was the queen of the Palmyrenes <note>Palmyra was an ancient city in modern-day Syria which became a powerful empire under Zenobia’s rule in the 3rd century AD.</note>; and, by the testimony of ancient writings, she was of such excellent virtue that, by renown, she ought to be placed above all other peoples (3). She was first of all noble by nation, because the ancients say that she had a famous origin from the Ptolemies, the kings of Egypt, although there is no record of who her father and mother were (4). And they say that from her earliest childhood, she despised the exercises of the

<note>(1) From Codex A and the Florentine manuscript. Codex B and the printed edition have *Faustine*. It should be noted that throughout this chapter Boccaccio conflates much of the history of the two Faustinas <note>Faustina the Elder and her daughter, Faustina the Younger</note> into a single person.</note>
<note>(2) Chapter 97 in the [Latin] text is *Concerning Julia Soaemias <note>original: "de Semiamira"</note>, the woman of Emesa*, and Chapter 98 is *Concerning Zenobia, Queen of the Palmyrenes*. Albanzani <note>The 14th-century translator of this Italian version</note> did not translate <note>original: "volgarizzò" – rendered into the vernacular</note> Chapter 97 (according to the known manuscripts) because it was too scandalous <note>original: "libero" – referring to the sexually explicit nature of the account of Soaemias</note>; and we, who provide his translation and nothing else, do not care to fill such a gap ourselves or with another version, even that of Brother Antonio da S. women: and, having grown somewhat and become strong, for the most part she lived in the woods and wild places (1), and with bow and arrows she pursued, while running, deer and roebuck. And then, having become even stronger, she dared to grapple with bears; she pursued, waited for, captured, and killed leopards and lions; and, without fear, through one and another mountain pass (2), searching for the dens of wild beasts, and at night she slept in the open air; with marvelous patience enduring (3) the rain, the heat, and the cold. She, seeing Odaenathus intent on seizing the Empire of the East—since Valerian Augustus <note>The Roman Emperor Valerian, who was famously captured by the Persians in 260 AD</note> had been captured and condemned to a wretched servitude by Shapur, King of Persia, and his son Gallienus <note>Co-emperor with his father; historical accounts often portray him as ineffective during his father's captivity</note> was miserably weakened (1)—and not having forgotten (2) her original hardiness, she decided to hide her beauty under armor and engage in military service under her husband. And sparing herself no toil (3), sometimes training as a knight and sometimes as a captain (4), she defeated (5) not only that harsh man, who was skilled in the virtues of battle and arms, but it was believed that, through her efforts, Mesopotamia came under her dominion. who, under his father's name, had entered into the Empire of the East. And while already maintaining peace throughout the entire East—which belonged to the Romans (1)—alongside her husband, Odaenathus was killed (according to what some say) along with his son Herod by his cousin Maeonius out of envy. Although some others thought that Zenobia consented to the death of Herod, because she very often condemned his luxury <note>The original term "delicanza" refers to a perceived weakness or softness resulting from a life of ease</note> (2), and so that the succession of the kingdom would pass (3) to Herennianus and Timolaus, whom she had borne to Odaenathus. And while Maeonius was ruling, she remained quiet for a time. But after a short time, Maeonius having been killed by his own knights, and the possession being left as if vacant, that woman of noble spirit immediately entered into the desired lordship: and, while her sons were still small, she presented herself dressed and adorned (4) in the manner of a king, and in the name of her sons, governed the lordship much better than was deemed fitting for a woman; and not in a lowly way, because the emperors Gallienus, and after him Claudius (5), did not dare to attempt anything against her; nor likewise the eastern Egyptians, nor the Arabs, nor the—

(1) The Turin and Florentine manuscripts read consistently, *And already holding together with her husband in peace all the East which belonged to the husband*, contradicting the original Latin text: <note>original: "Orientem ad Romanos spectantem"</note> "the East belonging to the Romans," and the general sense. I have therefore not hesitated to follow the printed edition.

(2) *Delicanza* <note>meaning delicacy or softness</note> is from the aforementioned three manuscripts; *delicatezza* is from the printed edition.

(3) *Pervenisse* <note>to arrive or pass to</note> in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition. *Venisse* <note>to come</note> in the Florentine manuscript.

(4) The printed edition has only *dressed*. But the Latin would require even more than *adorned*.

(5) The punctuation of the printed edition spoils the very clear meaning. *And not lowly. Because Gallienus.* <note>original: "Nec segniter, nam Galienus."</note> "Not sluggishly, for Gallienus." The *because* provides the reason for why she did not rule *lowly*.

Saracens <note>original: "Saracini." The editor notes that this spelling is older than "Saraceni," following the Florentine manuscript</note> (1), nor the peoples of Armenia. Rather, all of them, fearing her power, were content to be able to defend their own borders. For she possessed such mastery of battle and such harsh military discipline that her enemies both prized and feared her greatly. When dealing with them, she never held parley unless she had a helmet on her head (2). And while traveling (3) she very rarely used a carriage. She often went on horseback, and sometimes on foot before the standards with the knights for three or four miles. And she did not find it bothersome at times to drink with her captains, although otherwise (4) she was sober; and she drank with the princes of Persia and Armenia, surpassing them in manners and pleasantness (5). <note>(3) A massive printing error exists here, reading "helmet on the head and with weapons; very rarely did she use a carriage," instead of "and while traveling very rarely," etc. The Latin text was enough to avoid this: <note>original: "et in expeditionibus vehiculo carpentario perrarissime utebatur"</note> "and on expeditions she used a carriage-vehicle very rarely."</note>
<note>(4) The consistent reading of the manuscripts shows that Albanzani <note>The translator of the Latin source into Italian</note> translated the word *alias* in the text as "at other times," when instead it means "otherwise." The text should read: "although she was otherwise sober."</note>
<note>(5) Regarding the phrase "she lived with the princes" found in all three manuscripts I know, I have preferred "she drank," which is absolutely required by the Latin: <note>original: "Nec fastidivit cum ducibus suis quandoque bibisse, cum esset alias sobria. Sic... cum Persis et Armenis principibus, ut illos urbanitate et facetia superaret"</note> "Nor did she disdain to have drunk at times with her generals, although she was otherwise sober. Thus [she drank] with the Persian and Armenian princes, so that she surpassed them in refined manners and wit."</note>

Regarding her treasures, none was more magnificent, nor more generous <note>original: "più largo." The editor notes that the Latin term *profusior* supports both the "more generous" reading of the Turin manuscripts and the "more splendid" reading of the Florentine manuscript.</note> (1), wherever she saw a need. This refers to the Egyptian language or its written characters.</note> (3). She also learned Greek letters under the philosopher Longinus, who was her master <note>The philosopher Cassius Longinus, a famous rhetorician and advisor to Zenobia.</note>. With the help of these, she examined all the Latin, Greek, and foreign histories with the greatest study, and committed them to memory <note>The editor corrects a printing error here; where the printed version read "not by sending them to memory," the manuscripts correctly read "committed them to memory."</note> (5). And not only this, but it was also believed that she abridged those histories into a brief form. In addition to her own native language, she knew the languages of Egypt and Syria; and she insisted that her sons speak Latin <note>This highlights her ambition to align her dynasty with the prestige of the Roman Empire.</note>. Why use more words? Certainly, this woman was of such great value that, after the emperors Gallienus, Aurelius, and Claudius were defeated, she drew against herself Aurelian, a man of perfect virtue. He, having been made emperor, sought to purge the shame brought upon the Roman reputation and to acquire great glory <note>original: "per purgare la vergogna della nominanza de' Romani." After the war of Marcus Mannicus <note>original: "Marco Mannico." This is likely a corruption in the text referring to the Marcomannic Wars or a specific Roman figure like Marcus Manlius.</note> was concluded and affairs in Rome were settled, Aurelian, with all diligence, began his march against Zenobia (1). Moving against the barbarian nations and having nobly defeated many legions, he finally arrived not far from the city of Emesa (2). Near this city, Zenobia—undaunted—had positioned herself with her army alongside Zeba <note>original: "Zeba." This refers to Zabdas, Zenobia’s chief military commander.</note>, whom she had taken as her companion in the war. In that place, a bitter and lengthy battle was fought between Aurelian and Zenobia for the ultimate outcome of the conflict (3). Finally, as Roman courage appeared victorious, Zenobia was put to flight along with her troops (4). She retreated to Palmyra, where she was immediately besieged by the victor. For some time (5), she refused to listen to any terms of surrender (6), defending herself with marvelous—

***

*...with Claudius Augustus removed and Aurelian, a man of unblemished virtue, having succeeded to the principate, he took it upon himself to atone for the disgrace of the Roman name and to achieve great glory.* <note>The editor provides this Latin snippet to clarify the transition between the reigns of Emperor Claudius Gothicus and Aurelian.</note>
(1) *In the printed edition, this passage is corrupted and incorrect to this point: "The war of Marco Mannico being finished, Aurelian being also quieted with every diligence, he took the path against Zenobia!!"*
(2) *Due to the disagreement between the manuscripts, it is impossible to find a better construction than this, which however is not the one in the original text: "And having excellently completed many things while going against the barbarian nations, he finally arrived with the legions not far from the city of Emesa."*
(3) *Due to excessive loyalty <note>The editor is criticizing a translation that is too literal to the Latin, making it clunky in the vernacular.</note>, the translation here is obscure: "In that place, between Aurelian and Zenobia, it was fought bitterly and long for the sum of things." The printed edition then makes it much worse by introducing a superfluous explanation: "And in that place was fought (correct: fought) bitterly and for a long space of the sum of the fact between Aurelian and Zenobia; put to flight with her own, that is Zenobia by Aurelian for the virtue of the Romans, etc."*
(4) *"Put to flight" according to the Florentine manuscript.*
(5) *"For space" <note>original: "Per spazio."</note> is an elliptical expression found in the Turin and Florentine manuscripts.*
(6) *"To give herself up" <note>original: "rendersi."</note> appears only in that manuscript. The others and the printed edition use "to surrender" <note>original: "arrendersi."</note>.*

I have chosen "carried" because the other reading redundantly repeats the fact of her departure from Palmyra.</note>, where, being pursued, she was captured with her children by Aurelian’s men and presented to him alive. ...which Zenobia had caused to be built <note>(1) The word "fabbricare" (to build) is found in this manuscript. The original Latin text says "Zenobia fabricari fecerat" (Zenobia had caused to be built). The printed edition and the Turin manuscripts use "fare" (to make).</note> of very great value, made of gold and gems <note>(2) This is a strong reading from the Florentine manuscript, which matches the Latin "auro et gemmis" (with gold and gems). The Turin manuscripts and the printed edition read "of gold and pearls," but pearls are mentioned specifically later in the passage.</note>, for she had hoped to come to Rome not as a prisoner, but as a victorious and triumphant woman to possess the Roman Empire. Before that chariot, she walked with her children, and she was bound by the neck, the hands, and the feet with chains of gold, wearing a crown and royal garments, weighed down with pearls <note>(3) This reading is preferred over the printed edition’s "royal garments loaded with pearls," because the Latin text reads "vestimentis regiis, ac margaritis et lapidibus praeciosis onusta" (burdened with royal garments, and with pearls and precious stones).</note> and precious stones; so much so that, although she was a very strong woman, she often came to a standstill <note>(5) "Stood still" (stava ferma) accurately reflects the Latin "subsisteret" found in the text, the Turin manuscripts, and the printed edition; it is a better reading than "remained immobile" found in the Florentine manuscript.</note> because of the weight <note>(4) Grammarians should note that the Florentine manuscript—which is superior to the others in the purity of its language—always uses the form "per il" rather than "per lo."</note>. 

And when the triumph—which was marvelous for its treasure and for the virtue of Aurelian—was finished, it is said that she grew old with her children in the dress of a private citizen among the Roman women. An estate was granted to her by the Senate near Tivoli, which for a long time afterward was named "Zenobia" after her; it was located not far from the palace of the divine Hadrian <note>The Emperor Hadrian's famous villa at Tivoli.</note>, in that place which was called Conche by the local inhabitants.

***

(1) *Fabbricare* [to build] in this manuscript. *Zenobia fabricari fecerat* [Zenobia had caused to be built] in the Latin text. *Fare* [to make] in the printed edition and the Turin manuscripts.
(2) A good reading from the Florentine manuscript, corresponding to the Latin *auro et gemmis* [gold and gems]. *D' oro e di perle* [Of gold and pearls] (the pearls are mentioned later) in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.
(3) And not, as in the printed edition, *vestimenti reali carichi di perle* [royal garments loaded with pearls], because the text has, *vestimentis regiis, ac margaritis et lapidibus praeciosis onusta* [burdened with royal garments, and with pearls and precious stones].
(4) Let grammarians note that the Florentine manuscript, which is better than the others regarding the purity of the language, always uses *per il* and not *per lo*.
(5) *Stava ferma* [she stood still], which is the *subsisteret* of the text, the Turin manuscripts, and the printed edition, is correct, rather than the *stava immobile* [she remained immobile] of the Florentine manuscript.

# CHAPTER LXXXVIII.

## Pope Joan.

John, although appearing to be a man in name, was nonetheless in fact <note>(1) The word "effetto" (fact/effect) from the Florentine manuscript is preferred over "al fatto" (in deed) found in other manuscripts and the printed edition.</note> a woman: whose unheard-of presumption made her known to the whole world, and she is known to posterity <note>(2) This reading is found in all the manuscripts, though it does not perfectly translate the original Latin: *cuius inaudita temeritas, ut orbi toto notissima fieret, et in posterum nosceretur effecit* (whose unheard-of audacity brought it about that she became very well known to the whole world and was recognized by posterity).</note>. And although it is said by some that she was from Mainz <note>original: "Magonza"</note>, it is hardly <note>(3) "A fatica" (hardly) is used in the Florentine manuscript; others and the printed edition use "a pena" or "appena" (scarcely). The Turin manuscripts use "Magonza," while the Florentine uses "Magonto."</note> known what her proper name was, notwithstanding that <note>(4) The two Turin manuscripts use the phrase "avvenga dio che" (it happened by God that).</note> some say she had the name Gilberta. And this is manifest: that she was loved by a young scholar in her youth, whom they say she loved so much that, having cast aside <note>(5) Reading from the Florentine manuscript; the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition read "putting aside the fear and shame of a young woman."</note> the shame and fear of a woman, she fled secretly from her father’s house to her lover; and, having changed her name and wearing the clothes of a boy, she followed him. Studying alongside him in England <note>The text reads "Anglia" (England), though many versions of this legend, including Boccaccio's original Latin, specify "Athenis" (Athens) as the location of her studies.</note>, everyone thought she was a cleric <note>(6) The Turin manuscripts and the printed edition use "chierico" (cleric), matching the Latin *clericus existimatus*. The Florentine manuscript uses "chiericuccio" (little cleric).</note>, where she belonged both to the militia of Venus <note>A metaphorical reference to romantic or sexual pursuits.</note> and to her studies <note>(7) The scribe of the Florentine manuscript, though writing in the 15th century, ignores the grammatical rule regarding the "impure s" (a phonetic rule in Italian), writing "del studio" here and "il studio" further down rather than the standard "dello" and "lo."</note>. Then, the young man having died, she knowing...
