<meta>This page continues the story of the legendary Pope Joan, following her departure from her home to pursue studies in England alongside her lover while disguised as a man.</meta>

->— 354 —<-

...having found herself to possess a good intellect, and being drawn by the sweetness of knowledge, she maintained her disguise and did not wish to reveal herself to others nor manifest that she was a woman. Instead, by attending diligently to her studies <note>(1) This reading from the Turin manuscripts is preferred over the word "persisting" in the printed edition or "frequently attending" in the Florentine manuscript.</note>, she made such progress in the liberal arts <term>liberal arts|The 'artes liberales' were the core subjects of medieval education, including grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy.</term> that she was held to be excellent above all others. 

And so, adorned with wondrous knowledge and now of mature age, she departed from England <note>original: "Anglia"</note> and went to Rome. There, teaching the primary sciences <note>(2) By the phrase "reading the primitive sciences for some years," the translator Albanzani has rendered the original Latin "teaching the trivium." These were the fundamental studies, which perhaps held that name because they were sometimes taught publicly and in the open air.</note> for several years, she had a marvelous following of listeners. Because she possessed singular honesty and holiness in addition to her great knowledge, everyone believed she was a man. 

For this reason, being well known to many, upon the death of Pope Leo V, she was elected Pope in place of the deceased Leo <note>(3) The Florentine manuscript reads: "In place of the one who had died"; the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition use the phrasing translated here.</note> by the common consent of all the venerable cardinals, and she was called John. It might not have even happened that she was elected if she had been a man <note>(4) This sentence is missing from the printed edition. Since the three manuscripts known to me agree on this reading, it must be said that the translator Albanzani had a source text quite different from the printed version, which reads: "if he had been a man, it would have happened that he was the eighth of that name [John VIII]."</note>. 

This woman, not fearing to ascend <note>(5) "To ascend" (salire) in the Florentine manuscript; "To mount" (montare) in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.</note> the Chair of the Fisherman <note>original: "sedia del Pescatore"; a title for the Papal throne, referring to St. Peter’s original profession.</note>, nor to perform all the sacred mysteries <note>(6) "Ministries" in manuscript A; "Mysteries" in the original Latin text.</note> and administer them to others—acts not permitted to any woman by the Christian religion—held the high office of the papacy for several years. A woman, therefore, held on earth the Vi— <note>The text cuts off here, likely intending to say "Vicar of Christ."</note>

<summary>This page describes how the disguised Joan excelled in the liberal arts and moved to Rome to teach, eventually being elected Pope as John after the death of Leo V. The text emphasizes her scholarly success and the unprecedented nature of a woman occupying the Papal throne.</summary>

...vicariate <term>vicariate|The office or jurisdiction of a vicar; in this context, the Pope acting as the earthly representative of Christ.</term> of Christ. And, God from heaven having (1) mercy on his people, he did not allow (2) that a woman should hold so marvelous a place, nor that she should rule over so many people (3), nor that they should be deceived by such a wicked error (4). For which reason, as the devil—who had led her there and held her in that villainous audacity—comforted her, she who in private life had maintained singular honesty (5), having ascended to such a high pontificate, rose into the ardor of lust (6). And she, who for a long space of time had known how to hide that she was a woman, found ways to fulfill her lust (7). For having found one who might lie with (8) the successor of St. Peter, and one who might scratch the

---

(1) *Reading from Codex B: "And the celestial God having mercy on his people," the Florentine manuscript. "And he (that is Christ, who immediately precedes) having mercy etc."* <note>The translator is comparing different surviving handwritten versions of this story to find the most accurate wording.</note>

(2) *"Comportò" (allowed/suffered) from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition; the Florentine manuscript uses "non sostenne" (did not sustain).*

(3) *"Soprastessi" (using a Florentine dialectal form) "to so many people" in the Florentine manuscript. "To so great a people," or "to so large a people," in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.*

(4) *From the two aforementioned sources, and this is the "unfortunate error" <note>original: "infaustus error"</note> of the Latin text. "Worst error" in the Florentine manuscript.*

(5) *The phrase "as she who in private life had preserved singular honesty" <note>original Latin: "ut quae privata praecipuam honestatem servaverat"</note> seems to me better rendered by the word "singular" from the Florentine manuscript than by the word "special" from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.*

(6) *"Rose into the ardor of lust" from Codex B is more faithful to the original Latin "she came into the ardor of lust" <note>original Latin: "in ardorem devenit libidinis"</note> than "returned to the ardor of lust" found in Codex A and the Florentine manuscript. Even worse is "fell into lust" from the main text.*

(7) *The printed edition lacks the word "Arts" (tricks/methods); it uses "art" in the singular, which is found only in the Florentine manuscript.*

(8) *"Subponessi" (lie with/place under) is from the Florentine manuscript, and, though very clear, it is less graphic than "might mount" in Codex B or "the successor" in Codex A, suggested by the Latin text "should mount the successor" <note>original Latin: "successorem conscenderet"</note>. This is missing from the printed edition.*

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the legendary Pope Joan's downfall. ->356<-

...burning itch, it happened that the Pope became pregnant (1). Oh, how unworthy a sin this was, and how marvelous the patience of God (2)! But what happened in the end (3)? Ingenuity failed to hide the adulterous birth for her who had for so long been able to deceive the eyes of men. Because the time being closer than she believed, while she was going to perform the annual sacrifice from the Janiculum <note>A hill in western Rome, though not one of the traditional Seven Hills.</note> (4) to the Lateran, between the Colosseum and the church of Pope Clement, since there was no woman present to serve as a midwife, she fell prostrate in the street (5) and gave birth publicly. And she revealed with what deceit she had fooled all men, except for her lover. Wherefore (6) she was cast into prison, wretched woman, with her offspring, where she ended her life. (1) *This filthiness is also from the text: "...and he might rub her burning itch" <note>original: "et exurentem pruriginem defricaret"</note>, it came to pass that because of this she conceived. "Ingravidò" (became pregnant) is from the Turin manuscripts; "s’impregnò" (became impregnated) is from the Florentine one. Fortunately, this story—which in Boccaccio’s day was held as history (and he did not feel it necessary to narrate it differently, especially since he shows reverence for the highest keys <term>highest keys|The keys of Saint Peter, the symbol of papal authority and the power to bind and loose on earth and in heaven.</term>)—is today proven to be a fable.*

(4) *Janiculum is from the manuscripts known to me, and corresponds to "Janiculus" in the original text. I do not know where the printed reading "annual sacrifice at the church of Saint Nicholas" comes from, unless "Janiculum" was misread as "Saint Nicholas"!!!*

(5) *"Prostrate" <note>Meaning fallen face-down or collapsed.</note> is from Manuscript B; "stopped" from Manuscript A and the Florentine one. "Ready in the street" in the previous edition is an error.*

### CHAPTER LII.

## **Irene the Athenian.**

Irene was a most noble woman of Athens, and was famous for her singular beauty. The Emperor Constantine, having had her brought from her home to Constantinople, gave her as a wife to his son Leo. And, after the death of Constantine, having become empress, she conceived by her husband and gave birth to a son named Constantine. Finally, after Leo died, she nobly governed (4) the empire with her son Constantine, who was then very young, for the space of ten years. But, he having already grown up a bit, and asserting that the lordship belonged to him...

---

(1) "He turns," which is found in the Turin manuscripts, or "they turn" from the printed edition, which translates the original text's *declinant* <note>Latin: "they turn aside" or "deviate."</note>, are to be preferred over "they leave the way" found in the Florentine manuscript.
(2) The aforementioned manuscript uses the phrase "they go by another road," which is a very poor reading. All the other versions use "paths." "Androni" <note>An Italian term for a passage, hallway, or alleyway.</note> is found only in manuscript A. The two Latin words *diverticula, vicosque* <note>Latin: "byways and streets."</note> suggest that Albanzani <note>Donato degli Albanzani (c. 1328–1411), a scholar and friend of Boccaccio who translated this work into the Italian vernacular.</note> added another word to "paths." I would find "androni" pleasing, were it not that—similar to "antroni" from "antro" <note>Italian: "cave" or "cavern."</note>—it is a word that indicates a covered and dark place.
(3) Father Tosti, in a note spanning six pages (from pages 311–317), refutes the fable of Pope Joan. It was not necessary <note>Luigi Tosti (1811–1897) was a Benedictine historian; the editor here implies the myth is so widely recognized as fiction that a lengthy academic rebuttal is overkill.</note>.
(4) "Nobly governed" is a good reading from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition, which well renders the original phrase *imperio praesedit* <note>Latin: "she presided over the empire."</note>. The Florentine manuscript reads, "nobly governed the empire."

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Byzantine Empress Irene of Athens, detailing her power struggle with her son, Constantine VI, and her eventual (though temporary) removal from power.</meta>

<header>— 358 —</header>

to him alone, he removed her from his company, according to what some say, for a period of eight years. Then that woman, of great spirit and eager to rule, having come into conflict with her son, with a woman’s cunning (1), seized her son—who had trusted in his own power—and having taken the lordship from him, held him prisoner. And she alone ascended (2) the throne—from which, in former times, the laws had been given to the entire world—and, as an empress more famous than other men, she ruled for five years with great glory. 

But, through the efforts of Constantine’s friends, it happened that with the help of the Armenians, Irene was removed from power; and Constantine, drawn from prison, was restored to his father's throne. He, being more merciful toward his mother than she had been toward him, and having greater hope in the power of his friends, did not put her (3) in prison, but was content to separate her from him. He placed her in the Palace of Eleutherius—which she herself had caused to be built with a great abundance of luxury—and he sent all of her friends into exile (4). ---

(1) In the printed edition, there is a gap <note>lacuna</note> between one mention of "son" and the other, where the words *with a woman’s cunning seized her son* are missing.

(2) The text, according to the Bernese printed edition, reads: *Solumque a quo universus orbis olim iura susceperat sola conscendit* <note>Latin: "And she alone ascended the soil from which the entire world once received laws."</note>; but it is evident that *solum* <note>soil/ground</note> is an error in place of *solium* <note>throne/seat</note>, as one never writes "to ascend the soil." Thus, the reading of the printed edition is rejected, which says: *and she only to the earth, from which before all the world had taken laws*, where, moreover, the verb is missing and it makes no sense. *Ascended* <note>Italian: "Salì"</note> from the Florentine manuscript, which renders the Latin *conscendit* <note>he/she ascended</note>, is to be preferred over *held* <note>Italian: "teneva"</note> from the Turin manuscripts.

(3) *Put* <note>Italian: "Mise"</note> from the aforementioned manuscripts; *placed* <note>Italian: "puose"</note> from the Florentine manuscript.

(4) The Latin, *amicis eius omnibus relegatis exilio* <note>Latin: "all her friends having been relegated to exile"</note> is rendered backwards in the printed edition, saying that he called all of her enemies back from exile.

### CHAPTER C.

## **Engeldruda, a Florentine maiden (6).** <note>Although this chapter is often titled "The Good Gualdrada," the translator has not changed the name found in the manuscripts: *Enguldrada* in Codex A, *Engualdrada* in Codex B, and *Engeldruda* in the Monte Cassino manuscript (matching the original Latin). This consistency suggests a theory that Boccaccio may have written *Eugualdrada*, using the Greek prefix *eu* (meaning "good") to mean "the Good Gualdrada" in a single word. As soon as the girl heard these words, she was immediately indignant, taking it poorly <margin>with annoyance</margin> that her father showed such a light opinion of her steadfastness and of the guarding of her virginal modesty <note>original: "virginei pudoris custodia"</note>. —and who would there be, however merciful they might be, who could forgive the Neapolitan editor for having placed here in his stead an *Illizione*, who has no other fatherhood except the false one given to him by the editor of the highly incorrect Bernese printed edition? <note>The author is criticizing a previous editor for misspelling the name of the nobleman Bellincione as "Illizione."</note>

bowed down (1), she spoke surely with humble words: "My father, be silent and do not speak; for, unless you force me, no one—except (2) the one whom you shall give to me as a husband—shall have that which you offer so generously (3)." And truly, that which is said well and at length (5) should not fall from the mind (4) of a great man. The emperor stood for a while in admiration; but then—German harshness not standing in the way (6), and although he did not yet know that girl (7)—he gathered (8) from her words the holy and chaste purpose within that virgin’s breast. And having praised the indignation and the speech of that girl with many

---

(1) Also the word *bowed* <note>original: "chinati"</note> from this manuscript is to be preferred over the *lowered* <note>original: "abbassati"</note> found in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition. <note>The OCR "nati" is a fragment of "inchinati" or "chinati," meaning to bow or bend down.</note>

(2) *Or except for that one* <note>original: "O salvo quello"</note> from the said printed edition and the Turin manuscripts.

(3) *Generously* <note>original: "Ampiamente"</note> from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition renders the Latin *profuse* with a better Italian word than the *broadly* <note>original: "latamente"</note> used in the Florentine manuscript.

(4) And *not in the mind* in the Florentine manuscript.

(5) The [Latin] text: *O good God, nowhere is it permitted that what has been said well and elegantly from the soul should fall from a man of great spirit.* <note>original: "O deus bone, nusquam quod ex animo bene eleganterque dictum est, ab ingentis animi viro cecidisse permissum est"</note>. The Cassinese manuscript ends with: *that which is said should not fall from the mind of a great man.* Manuscript A and the Florentine one use *at length* <note>original: "lungamente"</note>, which shows that the word *elegantly* <note>original: "eleganter"</note> was not in the text used by Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani was a 14th-century translator and friend of Boccaccio and Petrarch</note>.

(6) "Much better," writes Mr. Hortis (Studies on the Latin works of Boccaccio pp. 104 and 105), "than in the *Book of Famous Women*, Boccaccio tells the graceful story (of the good Gualdrada) in his Commentary on Dante." It is certain that there we do not find the parenthetical phrase, *German barbarism not resisting* <note>original: "germanica non obsistente barbarie"</note>, which Albanzani softened when translating into the vernacular as *German harshness* <note>original: "asprezza tedesca"</note>. And truly, it did not belong there, for in the words and the deeds that followed them, the Emperor Otto showed himself here to be anything but a barbarian.

(7) The manuscripts known to me have *already knowing that girl* <note>original: "conoscendo già quella fanciulla"</note>, and the adverb gives a hint of the true reading. But here the sense requires the negative [not yet knowing].

(8) And *did not judge* in the printed edition, while the *gathered* <note>original: "ricolse"</note> of the manuscripts is confirmed by the Latin *he gathered from the words* <note>original: "collegit ex verbis"</note> of the text.

words, he had a noble young man named Guido (1) called for. And so that the girl would not lack someone she could honorably kiss, if she so wished (2), in the presence of her father—who offered his thanks for the match—the Emperor gave Engualdrada <note>A variation of the name Gualdrada.</note> as a wife to him before he departed. He provided her with a noble dowry, considering that what the girl had said did not merely reside in her private thoughts, but had proceeded, by the force of righteous indignation, from a great cultivation of virtue; and for that reason, she was most worthy of the emperor's gift. 

Thus, she who had entered the temple as a virgin, through the integrity of her honor, returned to her father's house as a married woman, to the great joy of her father and her kin. And in the course of time, having given birth to many children, upon her death she left her husband's house adorned and famous with a noble lineage; and the multitude of her descendants continues to this day. (1) From the Florentine manuscript and the printed edition, *by name Guido*; but not *called by name* as in the Turin manuscripts, in which one of the two forms is redundant.

(2) Since Boccaccio put into the mouth of Bellincione the words: *if I command it she will kiss you* (and in the [Latin] text: "so that she may kiss you if I command it" <note>original: "ut te deosculetur si iussero"</note>), logically there should follow here *so that the girl would not lack someone she could honorably kiss* (the Latin: "lest the virgin should long be without one to whom she could offer an honorable kiss if she wished" <note>original: "ne diu careret virgo, cui posset honestum si vellet exhibere osculum"</note>). Instead, in his commentary on Dante, the same Boccaccio makes Bellincione say, *I would have the heart to make her kiss you, if it pleased you*, and shortly after Gualdrada says, *unless force is used against me, no one will ever kiss me, except for the one you give me as a husband*. Since the reading of the Florentine manuscript—that *she* <note>original: "lei"</note> *might kiss him honorably*—induced ambiguity, as *lei* [her] must be understood here as *ella* [she], I have chosen the reading of the other manuscripts.

## CHAPTER CI.

### Constance, Queen of Sicily.

Constance, the Roman Empress, was famous on earth from the highest peak of the world (5). But because sharing honor with many seems to have diminished the admiration of those who look at this (6), one must sea—

***

(1) Boccaccio called the girls of his time *modern* <note>original: "modernae"</note>; the habit of blaming "modern" people and things and praising the "ancient" has been and will be a feature of all times.

(2) <note>original: "Quarum tanta animi levitas est, et effrenati sunt mores"; translating to: "Whose levity of mind is so great, and whose morals are unrestrained."</note> It is fortunate that he said this here in Latin, for in the vernacular <note>The common Italian language of the people.</note> it is well known how he taught them to be strong and well-mannered.

(3) The usual "bowing" <note>Italian: "chinarsi"</note> found in the Florentine manuscript is not appropriate here, and even the "bending" <note>Italian: "piegarsi"</note> found in other versions is not enough to capture the sense of a total fall—or rather a precipice—intended by the text: <note>original: "ut oculis, gestibusque irruere in quorumcunque intuentium videantur amplexus"; translating to: "so that by their eyes and gestures they seem to rush into the embraces of anyone who looks at them."</note>

(4) "Gazes" <note>Italian: "guata"</note> appears only in the Florentine manuscript. "Looks" <note>Italian: "guarda"</note>, found in other versions, is too repetitive given the proximity of the word "glances" <note>Italian: "guardi"</note>.

(5) This is a convoluted translation of the straightforward Latin: <note>original: "Constantia, e summo orbis cardine, terris romanorum imperatrix effulsit"; translating to: "Constance, from the highest pole of the world, shone forth upon the lands as Empress of the Romans."</note>

(6) This does not match the printed version: *but because she shared common honor with many, she seems to have diminished the admiration of those who look*: those who wish to stand out in our age must seek another cause for excellence; all the more so since the note includes part of the text which confirms the reading of the manuscripts: <note>original: "Verum quoniam iam multis comune decus admirationem intuentium minuisse videtur, alia claritatis causa, nostro aevo apparere volentibus, quaerenda est"; translating to: "But because a common glory shared with many now seems to have diminished the admiration of onlookers, another cause for fame must be sought by those wishing to appear distinguished in our age."</note>

<summary>The text concludes a moralizing comparison between the virtuous Gualdrada and 'modern' women before introducing the biography of Empress Constance of Sicily, noting the difficulty of achieving unique fame when sharing titles with many others.</summary>

...seek another reason for fame for those who wish to be seen in our age. This was not lacking for her. And certainly, if it was not granted to her for any other merit, she became famous at least for her only son. She was the daughter of William, formerly the most excellent King of Sicily. At her birth, according to what many say, a certain Joachim, a Calabrian abbot <note>Joachim of Fiore (c. 1135–1202) was a mystic and theologian famous for his prophetic visions of history.</note> endowed with a prophetic spirit, told William that his daughter would henceforth be the destruction of the Kingdom of Sicily. Frightened and marveled by this omen, and believing it, the King began to think with anxiety how such a thing could happen through a woman. Seeing no way it could happen except through a husband or a son, and having compassion for his kingdom, he decided (1) if he could take this (2) away by his own foresight. And so that he might remove (3) the hope of marriage and children, he shut that young maiden in a convent of nuns and made her promise perpetual virginity to God. And his foresight (5) was not to be disparaged (4), if it had been of any use. But because we, foolish and weak, set our own strength against God—He who justly purges the wicked deeds of men—we certainly deceive ourselves...

***

(1) *Decided* <note>Italian: "Deliberò"</note> is found in the <term>Turin manuscripts</term>, and it corresponds well to the word *decided* <note>Latin: "statuit"</note> in the original text; the <term>printed edition</term> says *determined*, and the <term>Florentine version</term> says *concluded*.
(2) *This* <note>Italian: "Questo"</note> renders the Latin *this* <note>Latin: "istud"</note> from the text; it is a good reading from the <term>manuscripts</term> known to me, and it corrects the <term>printed edition</term> which says, *determined . . . . to take her away*, which would imply Constance herself.
(3) *Remove* <note>Italian: "Levasse"</note> is from the <term>Florentine manuscript</term>. *Take away* is used in the other manuscripts and the <term>printed edition</term>, but it repeats the phrase *take away* from the previous line too closely.
(4) *Disparage* <note>Italian: "Spregiare"</note> is found only in the <term>Florentine manuscript</term>.
(5) *His opinion* in <term>Manuscript B</term>. *Foresight* <note>Italian: "provisione"</note> is better, because it was an opinion put into action, and Master Giovanni <note>Giovanni Boccaccio, the author of this work.</note> would have approved of it, provided it had actually been helpful!

...we are deceived by the slightest blow! <note>The translator Albanzani likely used the word "blow" (Italian: *percossa*) to translate the Latin *impulsu* (impulse or push). ...might oppose, and even seemed to oppose. The Latin text reads: *annosa etiam videretur aetas obsistere* <note>original: "even though her advanced age seemed to stand in the way"</note>.

## CHAPTER 102.

### Camiola of Siena (1).

Camiola, a widow famous for the beauty of her body, her character, her courtesy, and her laudable virtue, was Sienese by birth and the daughter of Lorenzo di Torringo (2), a man of the military order. She led her life near Messina, a city in Sicily, with only one husband, and with her father and mother, no less (3) commendably than nobly. And, while Frederick III was king of the island—and that same man being emperor <note>Boccaccio appears to conflate Frederick II of Hohenstaufen, who was Emperor, with Frederick III of Sicily; the narrative follows Boccaccio's historical understanding</note>—and upon the death of her kin, she remained the heir to almost royal riches. While she preserved her virtue, and the aforementioned Frederick having already died and being succeeded by Peter his son, it happened that, by the King's command, a great armada was being prepared in Messina...

<note>(1) In some of the preceding chapters, and specifically in those concerning *Pope Joan* and *Constance, Queen of Sicily*, Boccaccio has told stories following traditions created by the common people, or increased by the common people with the passing years. Here instead he acts as a historian, and seemingly a faithful one, perhaps because the events he narrates were contemporary to him, or nearly so. It is certain that the *Intronati* [an academic society in Siena], authors of the *Sienese Annals* <note>original: "Fasti Senenses"</note>, at the end of the life of Camiola, in the second edition *enlarged and corrected according to the original manuscript* <note>original: "auctior et ad autographi fidem emendata"</note>, cite (p. 194) Boccaccio’s narrative as the primary source from which they drew. Manuscript B always uses *Camiuola*, both a diminutive and an affectionate form of *Camilla*.</note>

<note>(2) *Torigo* in Manuscript A and the Florentine edition, *Terico* in B, *Toringo* in the printed version and the Bern edition. I consider *Torringo* to be more correct, following the Dominican Fazello, *Concerning Sicilian Affairs* <note>original: "De rebus siculis"</note>. Decade 2, Book 9, Chapter 4, p. 528 of the first and rare edition which is among my books.</note>

<note>(3) *No less laudably than noble* <note>original: "Non meno lodevolmente che nobile"</note>, omitting the adverbial ending in the Florentine manuscript. The printed edition reads: *nevertheless laudably than nobly* <note>original: "nondimeno lodevolmente che nobilmente"</note>.</note>

The text transitions into a historical account of her life during the reign of King Peter of Sicily, distinguishing this chapter from Boccaccio's more legendary tales.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the military context established at the end of the previous page, detailing the naval expedition from Messina to relieve the siege of Lipari. -> 372 <-

...fleet, under the leadership of Giovanni, Count of Chiaramonte, at that time a man of the greatest prowess (1), to bring relief to the people of Lipari who were besieged and reduced to extremity by hunger. On this fleet, not only did knights hired for wages embark, but many nobles joined voluntarily—both those from the coast and those from inland (2)—to acquire the glory of arms. And Gofredo of Squillace (3), a valiant man, at that time the admiral <note>original: "prefetto di mare"</note> of Robert, King of Sicily and Jerusalem, had besieged that land. He had so weakened those within the town through the siege, with mangonels <term>mangano: a heavy medieval siege engine used for hurling stones or projectiles</term> (4) and other structures for combat, and through frequent battles, that it was hoped they would soon surrender (5). Having learned through spies (6) on certain boats that the enemy fleet was much larger than his own, he gathered his ships together and began to wait for the outcome of fortune. But the enemies, quickly seizing the abandoned positions without anyone hindering them, brought aid to those...

(1) The superlative in the Latin text, *a man most warlike in that time* <note>original: "ea tempestate bellicosissimi hominis"</note>, accounts for both the *greatest prowess* found in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition, as well as the *utmost prowess* of the Florentine manuscript.
(2) To the Latin *both coastal and inland* <note>original: "tam littorani, quam mediterranei"</note>, the more effective phrase *both those from the sea and those from the interior* of the Florentine manuscript responds better than the diluted *both those of the coast and those who lived in the interior* of the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.
(3) The name is not *Squilatio* as in the Florentine manuscript, and even worse, *Squillazzo* in the printed edition, which both take the *De Squilatio* of the Latin text literally.
(4) The equivalent for <term>mangano</term>, which in the 1300s was also used to mean a military machine, should be <term>ballista</term> in the Latin text; but in that text, this word or any other that could be so interpreted is missing.
(5) *Surrender* <note>original: "arrendesse"</note> in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition; *give up* <note>original: "rendesse"</note> in the Florentine manuscript.
(6) *By spies* <note>original: "per ispie"</note> in the printed edition; *by the spies* <note>original: "per le spie"</note> in the Turin and Florentine manuscripts.

of the land, which they had brought (1). Because of this success, Giovanni became arrogant and challenged Gofredo to battle. That man [Gofredo], being of a most ardent spirit, did not refuse; having reinforced his fleet during the night with towers and wooden bulkheads (2), and having arranged the ships and other equipment, at the break of dawn he encouraged his men to battle with fiery words. And already, losing faith in the outcome (5), having much raf-

---

(1) This construction, found in the manuscripts, is somewhat obscure. The original Latin text clarifies it: *they brought the aid they were carrying to the townspeople* <note>original Latin: "quae ferebant oppidanis intulere subsidia"</note>, or in the common tongue: *and the relief that they had brought with them, they brought to those of the land.*

(2) The agreement between the manuscripts shows that Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani, a 14th-century humanist and translator</note> translated the Latin in this way: *when at night he had reinforced the fleet with planking and towers* <note>original Latin: "cum nocte tabulatis ac turribus roborasset classem"</note>.

(3) The Latin text—*Giovanni, who did not intend for Gofredo to take up battle, nor even to look upon the mass of the Sicilian ships, had composed his ships not for conflict, but for pursuing those in flight* <note>original Latin: "Joannes cui non erat animus Gotfridum sumpturum, sed nec spectaturum Siculorum navigiorum molem, non in certamen, sed ad insequendas fugentium, suas composuerat naues"</note>—would require a different translation than that of Albanzani. Albanzani's version is further complicated by disagreements between the manuscripts: the Turin and Casinate versions read *wait for the power of the ships*, while the Florentine reads *look upon the force of the ships*. Rather than resorting to arbitrary changes in such an uncertain passage, it is better to leave it to the wise and discreet judgment of the reader.

(4) Only the Florentine manuscript renders the *feared* <note>original Latin: "timuit"</note> of the text: *nearly destitute of spirit, he feared, and repented, etc.*

(5) *Distrusting* <note>original Italian: "sfidandosi"</note> is found in manuscripts B and the Florentine, while *doubting* <note>original Italian: "diffidandosi"</note> is in manuscript A.

<meta>This page continues the description of the naval engagement between Gofredo of Squillace and the Sicilian fleet led by Giovanni of Chiaramonte. On the previous page, Gofredo had prepared his ships for a surprise battle, catching the overconfident Giovanni off guard.</meta>

— 374 —

<meta>catchword: escap-</meta>

his spirit having cooled, so that he would not seem entirely (1) cowardly, he immediately reordered (2) his line of battle; and in what space was granted to him, he gave the signal for battle (3). Already they were near the enemies, who, having raised a clamor, had mixed (4) their prows with the Sicilian fleet, which was approaching slowly. Once the battle began, having thrown iron grappling hooks during the first assault along with spears and arrows—the Sicilians standing idle and almost bewildered by the sudden change—Gofredo’s men moved forward and, charging of their own accord, presented themselves at the enemies' ships; they began to fight with hands and swords and to bathe everything in blood (5). The Sicilians, already losing hope (6), turning their prows (7), those who were able turned their backs and fled. And it being clear that Gofredo’s men were the victors, they sank (8) many of the Sicilian ships, and many were captured; a few others, being lighter to row (9) by the strength of the galley-slaves (10), escap-

---

(1) From the Florentine manuscript; "entirely" <note>original Italian: "postutto"</note> in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.
(2) Here this is a passive participle; the original Latin text reads "the order having been turned" <note>original Latin: "revoluto ordine"</note>. The printed edition says "He turned."
(3) "Or of the battle" in the Turin manuscripts.
(4) "Mixed" <note>original Italian: "meschiate"</note> is found only in the Florentine manuscript. The others use "mingled" <note>original Italian: "mescolate"</note>.
(5) The printed edition begins the sentence with "having thrown the grappling hooks."
(6) The Piedmontese <note>Turin</note> manuscripts, which just before have "challenging" <note>original Italian: "sfidandosi"</note>, here use "despairing" <note>original Italian: "diffidandosi"</note>; and the Florentine, which previously had "despairing," here has "challenging." These variations, occurring so close together, show the interchangeable use made of those words, which—if one knows how to use them—is a richness.
(7) The words "prow" <note>original Italian: "proda"</note> and "bow" <note>original Italian: "prora"</note> are also used interchangeably in the manuscripts, except in the Florentine, which has "prow" <note>original Italian: "proda"</note>.
(8) And not "they sank themselves" <note>original Italian: "affondaronsi"</note> as in the printed edition, nor "they caught fire" <note>original Italian: "affocoronsi"</note> as in the Florentine manuscript. The original text reads: *Very many of the Sicilian ships were sunk* <note>original Latin: "Plurimae sunt Siculorum demersae naves"</note>.
(9) This is missing from the printed edition, and is a most beautiful phrase that elegantly renders Boccaccio’s Latin: "easy to row" <note>original Latin: "remigio faciles"</note>.
(10) Also missing there is "by the strength of the galley-slaves," which is the Latin: "by the strength of the rowers" <note>original Latin: "remigantium virtute"</note>.

escaped safely. And in that battle few died, but many were wounded there. Giovanni, the commander of the fleet <note>Giovanni Chiaramonte, a Sicilian nobleman</note>, was captured, and with him almost all the nobles who had voluntarily joined the fleet; many of the knights and galley-slaves were also taken, along with many banners belonging to the knights and the ships, including the great royal standard which was carried upon the admiral's ship. And after the land had come to a point of surrender, following a long wandering (1) pursued by the misfortunes of the sea, they were led to Naples in chains and placed (2) in prison. Among these men was a youth named Orlando, the natural son (3) of the king <note>Referring to Orlando of Aragon, the illegitimate son of King Frederick II of Sicily</note>, handsome of body and valiant. While the ransom (4) was being sought for all the other prisoners, he alone was held back; as all the others departed, he remained sorrowfully in prison (5). This was because King Peter, whom his brother's deeds concerned (6), held both Orlando and the others who had been in that naval battle in hatred, because the affair had been poorly managed and carried out against his command. Therefore, while he was a prisoner and almost without hope of liberty, languishing in irons (7), it happened that he came into the memory of Camiola, who,

<meta>This page continues the story of Orlando of Aragon, who was left to rot in a Neapolitan prison after a naval defeat. seeing that man despised (1) by his brothers, she felt compassion for his misfortune (2), and deliberated with herself (3) whether she could honorably bring him back to liberty. And seeing no other way (4) to save the honor of her reputation, unless she took him as her husband (5), she secretly sent someone to inquire if he would be willing to leave prison in that manner. This was easily agreed upon. And so, having observed every legal formality, he married her as his wife through a proxy <note>A legal representative who stands in for a person during a ceremony, a common practice for high-ranking individuals at the time.</note>, with his consent (6), and under the pledge of the ring. And, without delay, two thousand ounces of silver having been sent by Camiola and the payment made, Orlando returned free from prison to Messina. But he did not go to the bride's house (7); he acted as if not a single word had ever been spoken of the matter. driven by anger more than reason, she civilly sent word to him to complete the marriage (1). He, having denied that he had any such business (2) with her, was summoned (3) by her before the ecclesiastical judge; with authentic documents and the testimony of honorable men, she proved that he was her husband. Once he had confessed this in shame, and once the lady’s kindness toward him became known, having been rebuked by his brothers and urged by his friends, he was persuaded (4) to agree to the lady’s demand and requested that the marriage be finalized. reason I have reproved your lie (1), which is a great offense to me, regarding you and your marriage (2). I think that you believed, while you were still in prison, that I had forgotten (3) myself and my condition, and that I foolishly desired (4) a husband of royal lineage <term>schiatta reale: noble or royal ancestry</term>, and that I craved (5) your beauty with feminine passion. And as much as you were able, you strove toward that end (8). to mock <note>original: *schernire*</note> from the Florentine manuscript, which is stronger. But "of the fact" is more properly a mockery <note>original: *scherno*</note> than a trick <note>original: *beffa*</note>.

(1) In the reading from the Florentine manuscript, *falseness and lie*, the first word is an intrusion.

(2) This is literally, "the lie which to me concerning you and your marriage is very great" <note>original: *mendacium quod mihi de te deque tuo coniugio permaximum est*</note>, from the text.

(3) "Forgotten" <note>original: *dimenticato*</note>, found only in Manuscript B.

(9) This is better than the "vile things" <note>original: *infime cose*</note> found only in the Florentine manuscript. <meta>This page continues the speech of Camiola Turinga, a Sicilian noblewoman, to Orlando of Aragon. Having legally established that he is her husband by contract after she ransomed him from prison, she now publicly rebukes him for his ingratitude and treachery before she ultimately rejects him.</meta>

— 379 —

your wickedness (1). And I have not done this so much to condemn your cruelty, nor for my own sake (2); but so that, henceforth, your brothers and others may (3) see what can be entrusted to your faith, what friends may hope for, and what enemies may fear. I have lost gold, and you your reputation. I have lost hope, and you the favor of the king and of your friends. The women of Sicily marvel at my courtly grace and magnify me (4) with their praises: you have become a shameful laughingstock to those who know you and those who do not. Nevertheless, in this matter, I was deceived for a time. I thought foolishly, by the power of the earth <term>per forza di terra: a reference to gold or material wealth</term> (5), that I had rescued a royal and magnificent youth from prison; whereas I see (6) I have liberated a liar, a scoundrel, a traitor, a ruffian, and a cruel beast (7). Nor do I wish you to think yourself of such great value that you alone believe you drew me to this—

***

(1) *Your wickedness revealed* <note>original: "Palesata la tua malvagitate"</note> from the Florentine manuscript is to be preferred over *your wickedness shown* <note>original: "mostrassi la tua malvagità"</note> in the printed version and Manuscript A, and *your wickedness discovered* <note>original: "discoprissi la tua malvagità"</note> in Manuscript B.

(2) The source text reads: "neither did I do this so much in detestation of your impiety by this act of mine" <note>original: "nec hoc tantum in detestationem impietatis tuae hoc meo feci facinore"</note>. The reader surely knows that the meaning of *facinus* <note>Latin: deed, act, or crime</note> is not always negative.

(3) Only the Florentine manuscript uses the variant *possino* <note>Italian: "may" or "can"</note>.

(4) From the Turin manuscripts. The Florentine manuscript reads *magnify me with their praise* <note>original: "magnificanmi con sua lode"</note>, rather than *their praise magnified me* <note>original: "magnificommi la loro lode"</note> from the printed edition. Twenty-three years ago, while drawing a new reading of this beautiful text from the Turin manuscripts, I wrote beside this passage: "beautiful with biblical beauty."

(5) *By the power of the earth* <note>original: "Per forza di terra"</note>, which is missing from the printed edition and Manuscript B, renders the source text’s "for the dregs of the earth" <note>original: "pro terrae fece"</note> in the Florentine and A manuscripts. In Boccaccio’s time, gold was the "dregs of the earth," just as today, being so greatly craved, it is "cursed money" <note>original: "maudit argent"</note>.

(6) The source text reads "where I see" <note>original: "Ubi video"</note>. The Florentine manuscript alone is less faithful with "where I know" <note>original: "dove conosco"</note>.

(7) This sequence of epithets translates the source text's "lying camp-follower, faithless one, glutton, and monstrous beast" <note>original: "mendacem lixam, infidum, ganeonem, immanem beluam"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the speech of Camiola Turinga to Orlando of Aragon. Having secured a legal judgment that he is her husband (due to a marriage contract signed while he was imprisoned), she is in the process of publicly shaming him for his ingratitude before formally renouncing him.</meta>

-> — 380 — <-

this <note>(1)</note>. I was moved by the memory of the favors of your father—if King Frederick, of holy memory <note>(2)</note>, was indeed your father, for I can hardly believe that from such a famous prince could be born such a **degenerate** son <note>(3)</note>. You thought it an unworthy thing that a widow, not born of royal blood, should have a husband of royal lineage, young, robust, and handsome; a thing which I willingly confess. But I would like, if you can reasonably do so, for you to answer me <note>(4)</note>. When I believed I had made you mine through my service, and when I paid a great sum of money for your freedom, where then was your nobility? Where your great strength? Where your beauty? They were covered by a dark cavern, in which you were held fast. All these things—the paleness from unseen light and the **stench** <note>(5)</note> of the dark prison, by which things you rotted away, weak, foul-smelling, and abandoned by everyone—these represent those "virtues" of yours <note>(6)</note> which you now, in your pride, magnify. At that time, you called me not—

***

(1) In the printed edition, this sentence is distorted as follows: *And I do not want you to believe you have drawn me into this.*

(2) The printed edition, which removes so many things, chose to prefix the title *most serene* to King Frederick, which is not read in the [original Latin] text, nor in the manuscripts of the Italian translation <term>volgarizzamento: the medieval practice of translating Latin texts into the common Italian "vulgar" tongue</term>.

(3) The word *degenerate* <note>original Italian: "disconcio"</note> from manuscript A, and *unseemly* <note>original: "sconcio"</note> from manuscript B and the Florentine manuscript, correspond to the Latin source word *inhonestum* <note>meaning: shameful or dishonorable</note>; but I truly do not know where the *disgraced son* <note>original: "scornato figliuolo"</note> of the printed edition comes from, or if it could even apply to Orlando.

(4) The phrasing *that you should answer me* appears only in manuscript B.

(5) *And the stench* <note>original: "il puzzo"</note> *of the dark prison* is from the Florentine manuscript, while the others and the printed edition use *the stink* <note>original: "la puzza"</note> *of the dark prison*, which to my ears sounds more base and vulgar.

(6) To the reading <term>lezione: a specific version of a text found in a particular manuscript or edition</term> of the Bernese edition *has dotes represserant* <note>Latin: "had suppressed these gifts"</note>, there corresponds the printed version: *they had oppressed these virtues of yours*. To explain the word *represent* <note>original: "rappresentano"</note> found in the manuscripts I know, one must suppose that in another Latin text, in place of *represserant* [had suppressed], one read *representant* [they represent]. On reflection, the first reading is perhaps better.

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues Camiola Turinga's speech as she confronts Orlando of Aragon for his betrayal. She has successfully sued him for breach of marriage contract after ransoming him from prison, and is now publicly rebuking him before she ultimately rejects him.</meta>

<lang>en</lang>
<page-num>381</page-num>

...only worthy of a royal youth, but of the celestial God. Oh how lightly <note>(1)</note> and quickly, you most **wicked** man, as soon as you saw the sky of your homeland—beyond even your own hope—you already changed your mind, no longer remembering that you were once at your own disposal <note>i.e., that he had the free will to make the contract</note>, and that I am Camiola: the only one who had compassion for your adversity; the only one who paid my own money for your safety. I am Camiola, who, with my own means <note>(2)</note>, plucked you from the hands <note>(3)</note> of the mortal enemy of your ancestors <note>(4)</note>, from chains, from prison, and from extreme misery. I raised you up to hope when you had already fallen into despair; I brought you back to your country; I returned you to the royal house and to your former life. I have made you a royal, robust, and handsome youth again, whereas in prison you were foul and weak. But why do I bring back to your memory those things which you ought to remember yourself, and which you cannot deny? And you, for such memorable services, have returned me these "thanks": you have dared to deny that you are my husband, and to despise the marriage established by honest and worthy <note>(5)</note> witnesses, and by signed <note>(6)</note> documents, and your recovery—

***

(1) The Florentine manuscript reads *lightly*, and includes the word *quickly*, whereas other manuscripts and the printed edition read *and how soon*.

(2) *With my own means* is from the Florentine manuscript; *with my own money* is found in the Turin manuscripts.

(3) *From the hands* <note>plural</note> in the Turin manuscripts.

(4) The Turin manuscripts read *and not from your evils* instead of *ancestors*. <note>The original Latin text is: "I snatched you from the hands of the mortal enemy of your ancestors."</note>

(5) The word *established* in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition is better than *confirmed* in the Florentine manuscript; also, *worthy* in that same manuscript is more appropriate than *holy*.

(6) *Signed documents*, and not *sacred documents*; for besides the impropriety and the misuse of sacred things, it would contradict the original text, which says <note>original Latin: "tabulisque signatis"</note> *and with signed tablets*.

<page-num>382</page-num>

recovery (1), to despise and debase; and to stain me, if you could have, with ugly suspicion. You, a man of unsound mind, were ashamed to have as a wife a widow born of a father of the <term>knightly order</term> <note>originally: "ordine di cavalleria"; referring to the class of knights or equestrians, a noble but not royal rank</note>. Oh, how much better it would have been to be ashamed of having failed your given word, of having despised the holy and terrible name of God, and, with your cursed ingratitude, of having shown what an abundance of vices you possess. I confess that I am not a woman of <term>royal lineage</term> <note>originally: "schiatta reale"</note>; but having been, since my early childhood, accustomed to the company of the king's daughters, the ladies, and the wives (2), it is no wonder that I have adopted their customs and spirit; which thing is enough to acquire royal nobility. But why use more words? I will do to you easily what you, with all your power (3), have strove (4) to do to me. You denied that you were mine, and yet you were; but I voluntarily grant that you shall not be mine, although I have won the case proving you are mine. Let royal nobility be yours—nevertheless stained with the infamy of falsehood; let youthful strength be yours, and yours be that fleeting beauty. And I, from this point forward, will be content in my <term>widowhood</term> <note>originally: "viduità"</note>: and the riches which God has lent me, I will leave to more honest heirs.

---

(1) *Recovery* <note>Italian: "ricuperazione"</note> in the <term>Florentine manuscript</term> <note>originally: "cod. fiorentino"</note> is to be preferred over *comparison* <note>Italian: "comperazione"</note> in manuscript A and *repurchase* <note>Italian: "ricomperazione"</note> in manuscript B and the printed edition.

(2) This is the reading of the <term>Turin manuscripts</term> <note>originally: "codd. torinesi"</note> and the Florentine one, whereas the printed edition has *the king's daughters, and the ladies of the wives*. The Latin source text: "but since from my cradle I have been among royal virgins, daughters-in-law, and wives" <note>original: "sed cum ab incunabulis apud regias virgines, nurus et coniuges versata sim"</note>.

(3) *With all your power* is from manuscript B. Manuscript A, the Florentine manuscript, and the printed edition use *with all your strength*, in which the word *strove* <note>Italian: "sforzasti"</note> that immediately follows sounds unpleasant to the ear.

(4) The Florentine manuscript, which, as mentioned, uses *with all your strength*, shows evidence of a well-tuned ear by reading, *have sought to do to me*.

of those who might be fathered by you. Go then, (1) unhappy youth; and since you have made it an unworthy thing for you to have me (2), learn at your own expense (3) with what arts and deceits you may mock other women. For this reason, I intend never to be together with you; and I think it much better to prioritize (4) keeping a chaste life than having a marriage with you. (1) The phrase "Go then, unhappy youth" in the <term>Turin manuscripts</term> <note>originally: "codd. torinesi"</note> and the Tuscan version renders the Latin text's "Go therefore, ill-fated young man" <note>original: "vade igitur infauste iuvenis"</note> more accurately than the word "depart" used in the printed edition.

(2) At first glance, the reading in the printed edition might seem better: "because you have done an unworthy thing against me"; but, since the Latin source text says, "because you made it unworthy for you to have me" <note>original: "quoniam me habere te indignum fecisti"</note> (which literally means, "you considered having me an unworthy thing"), it is better to stick to the reading of the manuscripts, which agree on this point.

(3) This is the "learn at your own expense" <note>original: "tuis disce sumptibus"</note> of the original text. To the scribe of <term>Manuscript B</term> <note>originally: "cod. B"</note>, it seemed that the expenses were Camiola's—as indeed they were—and so he wrote "learn at *my* expense."

(4) Here, rather than following the printed edition, the translator follows the reading "I think much [better] that it should be put forward" (meaning preferred or prioritized).

(5) "She rose" <note>Italian: "Levossi"</note> is the reading from the <term>Florentine manuscript</term> <note>originally: "cod. fiorentino"</note>; "she withdrew" <note>Italian: "tolsesi"</note> is found in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.

(6) "Advice" <note>Italian: "Ammaestramenti"</note>, used here with the meaning of counsel, corresponds to the Latin "warnings" <note>original: "monitis"</note> in the source text; the meaning of "flatteries" <note>Italian: "lusinghe"</note> given to it in the Florentine manuscript is not appropriate here.

(7) "Face downcast" <note>Italian: "Volto basso"</note> is the <term>reading</term> <note>originally: "lezione"; a specific version of the text found in a manuscript</note> of the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition. The Florentine manuscript has "bowed face" <note>Italian: "viso chinato"</note>.

(8) "Common men" <note>Italian: "Popolari uomini"</note> is the phrasing used in the printed edition and the Turin manuscripts.

### CHAPTER CIII.

# Joanna, Queen of Sicily or of Jerusalem.

Joanna, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily, above all other women of our time, is famous for her lineage, her power, and her character. It seemed a hateful thing to have remained silent about her <note>This reading from the Turin manuscripts is preferred over other versions that phrased it as "it seemed hateful if I had been silent."</note>; and yet, to remain silent about her was better than to write or say too little of her <note>The editor favors the manuscript reading "to write and say little," which rejects Father Tosti's version, "to write so little in vain." <term>Father Tosti</term> refers to Luigi Tosti, a 19th-century scholar who edited these texts.</note>. Therefore, she was the first daugh— <note>The text ends mid-word, likely "figliuola" (daughter).</note>

daughter of the most serene prince Charles, the glorious Duke of Calabria, firstborn of Robert of famous memory, King of Sicily and Jerusalem, and of Mary, sister of Philip, King of France. ...with strong virtue, she purged not only the cities and inhabited places, but even the alpine passes and remote places (1), the woods, and the dwellings of wild beasts from wicked companies of men. These men all fled or retreated to strong castles which, when besieged by men-at-arms under a noble captain, she did not abandon until the leaders of such bands were punished with the penalty of their heads <note>That is, they were executed by beheading.</note>. This was something that none of the past kings either wished to do, or were able to do. And she has brought the lands she possesses to such a state that not only the poor, but even the rich, by night and by day, can go where they please in safety (2). And (which is a no less healthy thing), she restrained (3) with great moderation the powerful men of the Kingdom and the barons, and drew them back from their dissolute (4) customs, so that, having set aside (5) their ancient pride, those who previously despised kings now fear the face of that woman when she is angry (6). ...that it consists of one sheet, that is, two pages, both because of the length and because I cannot believe that in any book only a single page would be missing.

(1) *This is the reading of Codex B. But the other reading from Codex A and the Florentine manuscript can also stand: "even the Alps and the remote places." The original Latin text: "the Alps and remote glades"* <note>original: "alpes et saltus devios"</note>.

(3) *"Restrained" in Codex B, which, by error, has "molestia" [trouble]. "Restrained" in Codex A.*
(4) *Or also "she improved their dissolute customs" in the Turin codices. "Useless customs" in the Florentine codex is an error. The original text: "she drew back their loose morals for the better"* <note>original: "Mores solutos retraxit in melius"</note>.

(7) *Prudent [Provveduta], that is, insightful or shrewd, corresponding to the original text: "watchful woman"* <note>original: "oculata foemina"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the character portrait of Queen Joanna I of Naples, transitioning from her intellectual prudence to her moral fortitude and resilience in the face of political turmoil and personal hardship.</meta>

<page-num>388</page-num>

...deceive through betrayal than through wisdom; and she is magnificent, not in a feminine way, but in a royal manner. She is so grateful (1), remembering services for a long time; and she is so constant that her good resolve would not bend (2) easily or in vain. This quality has been shown quite clearly through (3) past times and the persecutions of adverse fortune, by which she has very often been struck and shaken, and tossed about by various revolutions (4). For she has endured war caused by the discords of the royals within (5) the kingdom, and wars from without, which sometimes raged in the very heart of the realm (6); and so, through the faults of others, she suffered flight and went into exile, the harsh characters of husbands (7), the hatred of noblemen, an unmerited bad reputation, the threats of popes, and other things—all of which she bore with a strong heart (8). And finally, having uplifted that invincible spirit (9), she has overcome everything, although certainly these trials would have been great even for a strong man.

(1) *"She is so grateful" is the elegant phrasing of the Turin <term>codices</term> <note>A "codex" (plural "codices") is a hand-written manuscript in book form.</note>. "She is also grateful" is found in the <term>Florentine codex</term>.*
(2) *"Would bend" in those first <term>manuscripts</term> renders the Latin *flectere* much better than the "would change" found in the Florentine version.*
(3) *"Through the" [per il] is from the <term>Tuscan codex</term> written in the 15th century. "Per lo" is in the Turin versions.*
(4) *The <term>Florentine codex</term> incorrectly has "levoluzione," while the Turin ones clearly have "revolutions," a word as ancient as "turnings" [rivolgimenti].*
(5) *"Dentro" [inside] in the <term>Piedmontese codices</term>, "drento" in the Tuscan.*
(6) *The Latin is better understood here: "foreign wars sometimes raging within the bosom of the kingdom" <note>original Latin: "extera bella nonnumquam intra regni gremium debachata"</note>.*
(7) *I do not find "the harsh characters of husbands" in the [original Latin] text.*
(8) *The infinitives and the nouns following them are governed by the verb "endure" [sostenere] which precedes them: "For she suffered internal discords, and foreign wars sometimes raging within the bosom of the kingdom, so too by another’s crime, flight and exile, etc." <note>original Latin: "Nam perpessa est intestina dissidia, et extera bella nonnumquam intra regni gremium debachata, sic et alieno crimine fugam exilium etc."</note>.*
(9) *"Uplifted that," meaning her spirit. The Latin reads: "All these things she bore with a brave heart, and finally, with her spirit raised up and invincible, she overcame all things" <note>original Latin: "Quae omnia forti pertulit pectore, et tandem erecto invictoque omnia superavit animo"</note>.*

<margin>(1) Here, in the Bernese edition <note>The "Bernese edition" refers to a 1539 printing of Boccaccio's work</note>, Boccaccio's text ends, except for the conclusion, which was not translated into Italian by Donato Albanzani <note>Donato Albanzani (c. 1328–1411) was a noted grammarian and a close friend of Boccaccio; he was responsible for translating Boccaccio's Latin biographies into the Italian "vernacular" so they could be read by a wider audience</note>. Albanzani instead added a completion of the life of Queen Joanna which Mr. Hortis—taking it from the Harleian manuscript no. 4923 of the British Museum—published in Latin on pages 114-116 of his useful *Studies on the Latin Works of Boccaccio* (Trieste, 1879, in large 4to). At that point, he notes that "in the first edition of the translation of *Famous Women* published by the meritorious Father Tosti, Albanzani’s addition can be read (though missing the beginning) in the Italian language." Unable to agree with him regarding the merits of that Father for that publication, which we have frequently had to criticize, we make it known that Albanzani's continuation now sees the light of day for the first time, based on the version found in two Turin manuscripts and a third Florentine one, which served as our guides for this new edition.</margin>

...man, let alone to a woman. Furthermore, she possesses a marvelous and cheerful aspect, humble speech, and an eloquence that is gracious to every man. And just as she possesses royal majesty and is constant where necessary, she also has a sense of familiarity, pity, gentleness, and kindness, so much so that you would not say she was a queen, but a companion to her people. And what greater qualities would you seek in the wisest king? If one wished to express everything regarding the integrity of her mind, the speech would be very long. For these reasons, I consider her not only noble and excellent for her most illustrious fame, but a singular honor to Italy, the likes of whom has not been seen in past times by any nation (1).

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Queen Joanna I of Naples within a 14th-century Italian translation of Boccaccio’s "Concerning Famous Women." The text highlights Boccaccio's editorial decision to praise the Queen during her lifetime and the later translator's decision to add the "terrible end" of her life, which occurred after Boccaccio’s original work was completed.</meta>

[...illustri]ous in times past (1). Although he promises in this book to write the history of those women, nonetheless, sometimes—whether he was drawn by the pleasure of speaking, or moved by a desire to speak of virtuous things, which pleases me more—he leaves out the wicked things that could be said of them and touches only upon their praises (2). And it may be quite clear to those who search his books that, although it does not belong to history <note>In this context, "history" implies a strictly factual or chronological record</note>, he is drawn by a certain delight (3) and easily leans toward things that can be applied to moral conduct by anyone (4). And although he has taken this as a habit, striving to make women more famous (5), he studiously adorned this last woman <note>Referring to Queen Joanna I of Naples, the final entry in Boccaccio’s collection</note> with marvelous praises, because she lived during his time, and he desired this work to reach her hands (6). And for this reason, it happened that he remains silent, and by silence hides many things which could be told as true, and he forced himself with marvelous eloquence to magnify those things that pertained to her praise. And since she lived on after the writer of her history, it came to my mind to add in this place her terrible end, from...

---

(1) The [Latin] text does not speak of an *end*, but rather of *exile*. "To the exile of this once renowned" <note>original: *Ad huius inclitae olim*</note> (which in the Turin manuscripts is rendered as *in times past*, and in the Florentine as *for the past time*) "and most illustrious queen, it did not reach" <note>original: *atque clarissimae reginae exilium non pervenit*</note>.
(2) All the manuscripts have *his* [sue], but referring to *those women* <note>original: *nefaria de his*</note>, it should be understood as *their* [loro].
(3) The Turin manuscripts [say] *by a certain* (tal quale) *delight*; the Florentine [says the same]. "Enticed by a certain pleasure" <note>original: *Voluptate quadam illectum*</note>, says the [Latin] text.
(4) And not as in Manuscript B, *by which a man can apply himself to morals*. The [Latin] text [reads], "and which in some way can be applied to morals" <note>original: *et quae modo aliquo moribus applicari possint*</note>.
(5) A reading found in all the manuscripts, which poorly renders the [Latin] text, "trying to make famous women more famous" <note>original: *claras feminas clariores facere tentans*</note>; nor do I add "studiously" <term>de industria; a Latin phrase meaning "on purpose" or "with care"</term> which, according to the <term>vernacular translation; the "volgarizzamento," or the translation of a Latin work into the everyday Italian of the period</term>, belongs to the following clause: "he studiously adorned this most recent woman with wondrous praises" <note>original: *de industria hanc novissimam miris laudibus illustravit*</note>.
(6) *Might arrive in her hands*, [say] the <term>Turin manuscripts; "codd. torinesi" refers to handwritten "codices" or manuscript books preserved in Turin</term>.

<summary>This page explains Boccaccio's tendency to focus on the virtues and praises of his subjects, particularly Queen Joanna I of Naples, while omitting their scandals. It notes that since the Queen outlived Boccaccio's writing, the translator decided to append the account of her tragic death to the work.</summary>
<keywords>Boccaccio, Queen Joanna I of Naples, Famous Women, De mulieribus claris, translation, manuscript variants, moral conduct, historiography</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator’s introduction to the added biography of Queen Joanna I of Naples. The translator, Donato degli Albanzani, explains that while Boccaccio praised her during her life, he must now record her tragic end. He cites his patron, Nicolò II d'Este, as the inspiration for this task.</meta>

...to be held by all princes, [regarding] this queen, glorious before, but in the end (1) miserable. And I, Donato del Casentino (2), judged this to be necessary by the command of the illustrious prince Nicolò II, Marquis of Este, who takes such delight in books and such pleasure in famous histories that Philadelphus <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus, the King of Egypt who famously expanded the Library of Alexandria</note> did not surpass him; and since I am a member of his household, I was charged by him to find books, just as another Demetrius <note>Demetrius of Phalerum, the scholar credited with helping establish the Library of Alexandria</note>. 

When she had already passed fifty years of age, she married for the fourth time; and, by the common consent of her barons, she had her husband, Otto the German <note>Otto, Duke of Brunswick-Grubenhagen, Joanna's fourth husband</note>, brought into the kingdom, a man who had a great reputation for his character and his skills in warfare. In the meantime, while Urban VI held the papal throne—to whom the said queen, her husband, and her entire kingdom (3) were favorable and served as his firm support—a new and mortal schism <note>The Western Schism of 1378, where the Catholic Church was split between rival popes</note> began. And from this one learns (4) the sum of her misfortunes; because the matter proceeded so far that, while Urban was still living and not fearing such a thing (5), another pope was elected, like a poison to the Catholic faith (6). Toward whom, having changed her opinion, the said queen began to be fa—

***

(1) <note>Or *finally with these manuscripts*.</note>
(2) <note>Donato degli Albanzani of Casentino is not explicitly named in the vernacular translation in this way. Nevertheless, there is no doubt that both the Latin and the vernacular works are his doing.</note>
(3) <note>Here the gap in the Cassinese manuscript ends, and the printed text begins again.</note>
(4) <note>Or *was known*, according to the Turin and Cassinese manuscripts. But in either case, the phrase <note>original: "hinc disce malorum summam"</note> is not well rendered; it clearly means *from here learn, or grasp, the sum of evils*.</note>
(5) <note>Or *such a thing* according to the Florentine manuscript.</note>
(6) <note>*Like a poison* appears in the manuscripts I have seen, but not in the printed version; it is missing from the [original] text.</note>

<summary>The translator Donato degli Albanzani explains he is writing at the request of Nicolò II d'Este to complete the story of Queen Joanna I of Naples. He recounts her fourth marriage to Otto of Brunswick and the onset of the Great Western Schism, which led to her downfall.</summary>
<keywords>Donato degli Albanzani, Nicolò II d'Este, Queen Joanna I of Naples, Otto of Brunswick, Pope Urban VI, Western Schism, 14th-century Italy, bibliography, manuscript tradition</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
stage>i<meta>This page continues the account of Queen Joanna I of Naples during the Western Schism. The previous page ended mid-word, describing how the Queen had become favorable to the rival pope (Clement VII), which triggered a conflict with the Roman Pope, Urban VI. The translator, Donato degli Albanzani, is providing a vernacular Italian version of Boccaccio’s Latin work, with this specific edition containing extensive scholarly footnotes comparing various manuscripts.</meta>

<page-num>392</page-num>

<margin>q</margin> [fa]-vorable, whether fortune led her to ruin (1), or whether she was drawn away by the perverse flatteries of her followers. And because of this, everything was disturbed and thrown (2) into confusion, because the said Urban, the true pope <note>Urban VI, whose election in Rome sparked the Western Schism</note>, moved by such an unworthy thing (3), pronounced the aforementioned queen a schismatic and an enemy (4) of the faith of Christ and the Church—she whom he had previously called his "beloved daughter." And finally, he defamed her with public trials; and he did not cease until he deprived her of the kingdom. And having called from Hungary, from that same royal bloodline, Charles <note>Charles III of Naples, also known as Charles of Durazzo</note>, a young man of great daring (5) and fame—so much so that previously the said queen did not refuse to have him as her successor, and by everyone’s prediction it seemed it must be so—before it was his rightfully due time, he [Urban] substituted him as king (6). Thus, forcing...

***

(1) <note>original: "Il menasse"</note> *The "led" of the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition, or the "guided" of the Florentine, do not render—except weakly—the Latin "trahere" (to drag) from the original text.*
(2) <note>original: "O messa in confusione"</note> *Or "put into confusion" in the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition.*
(3) <note>original: "mosso per isdegno... indignitate"</note> *The printed version should be corrected from "moved by indignation," because "unworthiness" (indignitate) is also read in the Latin text, though I doubt the reading in "Urban the Pope, moved by the unworthiness of such things" is actually corrupted.*
(4) <note>original: "Christi Ecclesiae hostem... scismatica anima"</note> *In place of this reading, which is found in the manuscripts (except for manuscript B which has "ruin") and the Latin text, "enemy of the Church of Christ," Father Tosti printed: "Urban the true Pope, moved by indignation, pronounced the said queen a schismatic soul of the faith of Christ and the church." Could this be just one—not of the hundred, but of the thousand—merits of that editor who first published this vernacular translation?* <note>The translator is being sarcastic about Father Tosti’s poor editing and the typo "anima" (soul) vs "nemica" (enemy).</note>
(5) <note>original: "Magnae indolis ac nominis iuvene"</note> *The Latin text says "a youth of great character and name"; and therefore not "of great sight and fame," according to the printed edition.*
(6) <note>original: "et iam omnium auspicio futurus erat... eundem regem substituit"</note> *Reading in the printed edition: "inasmuch as before the queen did not refuse to have him as successor, and by everyone's omen he seemed to be the one to be substituted as king," one does not get the sense intended by the Latin text, "and already by everyone's omen he was going to be" (meaning Joanna's successor) "he substituted the same as king" (referring to Urban who substituted Charles).*

<summary>This page describes Pope Urban VI's retaliation against Queen Joanna I for her support of the rival papacy, including his decision to strip her of her crown and name Charles of Hungary as the new King of Naples. The footnotes provide a detailed scholarly critique of previous transcriptions, highlighting errors in Father Tosti's 19th-century edition.</summary>
