--- Page 235 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the conflict between Queen Joanna I of Naples and her challenger, Charles of Durazzo. The previous page ended with Pope Urban VI appointing Charles as the new king to depose Joanna. This page describes the ensuing military struggle between Charles and Joanna’s husband, Otto of Brunswick.</meta>

<page-num>393</page-num>

...the bold young man (1) to acquire the inheritance, and she, strong by ancient possession, forcing herself to defend the seat of her predecessors (2), with great preparations on each side (3), had placed the sum of the matter (4) <note>original: "la somma del fatto" / "rerum discrimen"; referring to the ultimate outcome or the decisive turning point of the conflict</note> in greatest danger. But almighty fortune, which cannot be resisted (5), as Virgil <note>The Roman poet Virgil, often cited in the Middle Ages as an authority on fate</note> says, guides all things. He who was less powerful remained the victor of the war. There was no parity between the parties, not in men (6), nor in military skill; for on one side was the aforementioned Otto <note>Otto of Brunswick, the Prince of Taranto and fourth husband of Queen Joanna</note>, highly trained in deeds of arms (7), with a most expert company of ancient barons of the Kingdom; on the other, the new king <note>Charles III of Naples, also known as Charles of Durazzo</note> being almost a foreigner, raised among barbarians (8) <note>original: "nutrito fra i barbari"; Charles had spent much of his youth at the Hungarian court, which the Italian author characterizes as "barbarian"</note>, inexperienced in lordship, ignorant of the affairs of Italy, unknown to the lords of our country (9), and only aided...

---

(1) <note>The text uses "bold young man" rather than "the said young man" found in the Florentine manuscript. The original Latin text reads *Fervidus adolescens* (a fiery youth).</note>
(2) <note>"Predecessors" [passati] is the reading from the Turin manuscripts and the printed edition; the Florentine manuscript uses "precursors" [precessori].</note>
(3) <note>"From all sides" [da tutte parti] in that same manuscript.</note>
(4) <note>"The sum of the matter" [La somma del fatto], which renders the Latin *rerum discrimen* (the crisis or decisive moment), is found in the manuscripts I have consulted. It rejects the very poor reading in the printed edition, which says they "had put the opinion in greatest danger."</note>
(5) <note>"To resist" [resistere] is the reading of the Florentine manuscript; the Turin manuscripts have "to make resistance," and the Cassinese has "to give resistance."</note>
(6) <note>In the printed edition, the text gets worse: "There was no known variety [varietà] of people between the parties, nor of military skill." Such readings, which are contradicted by the context, even if supported by manuscript authority, are either rejected or noted with doubt regarding their quality.</note>
(7) <note>This is the reading in all manuscripts and the printed edition. The Latin *armorum doctissimus* (most learned in arms) would seem to require "in the fact of arms" [nel fatto delle armi], which is slightly different from the Italian "in deeds of arms" [in fatti d'armi].</note>
(8) <note>Either "nourished" [nutrito] from the Florentine manuscript, or "nurtured" [notricato] from Manuscript A, but never "entangled" [intricato] as found in Manuscript B and the printed edition. The Latin original is *Barbarico nutritus coelo* (nourished under a barbarian sky).</note>
(9) <note>This is a better reading than "incognito to the lords" from the Florentine manuscript.</note>

<summary>This page describes the military imbalance between the experienced Otto of Brunswick, defending Queen Joanna's throne, and the young challenger Charles of Durazzo. Despite Otto's superior experience and local support, the text attributes Charles's eventual success to the unpredictable power of Fortune.</summary>
<keywords>Joanna I of Naples, Charles of Durazzo, Otto of Brunswick, Fortune, Virgil, Neapolitan War, philology, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the struggle for the throne of Naples between Queen Joanna I and Charles of Durazzo. The previous page ended with Charles (the "new king") being inexperienced but aided by external forces. This page begins by identifying that aid as the power of the King of Hungary and details the subsequent defeat and imprisonment of the Queen.</meta>

<page-num>394</page-num>

...by the power of the King of Hungary. With this inequality (1) <note>referring to the disparity in military experience and support mentioned on the previous page</note> having come to battle, suddenly with a single blow (2) <note>The Italian text says "sconfitta" (defeat), but the translator notes the original Latin "uno ictu" implies a single, decisive impulse or strike</note> having entered the kingdom while still uncertain of his own affairs (3) <note>original Latin: "verum suarum ambiguus"; Charles was still unsure of his standing or the loyalty of the people</note>, contrary to everyone’s opinion, he defeated the enemy troops, and took (4) the ancient city of Naples, the seat of the kingdom; and he besieged the queen by land and by sea in a castle which, according to what was said, was impregnable. 

She, who in a short time became old and miserable, surrendered—either because she was forced by necessity, or because, through the deceit of her own people, she was poorly advised (5) <note>original: "male confortata"; the notes suggest this translates the Latin "persuasum," meaning she was wrongly persuaded or misled by her advisors</note>, or as it was said, betrayed. And, stripped of all lordship and liberty, she was placed in a respectable prison at Nocera under good and trusted guard, where after several months she ended her life. But how she died is a matter of varying opinion (6). Some say (and this is the most famous opinion, and is held to be true) that she died naturally as the majority of men do, being overcome by infirmity, or perhaps because her unworthy and undeserved unhappy fate led her...

---

(1) <margin>And never with the printed edition; aided by the power of the King of Hungary, he came to battle with this [inequality], especially since in this way there would be an open contradiction, as it is the new king who, aided by Hungary, comes to battle with her!</margin>

(2) <margin>By translating as "with a defeat" [una sconfitta], the "one blow" [uno ictu] of the text (which means a single impulse) begins to obscure the meaning of this clause, which is much clearer in the Latin.</margin>

(3) <margin>This is how the Latin "uncertain of his own affairs" [verum suarum ambiguus] is rendered in the manuscripts.</margin>

(4) <margin>"Prese" [took] is from the Florentine manuscript; "pigliò" [seized] is from the Florentine manuscripts and the printed edition.</margin>

(5) <margin>"Whether by the fraud of her own people she was poorly persuaded" [Sive suorum fraude male ei persuasum sit]. The "persuaded" [persuasum] is rendered in the manuscripts as "comforted" [confortata]. In the reading of the printed edition, "badly advised and comforted," "advised" is a gloss and explanation of "comforted."</margin>

(6) <margin>Or, according to manuscript B, "there are various opinions."</margin>

<summary>Charles of Durazzo defeats Queen Joanna's forces and captures Naples, leading to the Queen's imprisonment at Nocera. The text notes the conflicting accounts of her death, though it leans toward a natural passing caused by illness or grief.</summary>
<keywords>Charles of Durazzo, Queen Joanna I of Naples, King of Hungary, Nocera, siege of Naples, imprisonment, 14th century history</keywords>

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Queen Joanna I’s death following her imprisonment by Charles of Durazzo. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how her unhappy fate led her to a state of despair or physical decline.</meta>

— 395 —

...to the end, almost as if disdainful of living (1). Others, speaking ill against the king—as is the habit of the wicked—have dared to say that she was poisoned; this opinion must seem empty and false, if I look at the <term>meekness</term> <note>original: "mansuetudine"; a quality of gentle restraint or mercy</note> and the kindness of that king toward all those he defeated. Certain others, led (2) by the delight of making the crime seem more harsh (3), have not feared to whisper that she was strangled with a cord, though it was without the king's awareness, and, in the end, with him knowing nothing of it. I have chosen to record these two latter opinions in this place, not because the first should be doubted in any way, but so that those who read may know that they reached my attention (4), and that I reject them as false and excessive. After she was dead, she was carried to a public place where she remained in the sight of every man, so that no one would later doubt [her death] by thinking she was still alive. Then she was buried with royal honors in her final resting place. And it was made manifest that human life is a fable, and that the poet's saying is true: that we must wait for the final day (5) to praise any man, and that no one should...

---

(1) This excellent reading from the <term>manuscripts</term> <note>original: "codici"; abbreviated as "codd."</note> I know, following the text, condemns the printed version, and perhaps because *she, not deserving or worthy of her unhappy fate, led herself to the end almost as if disdainful of living.*
(2) "Led by delight" is the reading from <term>manuscripts</term> A and the Florentine one. "Moved" appears in the printed edition, and "induced" in <term>manuscript</term> B.
(3) <term>Manuscripts</term> and the printed edition agree on reading *the harsher crime*, and thus in the text published by Mr. Hortis <note>Attilio Hortis (1850–1926) was a prominent Italian scholar and librarian</note> (work cited, page 115), instead of *exaspernandi* <note>Latin: to despise or spurn</note> it would be proper to place <term>exasperandi</term> <note>Latin: to make more severe or harsh</note>.
(4) *Reached my attention*, which is in the <term>manuscripts</term> known to me, corresponding to the Latin *Ad me pervenisse* <note>original: "Reached me"</note>, is missing from the printed edition.
(5) *Final day* <note>original: "ultimo dì"</note> is from the Turin <term>manuscripts</term> and the printed edition; *last day* <note>original: "ultimo giorno"</note> from the Florentine <term>manuscript</term>.

<summary>This page concludes the account of Queen Joanna I's death, weighing rumors of poisoning or murder against the official version of a natural death brought on by despair. The author defends King Charles's character while reflecting on the classical wisdom that no life can be truly judged until its end.</summary>
<keywords>Queen Joanna I, King Charles III of Naples, death of royalty, poisoning rumors, political legitimacy, Attilio Hortis, manuscript variations</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page concludes the biography of Queen Joanna I of Naples and provides extensive scholarly notes regarding the various manuscripts (codices) used for this edition, specifically comparing the Turin and Florentine versions to establish the history of the translation from Latin into the Italian vernacular.</meta>

-> — 396 — <-

...call a man blessed before he dies and is buried (1). <note>This completes the thought from the previous page, referencing the classical wisdom that a person's life can only be judged as truly happy or "blessed" after it has concluded.</note>

(1) At the bottom of the Turin <term>manuscript</term> <note>original: "codice"; a handwritten book</note>, which is marked with the letter A in the preface and notes, one reads: "The book of famous women is finished, compiled by Master Giovanni Boccaccio at the request of the most famous Queen Joanna of Puglia. Then it was translated into the common tongue by Master Donato da Casentino for the magnificent Marquis Niccolò d'Este, prince and lord of Ferrara. May the hand of the writer be saved at all hours." <note>original: "Finito libro de famose donne compilado per miser zuane bocacio ad peticion della famosissima Raina zuana de puglia. poi fo traslatado in idioma uolgar per maistro donato da Casentino al magnifico marchese Nicolo da este principio e signor de feraria. Manus scriptoris saluetur omnibus horis"</note> One can swear that the <term>scribe</term> <note>original: "amanuense"; a person employed to write or copy manuscripts</note> of this manuscript was not Tuscan.

At the foot of the manuscript that we mark with the letter B, one reads: THE END. PRAISE BE TO GOD <note>original Latin: "FINIS LAVS DEO"</note> and nothing else.

At the end of the Magliabechiano paper manuscript: "The book of famous women is finished, compiled by Master Giovanni Boccaccio at the instance of the most famous Queen Joanna of Puglia. Then translated into the common tongue by Master Donato da Casentino for the magnificent Marquis Niccolò d'Este, Prince and Lord of Ferrara, etc. It was completed in 1395, on the 9th day of April. Transcribed by the hand of Z. Ca., goldsmith." <note>original: "Finito il libro de fammosse donne conpillato per miser Giouanna Boccacio adinstancia di la fammosissima Regina Giovanna de Pulia. Puoi traslatato in idioma vulgare per mastro Donato da Casentino al magnifico Marchese Nicolo da Este Principe et Signore di Ferrara etcetera. Fu conpito in M.ª cccº lxxxxv, die viiijº Aprile. Trato per mano de Z. Ca. orevese"</note> And here one must make another oath regarding the truth of the <term>authentication</term> <note>original: "vidimazione"; the official act of verifying a document</note> of the copy commissioned by Count Luigi Passerini, prefect of that library. "Florence, July 17, 1874. This copy, having been <term>collated</term> <note>the process of comparing different copies of a text to identify variations</note> with its original, matches." If it matches, we are informed that the writer of the manuscript—which I call the "Florentine" because it is found in the National Library of Florence—was Venetian, given that in that dialect *orevese* means *goldsmith* <note>original: "orefice"</note>. But whether he was a goldsmith or something else, it is certain that he had a manuscript of good quality before him.

Finally, atop the forty-first <term>quire</term> <note>original: "quinterno"; a gathering of folded sheets of paper forming part of a book</note> of the folio-sized copy—which I made for my own practice twenty-three years ago, comparing the two Turin manuscripts of Albanzani's translation and Boccaccio's Latin text (Bern, 1539)—I wrote (bitter that the <term>Accademia della Crusca</term> <note>The primary institution for the study and preservation of the Italian language</note> and the authors of other dictionaries cited examples of poorly coined words from the Neapolitan printing): "Finished this day, the twenty-seventh of February, eighteen hundred and sixty, in the public Library of the Athenaeum of Turin, by me, Giacomo Manzoni of Lugo."

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>This page serves as the colophon for the volume, appearing after the conclusion of the Table of Contents. It provides the publication details for this 19th-century edition of Boccaccio’s work on famous women.</meta>

->Finished printing this day, March 31, 1882<-
<note>A colophon is a traditional statement placed at the end of a book to provide information regarding its production and publication.</note>
->at the Fava and Garagnani Printing House<-
<note>Fava and Garagnani was a well-known printing firm based in Bologna during the late 19th century.</note>
->at the expense of the publisher Gaetano Romagnoli<-
<note>Gaetano Romagnoli (1800–1887) was a distinguished Italian publisher and bookseller famous for his "Commissione per i testi di lingua," which focused on high-quality editions of early Italian literature.</note>

<summary>This page is the colophon of the book, identifying the date of completion as March 31, 1882, the printer as Fava and Garagnani, and the publisher as Gaetano Romagnoli.</summary>
<keywords>colophon, printing history, Gaetano Romagnoli, Bologna, 1882, publishing details</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the colophon and provides the "Errata Corrige" or list of corrections for the volume. It is a common feature in 19th-century scholarly editions where the editor identifies printing errors discovered after the pages were set.</meta>

| ERRORS | CORRECTIONS (1) |
| :--- | :--- |
| p. 16 place space | long space |
| p. 31 because <note>original: "perchè". The correction "per che" changes the grammatical function from "because" to "for which" or "for what reason."</note> | for what reason |
| p. 31 Some times <note>original: "Alcuna volte". Correction of plural "volte" to singular "volta" for grammatical agreement.</note> | Some time |
| p. 33 from the first <note>original: "da primo". Correction of the masculine "primo" to the feminine "prima."</note> | from the first |
| p. 48 and gives to all, the talents | and gives to all the talents <note>A correction to remove an unnecessary comma.</note> |
| p. 48 *In the note*, Were alone | Were alone <note>original: "Fusse solo" to "Fusse sola". A correction of gender agreement.</note> |
| p. 56 he asked | he commanded |
| p. 57 *In the note*, Laboritus | Laboribus <note>original: "Laboribus" is Latin for "labors" or "toils."</note> |
| p. 79 of the night, | of the night, and the reverence |
| p. 81 And this | And these |
| p. 91 retained | retained <note>original: "ritenuta" to "ritenuto". A gender agreement correction.</note> |
| p. 92 collected | collected <note>original: "raccolte" to "raccolta". A plural to singular correction.</note> |
| p. 124 Which thing also the sacred | Which thing also seems to<br>confirm the sacred |
| p. 125 Caudace | Candace <note>Candace was the title for queens of the ancient Kingdom of Kush.</note> |
| p. 140 *In the note*, in the manuscripts <term>that</term> | in the manuscripts <term>that</term> <note>original: "ne' codd." is an abbreviation for "nei codici" (in the manuscripts); the correction changes the gender of the pronoun from feminine to masculine.</note> |
| p. 140 if not this | if not this <note>A feminine to masculine pronoun correction.</note> |
| p. 149 from sins, | from the sins, |
| p. 156 to whom he gave | to whom was given |
| p. 159 nourished? | nourished. |
| p. 163 and the world is a feminine thing | and men are a<br>feminine thing |
| p. 164 m | in |
| p. 186 they came to make of it | they came to hunger <note>The error "farne" (to make of it) was a misreading of "fame" (hunger).</note> |
| p. 189 a Roman virgin | a nun |
| p. 197 money of a prostitute | money, of a prostitute |
| p. 199 moved to pity | moved to pity <note>A masculine to feminine agreement correction.</note> |

(1) For all the errors that I correct here, and for others that I have not been able to see—even though some clearly cannot be mine, such as the very serious one on page 186—I willingly take the entire burden upon myself. I promise myself indulgence from only those readers who know by experience that no one is a good corrector of the printing of their own writings.

# CURRENTLY IN PRESS

---

1. *The Treasure* by Ser Brunetto Latini <note>original: "Il Tesoro"; an encyclopedic work originally written in French (*Li Livres dou Trésor*) by Dante’s teacher, Brunetto Latini, around 1260</note>, translated into the vernacular <note>original: "volgarizzato"; referring to an early Italian translation</note> by Bono Giamboni, compared with the authentic French text edited by P. Chabaille, and emended under the supervision of Professor Luigi Gaiter, Volume IV.

2. *The Stories of the Narbonnese* <note>original: "Le Storie Nerbonesi"</note>, a chivalric romance from the 14th century, published under the supervision of Professor I. G. Isola, Volume II: Text. <note>This work is part of the "Cycle of William of Orange," a series of epic poems and stories about legendary Frankish knights.</note>

3. *Ancient Vernacular Rhymes* <note>original: "Le antiche Rime volgari"</note> according to the reading of the Vatican Codex 3793, edited by Alessandro D’Ancona and Domenico Comparetti (Volume 3). <note>The Vatican Codex 3793 is one of the most important primary sources for early Italian lyric poetry, containing works from the Sicilian School and the precursors to Dante.</note>

<summary>This page lists several significant scholarly editions of medieval Italian literature and poetry that were in the process of being published, highlighting the editorial work of prominent 19th-century philologists.</summary>
<keywords>Brunetto Latini, Bono Giamboni, chivalric romance, Storie Nerbonesi, Vatican Codex 3793, Italian philology, Alessandro D'Ancona, Domenico Comparetti, medieval literature</keywords>

->Made by LIBRARY BUREAU<-
</insert>

<note>This manila-colored pocket is a relic of pre-digital library management. Before the advent of barcodes and computers, every book contained a card in a pocket like this. When a patron borrowed a book, the librarian would remove the card, stamp it with the due date, and file it to keep track of the loan. The "Library Bureau" cited at the bottom was a company founded in 1876 by Melvil Dewey, the inventor of the Dewey Decimal System, to provide standardized equipment and filing systems for libraries.</note>

<summary>This page contains an administrative library card pocket from the University of Toronto, serving as a physical record of the book's history as a circulating library volume.</summary>

<keywords>library administration, University of Toronto, library card pocket, Library Bureau, provenance, twentieth-century libraries</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page represents the decorative endpaper of the volume, following the library card pocket shown on the previous page.</meta>

->**[Decorative Marbled Endpaper]**<-

<note>This page displays a fine example of traditional hand-marbled paper, often used as "endpapers" to connect a book's cover to its internal pages. The pattern shown here is known as a "snail" or "curl" motif, created by swirling pigments—in this case, blue, cream, and deep reddish-brown—on the surface of a liquid bath before transferring the design onto paper. 

In the 18th and 19th centuries, marbling was a specialized craft. Beyond their beauty, these patterns were practical: the intricate designs helped to hide the glue stains and wear that naturally occurred at the book's hinges. This specific page is framed by the dark brown leather of the volume's binding, indicating a high-quality, professional historical binding.</note>

<summary>This page consists of decorative marbled paper in a blue and brown snail pattern, serving as the artistic endpaper for the leather-bound volume.</summary>

<keywords>marbled paper, bookbinding, snail pattern, decorative arts, endpaper, material culture</keywords>
